Sample 4 - High-Scoring Sunset Boulevard Essay III

Topic How does Sunset Boulevard explore the corrupting effects of Hollywood and the desperation it causes? Response Although a grimly cynical film, Billy Wilder’s Sunset Boulevard presents a critical portrayal of the effects selfishness and desperation can have on characters. Through its backstudio narrativeLanguage & techniqueA genre term deployed casually and correctly — vocabulary signalling genuine film literacy., the film explores the corrosive nature of a heartless system that mistreats its subjects, and the desperate means necessary to survive in such an industry. By the end of the film, Wilder begins to envision an alternative but concludes by exposing the entrapping nature of a corrupt system. The Hollywood industry is constructed as a corrupt and heartless environment that acts as a catalyst for characters’ desperation. In the opening shot of the film a stark juxtaposition is formedStructure & evidenceThe body begins at the film's first frame — evidence sequenced from the text's own opening. between the glamour and corruption of Hollywood, the name connoting glamour and wealth, while the placement of the sign in the gutterStructure & evidenceClose reading of a single frame that yields the essay's central binary. Small detail, large idea. indicating the opposite. Wilder presents Hollywood as corrupt because it promises fame and fortune but cannot deliver to the vast majority of people. From the outset of the film, the struggling screenwriter Joe experiences the cutthroat nature of Hollywood. SitÝng on his bed in a robe, Joe is ‘grinding out’ scripts in a desperate attempt to recover his careerLanguage & techniqueA two-word quotation embedded mid-sentence just before this — economical quoting that keeps analysis flowing.. Wilder highlights the dire nature ofhis situation, and how the promises Hollywood once made to the previously successful script writer are no longer being delivered upon. When debt collectors come to repossess his car, Joe’s perilous attempt to cling on to it serves as a parallel to his instability and financial insecurityStructure & evidenceA plot moment is converted into a mirror of character state — evidence made to work twice.. Joe’s statement that ‘[he needs] about two hundred and ninety dollars and [he needs] it real quick’ works to develop this idea further, highlighting his desperation due to the importance of his car that is symbolic of success and wealth. Wilder exposes Hollywood’s corruption by exploring the false promises of fame and fortune that are never delivered upon. In order to cope with the mistreatment of Hollywood and the ramifications of rejection, characters seek to survive through desperate means and selfish behaviours. Prior to his arrival at Norma’s mansion, Joe has been corrupted by Hollywood, making him utterly desperate and vulnerable. Being rejected by the industry forces him into desperate and unethical behavioursIdeas & interpretationCause and effect made explicit — rejection is the engine, the behaviour its output.. He begins ‘rehashing’ old scripts, abandoning artistic integrity in his desperationIdeas & interpretationBehaviour is interpreted through the essay's key concepts, keeping the topic's terms alive mid-paragraph. to figure out how to succeed in the industry. Wilder examines the effects of desperation on relationships when Joe begins to exploit his contacts, using them as ‘assets’. Gillis’ deceptive nature is revealed through his exploitation of others, which is analogous to Hollywood’s commodification of its participantsIdeas & interpretationCharacter behaviour scaled up to systemic critique — the film's world treated as an argument, not a story.. Joe refers to the fact that Sheldrake had ‘always liked [him]’, displaying his intent to leverage personal relationships for his advantage. Wilder utilises Norma’s house as a reflection of Norma herselfIdeas & interpretationSetting read as characterisation. Push further: one concrete detail of the house here would pin the claim to an actual frame., the place being a ‘white elephant’, and a decrepit shrine to the pastLanguage & techniqueThe description is already an argument — 'shrine' imports worship, delusion and death in one word.. Norma’s construction of this shrine is her way of dealing with the rejectionIdeas & interpretationThe symbol is explained psychologically — the mansion is read as a coping mechanism, not just a metaphor. of an industry that previously gave her validation and identityIdeas & interpretationRejection weighed against what was once given — the psychology has a history.. Joe and Norma both create a narrative that suits their own personal interests, exploiting and manipulating one another. Norma is able to exploit Joe’s vulnerability, his need for money. She buys Joe’s companionship and loyalty, buying him a ‘vicuna’ coatStructure & evidenceA precise costume detail as evidence of the power dynamic — small, specific, impossible to argue with., and seeing him as a young man that can make her feel young and attractive again. Joe exploits Norma’s vanity and instability to get what he believes will be easy money to pay off his debts and reestablish himself in the industry. Gillis is driven to deceive Norma by the corrupt Hollywood system that causes his desperation for financial aid. Both characters are led to manipulate the other by their desire for personal gainStructure & evidenceA synthesis sentence that unites the paragraph's two case studies before the link back — cohesion made visible., acting in self-interest due to their desperation. This behaviour is analogous to the shallow values of Hollywood, living in an alternate reality and manipulating each other for their own gain. Whilst Wilder demonstrates an alternative approach to selfishly delusional living, the tragic ending of the film ultimately reveals that there is no real escape from the corrupt and Hollywood system for those that are too deeply entrenched. Wilder pictures Betty as a counterpart to Norma, she is an earlier version of NormaStructure & evidenceSees the characters as a designed system of parallels rather than individuals — a structural insight typical of top scripts., one that is ‘like all us writers when we first hit Hollywood’ and has not yet been corrupted by the system. Betty represents authenticity and artistic integrity, adamant in her belief that ‘film’s should say something’. She is aware of the hollowness of Hollywood referring to it as ‘all cardboard’. Wilder presents her as the antithesis to Norma, a beacon of hope that is realistic where Norma is delusionalStructure & evidenceAntithesis compressed into a single clause — the character contrast that organises the paragraph, stated cleanly.. Wilder offers hope through Betty, an alternative to living in an alternate reality. Furthermore, her working relationship with Joe is more collaborative and transparent. She wants to ‘do it together’ and does not attempt to manipulate and leverage his talent for her own profit. Wilder contrasts Betty’s behaviour to the other characters, as she is willing to work with Joe rather than exploit him. However, although her sincerity connects with Gillis, Wilder’s portrayal of Joe’s subsequent behaviours undermines any optimism proffered by his relationship with Chafer. While Joe reconciles with reality and attempts to leave Hollywood, he is ultimately unable to extricate himself from Norma’s grasp, a representation of Hollywood’s hold over him. For Norma, Joe’s departure would mean the end of her alternate reality. Joe’s death as he leaves Norma’s entrapment underscores all characters’ inability to escape the corrupt Hollywood system. Even Joe, the character that finally realised the effect Hollywood has had on him, is not allowed to escape, with this realisation instead leading to his eventual demise. In line with the prevailing cynicism throughout the movieStructure & evidenceThe ending's reading is justified by the film's tonal consistency — whole-text awareness supporting a local claim., Joe meets his demise just as he attempts to break free. Following Joe’s death, Norma’s descent into madness is complete. She retreats further into her fantasy world, believing that she is starring in a movie scene rather than facing the grim realityIdeas & interpretationThe final delusion described as genre confusion — Norma exits into cinema itself. of her actions. The final words of Norma and of Hollywood itself are ‘no one leaves a star’. Her last words are a testament to the way the system entrapsStructure & evidenceThe conclusion answers the topic's 'how' with a final, earned claim — resolving the topic, as the criteria require. and manipulates its participants. Sunset Boulevard is a scathing criticism of the cruelty of Hollywood and the corrupting effects of a mistreating system that causes desperation and selfishness. Relationships based on manipulation and self-interest in addition to characters exploiting others emphasises the film’s intention the expose the deceitful nature of Hollywood. Furthermore, while some hope is generated, the corrupting nature of Hollywood overshadows any positive features, overall revealing the corrosive effects that selfishness and desperation have on characters within the industry.

Sample 5 - High Scoring Sunset Boulevard Essay IV

‘Sunset Boulevard shows that the pursuit of fame is a perilous journey.’ Discuss. (VCAA Exam 2023)

Released towards the end of 1950, Sunset Boulevard, Billy Wilder’s incisive expose of the Hollywood industry and American society more broadly was met with scathing reviews by critics who felt Wilder had malignedIdeas & interpretationOpens with reception context — signals knowledge beyond the text while setting up the contention. the all-American pursuit of fame. Indeed, Wilder criticises in his film how the pursuit of fame often creates vulnerability to the exploitation of powerful figures, and more concerningly, the loss of oneself to discontent and even madnessIdeas & interpretationThe stakes are escalated in the contention itself — exploitation, then discontent, then madness — a graded claim the essay then proves in that order.. Despite this, Wilder endorses those in the industry who pursue meaning over fameIdeas & interpretationThe contention has an arc: danger conceded, then a counter-current identified. Complexity is built in from the start, which sets the ceiling for the whole essay (EQ1)., and illustrates how such a path can allow one to avoid the perils of the industry. Ultimately, Wilder suggests that while Hollywood is a dangerous place, it can be navigated by those who seek to produce good workIdeas & interpretationThe thesis ends with the conditions for survival — this clause is what makes the essay a discussion rather than a lament..

In the movie, the pursuit of fame leaves characters open to exploitation and even endangers their lives. Wilder reminds his audience that predatory characters wait around every corner to profit off the naivety of ‘the talent’. Joe’s callous agent tells him he should be grateful to be short on moneyStructure & evidenceEvidence introduced with its irony intact — the quotation indicts the speaker without the essay needing to say so., since “the greatest things were written on an empty stomach.” Despite professing to be Joe’s “friend”, Morino represents the most exploitative and profit-driven parts of deep Hollywood, always lurking in wait for the nextLanguage & techniquePredatory verb choice in the analysis itself — the essay's own language enacts the reading it argues. “10 percent” cut. Wilder symbolically illustrates the dirtiness of the industry right from the opening of the film, when he depicts the titular street name ‘Sunset Boulevard’ written not atop a street sign, but instead in the gutterLanguage & techniqueTechnique, placement and effect in one motion — and the camera angle is read for authorial attitude, not just described.. This placement represents the lowlife aspects of Hollywood, with the fallen leaves in particular subtly hintingLanguage & techniqueOne background detail promoted into meaning — reading the frame's edges, where weaker essays only read its centre. at the industry’s fallen and dead stars, while the high camera angle looking down on it tellingly indicates Wilder’s disgustLanguage & techniqueCamera position read as authorial attitude — the shot treated as narration, not illustration. towards the industry. The potentially fatal outcomes of chasing fame are reiteratedStructure & evidenceA transition that names what the next evidence will do — keeping the argument cumulative. in the next scene when the audience is presented with Joe’s dead body floating face down in a pool, while photographers crowd trying to get a good picture. Speaking from beyond the grave, Joe muses with dry empathy that “the poor dope…always wanted a pool.” As the cameras flash, he adds that heStructure & evidenceQuotation is sequenced — first the image, then the price — so the evidence delivers its own dark punchline in the right order. “got himself a pool – only the price turned out to be a little high.” This quote epitomises from the outset the ultimate perilStructure & evidenceEvidence explicitly ranked — 'epitomises' tells the reader this is the paragraph's key exhibit, not one item in a list. of seeking acclaim: death. Indeed, it was only through his murder that Joe achieved not only theIdeas & interpretationThe pool's irony resolved into the essay's darkest claim — success priced at death. “pool”, but also the success and fame this represents in Hollywood. Joe’s haunting narration keeps the thread of death running through every sceneStructure & evidenceA whole-text structural observation — the voice-over is read as the film's organising device, not one technique among many., reminding the audience that a reckless pursuit of fame can have potentially fatal consequences.

Perhaps even more sadly, Wilder disappointedly comments on the ability for the Hollywood industry to produce cynicism, discontent, and even madness. In his search for recognition, to “get [his name] up there”, Joe loses his desire to do anything other than chase cash. Although there is an indication Joe has “some talent”, Joe insists “that was last year; this year [he’s] trying to make a living”. As much as he feels hard-done-by in the industry, Joe here reveals that he himself has chosen to give up writing what is “true” in the name of trying to make money. Ironically, this leads Joe to a cycle of producing workStructure & evidenceCause and effect traced through irony — the analysis follows the logic of Joe's downfall step by step. that was either “too original” or “not original enough”, but in any case “it didn’t sell”. Through his cynical quips through the film and the lugubrious, jaded toneLanguage & techniqueTone vocabulary carrying analytical weight — the voice-over is treated as a technique with an effect. of Joe’s voiceover, Wilder conveys how for artists who approach Hollywood with the aim of making it big, only burnout and disappointment await. Likewise, Norma loses herself in the pursuit of public adoration. Despite beginning “as a lovely little girl of 17” full of “courage and wit and heart”, Norma became a “terror to work with” after years of “press agents working overtime” on herLanguage & techniqueThe quotation attributes Norma's ruin to the industry's machinery — evidence chosen for causation.. Norma’s inability to separate her stage self from her real self is established early on through the thick makeup accentuating her lips and eyes, reminiscent of the silent film styleIdeas & interpretationThe makeup is historicised — evidence tied to film history, showing why the detail exists rather than just that it does.. This makeup and her exaggerated facial expressions become only more extreme as the film continues, symbolising Norma’s mental decline because of her inability to adaptIdeas & interpretationThe makeup evidence is given a 'because' — the symbol is explained, not just identified.. The wish for fame and “a return” is a manifestation of Norma’s profound desire for attention, represented by attempts to entertain Joe with the “Norma Desmond Follies”, painfully incongruent with her ageing selfIdeas & interpretationThe judgement is precise and humane at once — incongruence named as the tragic mechanism, not mocked.. Watching Desmond’s cosmetic procedures and her increasing insanity is like watching a trainwreck in slow motionLanguage & techniqueA register risk that pays off — vivid, but immediately re-anchored to the film's horror. Push further: use such flourishes at most once per essay., and by the end of the film when Norma famously criesStructure & evidenceThe makeup motif tracked to the film's final scene — whole-arc evidence. she’s “ready for [her] close-up”, the audience is left with a profound sense of horror for how the desire for the adoration of “those wonderful people out there in the dark” can lead actors to totally lose touch with reality. The parallel arcs of Norma and Joe both are symbolicStructure & evidenceThe paragraph closes by uniting its two case studies under one authorial message — a summary sentence guiding the reading, the habit the assessors' reports single out. of Wilder’s broader message that a desire for fame leads people to lose their sanity and meaning.

Although Wilder’s depiction of the dangers of chasing fame is unforgiving, the director also suggests that those who seek meaning in Hollywood are able to withstand the pressures those only seeking material reward buckle under. Even when faced with Joe’s cynicism, asking “who wants true [stories]? Who wants moving?” Betty maintains she “[wants] to write” rather than be a reader “all [her] life”. Betty’s ambition is not to achieve fame or wealthStructure & evidenceThe counter-case is defined negatively first — what Betty does NOT want — which sharpens the contrast with Joe and Norma., but to develop herself, create a career, and ultimately make movies which “say a little something”. Betty is also able to bounce back from failureStructure & evidenceA mini topic sentence inside a long paragraph keeps the argument navigable — signposting without clunk.. As insistent as Joe is that Betty must “weep for those lost close-ups” after she was rejected as an actress, she responds that the whole ordeal “taught [her] a little sense”. Because she does not seek publicity or wealth, Betty is able to escape the disappointments Joe and Norma can not. The extras at Artie’s party, too, are positive, despite being “writers without a job, composers without a publisher”. When Joe enters their room on New Year’s, they are singing happilyStructure & evidenceThree plain words of scene evidence before interpretation — image first, meaning second.. It is not that their experience of Hollywood is different: indeed, they sing about how “Hollywood for [them] ain’t been so good”, and Artie jokingly breaks the fourth wall by commenting he can only afford “three drinks per extra”, a reminder of the financial limitations of HollywoodIdeas & interpretationEven a joke is treated as evidence — the fourth-wall gag read for what it reveals about the industry's bottom rung., particular for those on its lowest rung. However, the extras maintain a positive attitude, using humour to cope, and view this hardship more as a liveable realityIdeas & interpretationA counter-group is interpreted, not just mentioned — the extras become part of the film's argument. of Hollywood rather than as a personal affront. Even DeMille’s extras, despite being old and conventionally unattractive, outlast NormaStructure & evidenceMinor characters used as evidence — breadth of textual knowledge that separates top scripts from those recycling the same three scenes. in Hollywood because they are willing to work in the background, creating the scene, rather than starring in it. Thus, Wilder illustrates that while Hollywood is rife with danger, this peril is avoidable for those who choose to work on movies for the love of it, rather than to achieve money or fame.

In Sunset Boulevard, Wilder pulls back the curtain on the multitude of personalities who seek to work in the movie business. Those who seek fame ultimately lose either their physical lives, or their mental stability. However, those who choose meaning and passion over flimsy materialism are able to survive the perils of Hollywood. Thus, though the original critics may not have seen it, Wilder ultimately leaves his audience with the American Dream: that if you do what you love, one day you might just make it.

Practice Analytical Text Response Topics

1. ‘Rainbow’s End is a bitter condemnation of colonisation.’ Discuss. 2. ‘A real home is where there are people looking out for each other’ How does Rainbow’s End explore the importance of home? 3. "An empty place for Papa Dear." The men, in Harrison’s play are marginalised and have little impact on the play’s events. To what extent do you agree? 4. The main hardships characters face in the play are caused by gender inequality. Part 03 · Rainbow’s EndChapter 2 of 14

Introduction and Context

Author Background

Jane Harrison, a descendant of the Muruwari people, is an acclaimed playwright, novelist, and critic. As a child, she “always loved books” but was disheartened by the absence of Aboriginal representation in Australian literature. In writing Rainbow’s End, Harrison sought to give voice to those experiences that had long been silenced or marginalised. Drawing upon oral histories from Aboriginal Elders, she captures the resilience and humour of families who lived on “The Flats” and later at Rumbalara—the First Nations housing estate in Mooroopna. Harrison’s work strongly condemns the substandard housing and systemic neglect endured by First Nations families during the 1950s. Through her writing, she positions herself as a conduit for truth-telling, giving dramatic expression to both the hardships and triumphs of Aboriginal life. Her aim, as she has stated, is to use fiction as “an important way for First Nations people to have a voice.”

Analysis

Harrison’s authorial perspective intertwines personal heritage and political conviction. By dramatizing the everyday realities of Aboriginal families, she reclaims history from the margins and rehumanises voices often reduced to statistics or stereotypes. Her compassionate yet incisive storytelling challenges audiences to confront both the past and its lingering presence in modern Australia.

Historical Context

The colonisation of Australia, beginning with the arrival of the First Fleet in 1788, unleashed devastating consequences for First Nations peoples. The ensuing Frontier Wars—violent conflicts between Aboriginal people and British settlers—resulted in the massacre and displacement of countless Indigenous communities. Throughout much of the twentieth century, government policy continued to pursue assimilation and cultural eradication. One of the most tragic outcomes of these policies was the forced removal of Aboriginal children from their families. These children, later known as the Stolen Generations, were stripped of their language, names, and cultural identity. Many were placed in institutions or adopted into white families, where they were exploited as unpaid labour and suffered emotional and physical abuse. In Rainbow’s End, Harrison threads this history through the lives of Nan and Gladys, whose trauma reflects the generational wounds inflicted by colonial intervention. Gladys’s experience of being taken from her family and made to work as a domestic servant mirrors the broader policy of forced assimilation. Her obsession with Dolly’s education becomes a personal act of restitution—an attempt to reclaim what was taken from her by systemic injustice.

Analysis

By embedding the historical trauma of dispossession within intimate family narratives, Harrison compels her audience to recognise the enduring emotional and social consequences of colonisation. Her portrayal of the Stolen Generations not only condemns past wrongs but also foregrounds the resilience and agency of those who survived them.

The Flats and Cummeragunja

Cummeragunja, founded in 1888 on Yorta Yorta/Bangerang Country, represented both refuge and resistance for Aboriginal people. Initially a self-sufficient farming community, it was later undermined by the New South Wales Protection Board, which seized control in 1915. Aboriginal workers were paid in meagre rations, and funds generated by their labour were appropriated by the Board. By the 1930s, disease and poverty had decimated the community. In 1939, the residents of Cummeragunja staged a historic protest—the first mass strike by Aboriginal Australians. Many left the mission and settled on “The Flats,” a floodplain near Mooroopna and Shepparton. Despite harsh living conditions, including humpies made from “flattened kero tins” and exposure to extreme weather, the community retained its cultural vitality and kinship ties. By the early 1950s, around 300 people lived there.

Analysis

The Flats serve as both a literal and symbolic setting in Rainbow’s End. They embody dispossession yet also community, poverty yet perseverance. Through this setting, Harrison contrasts the instability imposed by colonial structures with the cultural rootedness of Aboriginal families. The Flats become a testament to the capacity of a people to adapt, resist, and endure despite systemic neglect.

Part 03 · Rainbow’s EndChapter 3 of 14

Analysis of the Historical and Cultural Context in Rainbow’s End

Overview

Jane Harrison’s Rainbow’s End emerges from a landscape shaped by colonisation, segregation, and resilience. Set in 1950s Australia, the play reflects both the harsh realities and quiet triumphs of Aboriginal families living under systemic discrimination. The historical context is indispensable to understanding the characters’ identities, the injustices they face, and the endurance that defines their humanity. Harrison’s work gives voice to the unsung heroes—especially women—who sustained their communities despite persistent marginalisation.

Acknowledgement and Purpose

The play’s thematic foundation begins with an acknowledgment of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples as the traditional custodians of the land. This recognition situates Rainbow’s End within an ethical framework that honours history and memory. Harrison’s intent is not merely to tell a story but to preserve a cultural legacy and challenge erasure. Commissioned to highlight Aboriginal women’s experiences, the play transforms domestic life into political narrative, demonstrating that resistance often begins within the home.

Analysis

By embedding acknowledgment into the narrative fabric, Harrison bridges past and present. The act of remembering becomes both art and activism, inviting audiences to reflect on how storytelling can restore dignity to those history overlooked. Her focus on Aboriginal women redefines heroism as endurance and nurturing in the face of dispossession.

Historical Background: Colonisation and Segregation

The origins of the play’s conflict trace back to the legacy of colonisation and government policies such as the Protection Acts, which sanctioned segregation and the forced removal of children. Aboriginal families were confined to reserves like Kamarra Ganja, where strict management and limited access to resources entrenched poverty. These lived realities form the backbone of Harrison’s narrative world, revealing how state control sought to diminish autonomy while families fought to preserve it.

Analysis

Harrison transforms historical record into lived drama. The reserve system becomes a stage for both oppression and resilience. By drawing from documented injustices, she crafts a narrative that demands empathy and accountability. The audience confronts how legislative cruelty translated into generational trauma.

Life on the Reserves and the Flats

After the walk-off from Kamarra Ganja, many Aboriginal families relocated to makeshift settlements known as “the flats.” These spaces, though impoverished, became hubs of culture, laughter, and communal strength. The families’ persistence in maintaining cultural traditions symbolises defiance against systemic neglect. In this environment, children learned resilience as a form of inheritance, and hope became both a necessity and an act of protest.

Analysis

The depiction of life on the flats captures a paradox — material deprivation coexists with emotional and cultural richness. Harrison celebrates community cohesion as a radical response to marginalisation. The domestic setting transforms into a microcosm of resistance where identity is preserved through humour, storytelling, and maternal leadership.

Government Policy and the Metaphor of the Rainbow

The government housing schemes of the 1950s, such as those in Rumbalara, promised integration but perpetuated segregation through inadequate living conditions. The metaphor of the rainbow recurs as a powerful symbol throughout the play. To the characters, the “end of the rainbow” represents dreams of equality and acceptance — visions that remain just out of reach. This unattainable horizon mirrors the historical struggle for recognition endured by Aboriginal Australians.

Analysis

The rainbow encapsulates the duality of hope and despair. It is both a dreamscape and a mirage — a reflection of the distance between aspiration and reality. Harrison employs this image to expose the cruel optimism embedded within promises of assimilation. The characters’ pursuit of the rainbow becomes a metaphor for the broader national pursuit of reconciliation still incomplete.

The 1950s Social Landscape

Post-war Australia was marked by conservative values, economic expansion, and racial inequality. For Aboriginal peoples, this period intensified the contradictions between visibility and exclusion. Women, in particular, bore the burden of navigating these intersecting oppressions. Through Gladys, Dolly, and Nan Dear, Harrison portrays how Aboriginal women negotiated identity, motherhood, and dignity within a society determined to silence them.

Analysis

Harrison’s representation of 1950s Aboriginal womanhood dismantles stereotypes of passivity. Her characters inhabit a world of limited choices yet wield moral and emotional power. Their courage lies not in grand rebellion but in persistence — in cleaning, teaching, loving, and speaking truths that white Australia refused to hear.

Conclusion

Understanding the historical and cultural context of Rainbow’s End enriches every reading of the play. Harrison’s depiction of segregation, displacement, and resilience transforms history into human experience. The struggles of the Banks and Dear families are not isolated but emblematic of a broader narrative of survival. The play stands as both testimony and challenge: to remember, to acknowledge, and to act. In recognising the truths of the past, Harrison invites audiences to see the enduring strength of Aboriginal identity — not as tragedy, but as triumph.

Structure of the Play

Rainbow’s End opens in medias res, plunging the audience directly into the aftermath of a flood. This sudden beginning creates an atmosphere of crisis and displacement, immediately reflecting the instability that defines the characters’ world. The play’s structure is unconventional—beginning with a prologue, followed by scenes divided into parts A and B, reflecting fragmentation and lack of control in the characters’ lives. Harrison integrates dream sequences that blur the boundary between fantasy and reality. These imaginative interludes, such as Gladys’s visions of meeting the Queen or Dolly’s aspirations for education, express both hope and futility. The juxtaposition of dreams and deprivation highlights the gap between aspiration and circumstance. Two narrative threads underpin the play: Gladys’s political awakening and Dolly and Errol’s romance. Each culminates in an epiphany—Gladys’s courageous confrontation with the Shire Council and Dolly’s recognition of Errol’s unconscious racial assumptions. The play concludes with two significant symbols: Nan’s dream of Dolly’s wedding and the song “Que Sera, Sera.” The latter, appearing at both the beginning and end, transforms from a cynical reflection of government indifference into a subtle anthem of hope and change. Harrison’s structural choices mirror her thematic concerns. The fragmented narrative echoes the disrupted lives of Aboriginal families, while the interweaving of dreams and realism underscores the endurance of hope amid systemic oppression. The dual climaxes—Gladys’s activism and Dolly’s assertion of independence—affirm the possibility of transformation both personal and political. The cyclical use of “Que Sera, Sera” redefines fatalism as faith: a belief that the future, though uncertain, can still hold renewal.

Part 03 · Rainbow’s EndChapter 4 of 14

Structure and Themes in Rainbow’s End

Overview

Jane Harrison’s Rainbow’s End is constructed as a two-act play divided into short, interlinked scenes that illuminate the resilience, struggles, and dreams of First Nations families in 1950s Australia. The fragmented structure mirrors the fractured social and political realities of Aboriginal life, where moments of humour and hope coexist with hardship and prejudice. Harrison’s manipulation of stage direction, song, and dialogue builds dramatic tension and emotional intimacy, guiding the audience through a tapestry of generational experience.

Act Structure and Thematic Design

Unlike traditional plays divided by chapters, Rainbow’s End unfolds through acts and scenes that offer glimpses into domestic and emotional moments. This episodic format reflects the uncertainty that governs the Dear and Banks families’ lives. Scenes often end abruptly or transition during moments of crisis, symbolising the instability caused by poverty and discrimination. The two major narrative threads — Gladys’s political awakening and Dolly’s coming-of-age romance — develop in parallel, converging at the climax where both women redefine their strength and identity.

Analysis

Harrison’s two-act structure underscores her social commentary. The brevity and rhythm of scenes intensify audience empathy while exposing the invisible pressures imposed by systemic inequality. By structuring the play around fragmented lives, Harrison creates a dramatic form that mirrors cultural and emotional dislocation — yet also reveals endurance and connection within the Aboriginal matriarchal family.

In Media Res and Dramatic Openings

The play begins in media res amid the chaos of a flood, a moment both literal and symbolic. Accompanied by the recurring song “Que Sera, Sera,” the opening sequence immediately juxtaposes hope with helplessness. This song returns at the play’s conclusion, binding the narrative through a motif that evokes the tension between aspiration and acceptance.

Analysis

Beginning in the middle of action thrusts the audience into the characters’ reality without exposition, reflecting the immediacy of their struggle for survival. The flood motif encapsulates emotional and social inundation, while the musical refrain serves as both comfort and irony — a lullaby masking pain.

Dream Sequences and Juxtaposition

Dream sequences punctuate the play, revealing the characters’ inner lives and unfulfilled desires. Gladys’s fantasy of meeting the Queen becomes a recurring image of colonial aspiration — an impossible vision shaped by internalised subservience and social exclusion. These dreams contrast sharply with the family’s reality in a riverside shack, where dignity must be defended daily.

Analysis

Through dream imagery, Harrison critiques the illusion of social mobility within a racist society. The juxtaposition of fantasy and deprivation exposes the psychological toll of colonisation, while simultaneously affirming the imagination as a site of survival and self-worth.

Character Growth and Narrative Resolution

Both major storylines reach their culmination in acts of self-realisation. Dolly challenges her mother’s fixation on external validation, forcing Gladys to confront the limits of her own deference. This confrontation propels Gladys toward activism, culminating in her powerful declaration at the Shire meeting: “We demand proper schooling … The white people too — they need to be educated about us” (p.197). Though the play ends with personal growth, systemic barriers remain unresolved, reflecting the persistence of structural inequality.

Analysis

The dual transformation of Gladys and Dolly demonstrates Harrison’s belief in intergenerational empowerment. Their evolution symbolises a shift from private endurance to public assertion — a necessary transition for community progress. The incomplete resolution emphasises realism over idealism, reinforcing that the struggle for equality continues beyond the stage.

Dramatic Intensity and Unseen Forces

Harrison’s use of short, tightly constructed scenes sustains dramatic tension and emphasises the relentlessness of external pressures. Many of the most significant events occur offstage, their impact conveyed through dialogue and reaction. The small cast heightens intimacy while unseen antagonistic figures — landlords, officials, and employers — personify the faceless nature of institutional discrimination.

Analysis

By keeping oppression unseen yet omnipresent, Harrison amplifies its psychological weight. The absence of visible villains shifts focus toward resilience, love, and humour as tools of survival. The minimal staging and interwoven dialogue allow audiences to inhabit the emotional pulse of the characters’ world, where silence often speaks louder than confrontation.

Conclusion

The play’s structure functions as both narrative device and political metaphor. Through acts, scenes, dreams, and songs, Harrison crafts a world where fragmented experiences reveal collective truth. The rhythm of the play mirrors the cycles of struggle and renewal within Aboriginal communities, inviting readers and audiences to engage with the ongoing dialogue between past injustices and present resilience. Understanding the form of Rainbow’s End thus deepens appreciation for its portrayal of identity, endurance, and hope in mid-twentieth-century Australia.

Part 03 · Rainbow’s EndChapter 5 of 14

Audience and Purpose in Rainbow’s End

Overview

Jane Harrison’s Rainbow’s End (2005) was written to educate and engage both Indigenous and non-Indigenous audiences through its portrayal of three generations of Aboriginal women — Nan Dear, Gladys, and Dolly — living in 1950s Victoria. Harrison’s purpose extends beyond recounting hardship; she celebrates resilience, dignity, and familial love amidst oppressive social structures. The play invites audiences to confront the consequences of colonisation and assimilation while recognising the strength of community and cultural continuity.

Purpose of the Play

Harrison’s central purpose is to foreground the untold stories of Aboriginal women and their everyday heroism. Through the intimate domestic setting of the Dear and Banks family, she challenges the invisibility imposed upon First Nations peoples by historical narratives. Her play functions as both cultural preservation and political statement — illustrating that Indigenous survival is not defined by victimhood but by persistence and pride. Harrison’s writing embodies the principle that art can both memorialise and mobilise. By humanising historical trauma, she transforms policy and prejudice into personal experience, allowing audiences to empathise rather than merely observe. The play’s tone oscillates between warmth and sorrow, comedy and critique, mirroring the emotional complexity of survival.

Analysis

Through Rainbow’s End, Harrison asserts that storytelling is a mode of reclamation. Each scene restores voices historically silenced by government policy and social discrimination. Her purpose is not only to depict resilience but to invite truth-telling — to reposition Aboriginal women as custodians of memory, agency, and identity.

Intended Audience

Rainbow’s End addresses a dual audience. For non-Indigenous viewers, it acts as an awakening — revealing the lived experiences obscured by whitewashed Australian history. For Indigenous audiences, it affirms continuity and pride, validating intergenerational strength and the endurance of culture. By positioning humour and tenderness alongside hardship, Harrison ensures accessibility for classroom and theatre audiences alike. The play encourages educational engagement, prompting critical reflection among students while offering Aboriginal spectators the comfort of recognition. The inclusion of intergenerational female perspectives fosters empathy across age groups and cultural divides.

Analysis

Harrison’s nuanced approach ensures that the play operates on multiple emotional and intellectual levels. Non-Indigenous audiences are confronted with complicity in structural injustice, while Indigenous audiences are offered representation that transcends trauma. This balance of challenge and affirmation defines the play’s enduring impact within Australian theatre and education.

Historical Awareness and Cultural Responsibility

The play’s authenticity stems from Harrison’s meticulous research and personal connection to her Murrawari heritage. Drawing upon oral histories, newspaper archives, and community interviews, she constructs a historically grounded yet emotionally resonant narrative. Her inclusion of real figures, such as Pastor Doug Nichols, situates the fictional world within an authentic cultural framework. This fidelity to truth underscores Harrison’s responsibility as both playwright and cultural custodian. Her depiction of the 1950s assimilation era reflects an ethical commitment to remembrance — to ensuring that audiences understand the policies that fractured families and communities, and the resilience that rebuilt them.

Analysis

Harrison’s creative purpose merges documentation with dramatisation. She transforms history into a living dialogue between past and present, ensuring that Aboriginal experience is neither mythologised nor forgotten. By embedding historical authenticity within intimate storytelling, she teaches audiences that understanding cultural identity begins with acknowledging historical reality.

The Role of Representation in Theatre

For Harrison, representation is activism. The play reclaims space within the Australian canon for Aboriginal voices, particularly those of women. Through humour, vernacular speech, and emotional candour, she dismantles stereotypes and restores humanity to communities too often reduced to statistics. Rainbow’s End thereby becomes a vessel for truth-telling, reconciliation, and education.

Analysis

Representation in Rainbow’s End is not symbolic but lived — an act of cultural assertion. Harrison’s purpose is fulfilled when audiences see Aboriginal women not as passive subjects of history but as authors of their own narratives. The play’s enduring presence in school curricula ensures that these voices continue to educate, inspire, and transform generations of Australians.

Conclusion

Harrison’s Rainbow’s End unites purpose and audience in a dialogue of empathy, education, and recognition. Her portrayal of three Aboriginal women transcends their fictional identities to stand as symbols of cultural endurance. By crafting a story grounded in truth and delivered with compassion, Harrison reshapes the Australian stage into a site of reconciliation — where understanding begins through listening, and hope endures through storytelling.

Part 03 · Rainbow’s EndChapter 6 of 14

Language in Rainbow’s End

Overview

Language in Jane Harrison’s Rainbow’s End is a powerful vehicle for expressing identity, class, and cultural conflict. The characters’ speech patterns reveal their personal histories and social positions, while Harrison’s authorial voice is embedded within their dialogue, reflecting her own commentary on racial inequality, resilience, and generational change. Language, therefore, functions not only as a mode of communication but also as an indicator of power, prejudice, and pride within the 1950s Australian context.

Nan Dear

Nan’s language is colloquial, humorous, and often sharply ironic. When she informs Gladys that she “won’t be needing any of them encyclops” (p.136), she dismisses education’s relevance to her family’s daily struggles, revealing both her pragmatic worldview and the limited opportunities afforded to her generation. Her unpolished speech reflects her lower-class status and lack of formal education—an outcome of systemic exclusion faced by Aboriginal Australians. Yet Nan’s language also injects comedy into the play. Her dry humour and literal phrasing create moments of levity that highlight resilience in hardship. When mocking Gladys’s excitement about meeting the Queen, Nan’s irony underscores the absurdity of colonial class structures. Through Nan’s speech, Harrison reveals both the pain and pride of a woman grounded in survival, whose humour becomes an act of quiet rebellion against social inequality.

Analysis

Nan’s dialect situates her within a marginalised social class while asserting authenticity and self-respect. Her vernacular speech becomes an emblem of identity, opposing assimilationist pressures that sought to erase Aboriginal voice. Harrison uses Nan’s language to celebrate oral tradition and defiant humour, challenging notions that education alone defines intelligence.

Dolly Banks

Dolly’s language reflects her education and confidence but also her awareness of the social hierarchies constraining her. She is direct and assertive, particularly when confronting racism. Her rebuke of Errol’s remark about “you people” (p.141) exposes his unconscious bias and the normalisation of racial division. Likewise, her challenge to her mother — “You’re always telling me to stick up for myself, but when do you, eh?” (p.162) — forces Gladys to examine her internalised inferiority. Dolly also code-switches, adopting Indigenous vernacular when she threatens to “kick Ester’s boys up to moon” (p.137), using humour and familiarity to assert belonging within her community. Her fluency, both in educated English and colloquial Aboriginal dialect, embodies her dual identity as a bridge between two worlds. Her closing assertion to Errol — that she would never trade her family or heritage for a “better life” (p.172) — epitomises pride and defiance.

Analysis

Dolly’s language signifies empowerment through articulation. Harrison crafts her speech to demonstrate that mastery of language is not assimilation but assertion. Dolly’s ability to speak for herself—and against injustice—makes her a vehicle for Harrison’s advocacy of Indigenous self-determination. Her linguistic versatility becomes symbolic of cultural strength rather than concession.

Errol Fisher

Errol’s halting and awkward speech reflects both his nervousness and his ignorance. As an encyclopedia salesman who depends on persuasion, his incompetence is ironically comic. When he awkwardly notes that Dolly’s glass is “made out of a can” (p.140), he unwittingly insults her. His language often reveals class and racial privilege cloaked in politeness — exemplified in his use of “you people” (p.140) to generalise Aboriginal Australians. His verbal missteps and tone-deaf expressions highlight his sheltered upbringing and lack of cultural awareness. Over time, however, Errol’s language softens and becomes more considerate, mirroring his emotional growth and respect for Dolly’s world.

Analysis

Harrison uses Errol’s speech to critique the well-meaning but ignorant paternalism of mid-century white Australia. His progression from patronising to empathetic reflects the broader social journey toward understanding and equality. His linguistic awkwardness thus serves as a tool of both comedy and commentary.

Gladys Banks

Gladys’s voice develops from timid dreamer to assertive activist. During the Rodney Shire meeting, her passionate declaration — “We demand proper schooling … The white people too — they need to be educated about us, and our ways” (p.197) — becomes a turning point where her speech echoes Harrison’s own political message. Her earlier language is often infused with fantasy and aspiration: daydreams of the Queen’s attention or Dolly’s graduation soften her struggles. Yet by the play’s end, Gladys has found a political and moral voice that embodies the collective frustrations of Indigenous Australians denied dignity. Her transition from domestic to public speech represents empowerment through language.

Analysis

Through Gladys, Harrison demonstrates the transformative power of speech as activism. The act of “speaking up” replaces passive dreaming with agency. Gladys’s voice becomes that of her people — articulate, righteous, and unafraid — asserting the necessity for visibility and reform.

The Royals

The Queen’s language is formal, elevated, and exclusionary. Her reference to Australia as “the birthplace of the nation” (p.124) erases 65,000 years of Aboriginal civilisation, while her statement about standing “at last among Australians” implicitly refers only to white citizens. This polished rhetoric embodies colonial arrogance. By contrast, the Duke’s language is more spontaneous and unguarded. His offhand question — “Where’s Father?” (p.127) — humanises him but also underscores the royal couple’s detachment from the lived realities of those they visit.

Analysis

Harrison employs the Royals’ diction as social critique. Their words — ceremonious, detached, and self-congratulatory — expose the gulf between imperial symbolism and the Indigenous suffering it conceals. The Queen’s non-inclusive language mirrors the nation’s non-inclusive identity at mid-century.

The Radio Announcer

The radio announcer’s language is superficially cheerful yet profoundly condescending. In describing the royal visit, he mentions a demonstration of “how to throw a boomerang” and marvels that it “really does come back” (p.128). His tone trivialises a complex cultural artefact, reducing Indigenous skill to entertainment for the royal couple.

Analysis

The announcer’s commentary encapsulates institutional racism disguised as enthusiasm. Harrison’s use of irony highlights how mainstream media perpetuated ignorance, framing Aboriginal culture as novelty rather than heritage. His words parody the way national pride excluded First Nations people.

Structural and Symbolic Language

Beyond dialogue, Harrison uses language in her stage directions and symbolism to reinforce meaning. Descriptions of the Dear family’s humpy — “waterlogged” and “mud-spattered” (pp.123, 176) — visually communicate poverty and marginalisation. The title itself, Rainbow’s End, draws on the myth of a pot of gold, suggesting the illusion of prosperity and the unattainability of a promised better life. The reference to “Somewhere Over the Rainbow” from The Wizard of Oz introduces an ironic hopefulness. Its lyrics — “the dreams that you dare to dream really do come true” — echo the family’s optimism but also question the realism of those dreams in a society unwilling to grant equality.

Analysis

Harrison’s manipulation of both literal and figurative language constructs a layered critique of Australia’s racial landscape. The interplay between hope and futility in her metaphors underscores the central tension of the play — between aspiration and limitation, imagination and reality. Language, whether spoken, written, or symbolic, thus becomes both a weapon and a wound.

Part 03 · Rainbow’s EndChapter 8 of 14

Theme Analysis

Love and Belonging

Harrison explores love and belonging in rainbows end, with Nan and Gladys creating a warm, loving home for Dolly. When Errol offers Dolly a “better life” in the city with a “real home,” Dolly is taken aback by his naivety to her culture. Even though the humpy had its downside, Dolly acknowledged that it’s the fierce love and loyalty that made living there easier.

Nan. Sometimes, you have to move on. Leave things behind. Even things you love. (P.187) Dolly. No matter how they come into the word, you still love’em the same. (P.186) Errol. What matters is [her] / not [her] address (p. 149). Nan. Oh, for heaven’s sake, Dolly marry that boy, before someone else does. (P.198)

Leadership

Leadership is explored through Gladys’ speech where she took Papa Dears place at the petition. Harrison displays not only the leadership in the family dynamic but also the government taking leadership over the indigenous Australians and believing that they know what is best for them and displaying leadership in a very different way. This is displayed by the Inspector as being the character showing the government in ‘Rainbow’s End’.

Inspector: “but someone must take leadership” Gladys: “I will present the petition” Gladys: “She needs to know the world is bigger than just this.” (pg 131) Gladys: “I’m not an interloper- I belong here –this is my land!” Gladys: “We’ve been humble for too long.” Gladys: “I make the decisions regarding Dolores, thank you.” Nan Dear: “Grab the flour, Glad, and the tea.” Gladys: “Oi, Re the so-called ‘housing problem’, it is only a problem because of us Aboriginals-” Nan Dear: “I don’t care what you think! You and your visiting hours. Your rules. No singalongs after dark. Your spying. You, mister, can go to blazes! I’ll give you ‘one’...” Gladys: “We demand to be heard.”

Race and Racism

- The love plot in the book is purposefully between Dolly and Errol, Harrsion aims to defy the otherwise segregated nature of the society the play is set in. - Whiteness is a constant theme throughout the play, symbolising the belief that white is somehow ‘cleaner’. - All white characters in the play besides Errol are a source of authority; The councilmen, bank manager and inspector all represent different amenities needed by Aboriginal people that have been controlled by the white population. Nan Dear: “They forced us to leave. Forced us to leave Cummeragunja. Our home” the repetition of “forced” and the use of the words “our home” suggests that Harison is trying to show that the white people think they know better than the indigenous people in the play. p126. Nan Dear: “and hospitals is where they take our baby’s away” Nan Dear: “A girl from The Flats? I don’t even see the town Aboriginals working in stores.” P127 “the hessian that I couldn’t get through and couldn’t even peek over.” P127... “stop the likes of her seeing our humpies.” P152 “I don’t know how you do it...With river water, no less!” P164 “- sorry, report means -” bank manager assumes she doesn’t know. P140 “You people” - Errol – oblivious P163 “What I’m wondering is how she’d fit in” “and hospitals is where they take our babies away...” (Nan pg129) “and your whites are so white!” (Inspector pg152)

The Intersection of Class and Race

- The intersection of race and class in Rainbow’s End is exemplified by the dynamic between the white population and the Indigenous population. This relationship is explored in scenarios such as Dolly’s search for job, in which it is seen as unlikely she will be able to receive employment. This creates the inescapable cycle that is forged when race meets class, that to escape a lower class, it’s required to achieve a job that could be considered higher status to society. Race hinders this progress due to the prejudices and barriers held against Indigenous people. - The inspector’s view in regard to the flood, as he seems to have no awareness of the effect this will take upon first nations families, particularly the Dear family. - Gladys having to save every penny to afford the encyclopedias just to invest in the cultural capital. - Profit from proximity to whiteness (town Aboriginals) - Government policies Inspector: “property can be replaced” “I’d kind of like to be a nurse”- Dolly, which she believes to be silly. Dolly- “What tree? It would be nice to have a tree and presents.” Pg 169 Inspector “I’m not familiar with the department stores in this area” – pg. 152 (in reference to Daisch’s the town tip” “Your whites are so white! With river water no less!” Pg 152, the inspector. Subtle reference to white is believed better. “When it was our sunroom curtains. But you know i thought we took them to the tip” (in reference to dolly’s ball gown) pg.166 Gladys- “Not on a picker’s wage. Gawd we’d be living on Johnny cakes” pg. 174 (in reference to when Jungi says that property can be replaced.) “The old, ruined lino” pg. 123 The dears have to make dear with what they can get access to unlike the whites who it is privileged. “Love your dress, Dolly. Love the fabric. My mother quite liked it too. When it was our sunroom curtains. But, you know, I thought we took them to the tips.” Pg 166 Nancy embarrasses dolly about her dress when Dolly doesn’t have access to the same privileges that Nancy does. Bank manager: “But everyone needs savings” when Gladys says she’s never been in a bank, the white population has the privilege of using banks while the aborigines make do with tins to store their money. Pg 164 “Rapport with our customers is important –sorry”. Pg. 164 “Isn’t there any way?” Errol, when Gladys says she no longer needs the encyclopedia; The dears unlike whites would have trouble with paying for the encyclopedias and the metre box. Errol doesn’t understand this and there for asks the quote. “I’d think a hospital would be a more suitable place-“when Dolly goes into labour. Unlike white people Aboriginals babies are taken away at the hospitals and the dears are unlikely to be able to afford to go to the hospital. Gladys: -read and write” Errol starts to say that he’s never met anyone who cannot read or write and Gladys cuts him off. Gladys unlike Errol did not have the privilege of being taught yo read and write. Gladys had been sent to work for a family by a terrible mission manager and then had children and then there was the war, so she never had the opportunity Errol did. “Homemade glue” pg. 123 Errol: “What matters is you, not your address...”

Connection to Land and the Importance of Home

- When the floods hit Nan, Dolly and Gladys, their house is destroyed and they are forced to move out of their home, the importance of home comes from Gladys when she is determined to “Fix my own house.” Although Gladys does not have the money to fix her home she is sharing the importance of the connection to the land by not leaving and letting the white population win. Dolly also expresses the importance of home when Errol and Dolly argue about leaving the flats to live in the city, Dolly tells Errol that her world is about family rather than leaving them for what Errol calls a real home in the city. “I’m gunna go back and die there”- pg. 125 nan Gladys: “I’m not an interloper – I belong here – this is my land! “a real home is where there are people looking out for each other”- pg. 172 dolly Nan Dear: “Least here we do things our way.” The Rumbalara housing is “white and featureless” and “Anything but loveable.” (Stage directions) Radio: “the most vigorous attempt yet to solve Aboriginal housing” Gladys: ‘[I’m going] to fix my own house.” Errol: “It’s like your family’s from another country or something” Dolly: “Our life isn’t perfect, but like Nan says, it’s ours.” Gladys: “People is what matters.” Gladys: “Mrs Windsor, would you live at Rumbalara? Then why is it good enough for us?”

Types of Knowledge and Ways of Knowing

Much of the family’s energy is expended on searching for access to the institutions of the white majority. Gladys never had time for learning, to busy to earn a living to have time to make up for the education that she was denied when she was removed from her home. Gladys has knowledge that far eclipses that of the ‘encyclops boy’. Harrison addresses the sparce of the colonial model of education and the aboriginal knowledge and ways of learning have faced since colonisation.

Part 03 · Rainbow’s EndChapter 9 of 14

Character Analysis

Dolly Banks

Dolly is the teenage daughter of Gladys and granddaughter of Nan Dear. Throughout the play, she serves as a receptacle for her mother’s dreams and her grandmother’s fears. Gladys fantasies often about what a better life might look like, but she doesn’t believe that such a life is possible for her. Instead, she dreams on Dolly’s behalf, and she tries to encourage Dolly to pursue goals that seem impossible, like getting a job at a bank that does not want to hire Aboriginal employees. Nan Dear, meanwhile, views Dolly as vulnerable to the same discrimination and violence that Nan Dear and Gladys have experienced. She is especially wary when Dolly begins seeing Errol, a White boy. Dolly’s relationship with Errol is complicated by Errol’s unconscious biases, and she temporarily breaks things off with him when he is unable to put aside his assumption that White middle-class life is superior to the more community-based life Dolly wants. Her relationship with Errol also enrages Dolly’s cousin Leon, who sexually assaults her and leaves her pregnant. Despite the violent origin of the pregnancy, Dolly loves her baby and names it after Papa Dear, cementing baby Regina’s status as a beloved member of the family included in the traditions and legacy it carries.

Analysis

Dolly Banks is a crucial character in Jane Harrison’s play "Rainbow’s End," capturing the experiences of a young Aboriginal woman during the 1950s, a time rife with changes and challenges for her community. The daughter of Gladys Banks and the granddaughter of Nan Dear, Dolly is a character who embodies the bridge between the prior generation’s traditional views and the new generation’s aspirations. Throughout the play, Dolly asserts her desires and individuality in a society that would typically dismiss them. In one scene, she interacts with Errol, a character symbolising the broader society’s views, displaying her self-assured yet personable character. When Errol calls her "Miss Banks," she promptly corrects him, saying, "Dolly. We’re not too fussed about fancy titles," indicating both her lack of pretense. Errol complements her name, but once he learns her full name is Dolores, Dolly momentarily moves away, reflecting a subtle consciousness of boundaries and self-respect as she navigates her interactions with a non-Aboriginal man. Dolly’s aspirations and accomplishments are a point of pride for her mother, Gladys, who notes that Dolly is the first in the family to complete her Leaving Certificate, a testament to her intelligence and drive. The circumstances in which this achievement is presented, however, bring to light the stark realities of racial prejudices they confront. Gladys holds a photo of Dolly, which prompts the bank manager to acknowledge Dolly’s physical appearance but does not immediately understand the reason for Gladys’s visit. The scene showcases

Dolly through her mother’s eyes—as capable and promising— and reveals the obstacles that she would face in achieving her potential in a society that does not readily afford opportunities to Aboriginal people. Dolly’s character arc within the play reveals the nuanced complexities of identity, as she must balance the expectations of her cultural heritage with her own personal ambitions in a society that often sees her and her community as lesser. Her actions and decisions throughout the play demonstrate the younger generation’s determination to both honour their heritage and pursue a future they define for themselves. Through Dolly, Harrison presents themes of cultural identity, generational change, and the possibilities that lie ahead for Aboriginal women in a changing world.

Gladys Banks

Gladys is Nan Dear’s middle-aged daughter and Dolly’s mother. As a child, Gladys was taken away from her family by the government and put to work as a maid for a White family. As such, she never learned to read, and the only way she could make a life for herself was to follow in her mother’s footsteps as a manual labourer. Despite the hardship she has faced, Gladys is relentlessly optimistic. She frequently enters into fantasy sequences where the world is just, her family is happy, and Dolly has endless possibility before her. Even outside those fantasy sequences, Gladys believes that Dolly can live a better life than the generations of women before her. She is steadfast in this belief, even when Dolly herself doubts it. Gladys’s optimism proves a vital tool for her eventual activism. After Dolly accuses Gladys of projecting her own dreams onto Dolly, Gladys commits herself to working toward making the world a better place for the sake of herself and her family. She confronts the city council on the radio about Aboriginal housing, and she takes over Papa Dear’s speech at a public presentation of a petition when he fails to arrive on time. Gladys’s optimism lets her imagine and believe in a better future, which in turn empowers her to fight for that future.

Analysis

Gladys Banks represents the aspirations and challenges of an Aboriginal woman in 1950s Australia. This multi-dimensional character is caught between her cultural heritage and the desire for progress and recognition within the broader, often unfriendly, society. As the daughter of Nan Dear and mother to Dolly, Gladys mediates between the old ways and new possibilities that her daughter may have. A scene in the play that captures her ambitions and protective instincts as a mother is her confrontation with the bank manager where she presents her daughter Dolly for a teller’s position. Proudly asserting "She’s just completed her Leaving Certificate—the first in the family—with real good grades… ‘N’ top of her class in algebra," Gladys is met not with recognition but indifference, with the bank manager pouring himself tea but pointedly ignoring her. The scene captures the societal barriers that Gladys and her daughter face, as well as Gladys’s determination to push against them. Gladys’s agency is further highlighted in her firm stance on her daughter’s autonomy and social life. Despite Nan Dear’s apprehensions, Gladys declares decisively, "I’ll make the decisions regarding Dolores thank you. She’s going to the ball. And Errol Fisher is walking her home." This reveals her resolve and the assertion of her role as a parent, as well as the modernising viewpoint she holds compared to Nan Dear, who is more rooted in traditionalist concerns. Moreover, Gladys’s readiness to confront and challenge Nan Dear’s objections demonstrates her strong will as she endeavours to protect her children’s rights to forge their own paths. Gladys’s complexity extends into her views on housing and respect. When discussing the Queen’s visit and the humiliation of having their poor living conditions concealed behind hessian curtains, Gladys expresses upset not just over the cosmetic cover-ups but the deeper issue of decent housing and respect for her community. This highlights her awareness of the broader socio-political context and her aspiration for improved conditions for her people. Yet, she also values the autonomy they possess in their current living situation, preferring the freedom they enjoy at the shanty on the river to the restrictions experienced in more controlled environments like Cummeragunja, an Aboriginal settlement she and Nan Dear remember with claustrophobia. Gladys Banks embodies the transition between tradition and progress, acting as both a nurturer and a pioneer. Her interactions with others showcase her resolve to advance her family’s status despite the systemic challenges imposed by aw orld that often disregards their aspirations and dignity.

Nan Dear

Nan Dear is the matriarch of the family at the centre of the play. She is Gladys’s mother and Dolly’s grandmother, and she takes her duty of care to them seriously. Nan Dear is a bastion of tradition, teaching Dolly about her family history and aspects of their culture that she doesn’t learn at school. She has suffered under institutions of racism and ideologies of White supremacy, especially at the Aboriginal reserve in Cummeragunja, where Nan Dear was abused and her daughter Gladys was taken away from her. As such, Nan Dear has little faith that the world can improve, and she doesn’t understand Gladys and Dolly’s attempts to improve it. She especially fears for Dolly when Dolly begins a romantic relationship with Errol, a White boy with the same last name as a White man who raped Nan Dear years ago. Nan Dear works to separate Dolly and Errol, but at the end of the play she realises that Errol is a considerate and respectful young man. When she finds out for certain that Errol is not related to her attacker, she gives him her blessing and encourages Dolly to marry him, demonstrating that even someone as traditional as Nan Dear can grow and change.

Analysis

Born by the Murray River as her birth certificate states, Nan Dear is steeped in her Aboriginal heritage and connections to her traditional lands. Her identity is closely tied to the Murray River and the land from which she was displaced , specifically from Cummeragunja, marking a bitter and forcible removal from her home. This dislocation is a source of deep-seated resentment and shapes her worldview. Despite the trials she has faced, Nan Dear is characterised by a deep sense of duty and pragmatism . She is concerned with everyday survival and the practical needs of her family—working to put food on the table and keeping her family safe. As the matriarch, Nan Dear’s relationships with her daughter,Gladys, and granddaughter, Dolly, are a mix of affection and stern guidance. She is quick to offer wisdom to Dolly, such as reminding her of the importance of knowing family connections to avoid unwitting relationships with close relatives. Nan Dear’s knowledge of family history is implied to be extensive, though she may feign ignorance when it suits her, suggesting a complicated relationship with the past. Her cultural beliefs surface subtly through her reluctance to embrace the visiting monarchy, mocking the idea of needing white gloves for a royal visit and declaring, "She’s not my queen". This resistance to the monarch’s visit and the detachment she expresses towards Gladys’ interest in royalty reveals an underlying rejection of colonial authority and ap reference for the Aboriginal ways of life. Nan Dear’s resilience is not just a personal trait but serves as symbolic of her community’s endurance. Statements like "Born[by the Murray River] and by crikey I’m gunna go back and die there" demonstrate her strong will to return to her roots despite the adversity faced. This return, for her, is associated with embracing the simplicity and beauty of her origins, like having a feed of swan eggs before she passes, signifying a connection to her birthplace. Her experience of displacement is steeped in historical injustices, as evidenced by her bitter recollection of being forced to leave Cummeragunja. This comment on forced relocation exposes the reality of Aboriginal displacement and the loss of home and culture. These observations offer a critical perspective on the consequences of colonial policies for Aboriginal people.Nan Dear is a fiercely independent individual whose character lends gravity and authenticity to the narrative of "Rainbow’s End." Her dialogue is delivered with an undercurrent of knowing defiance, revealing a woman shaped by adversity yet undiminished in spirit. She represents a generation of Aborigina lpeople who, despite systematic disenfranchisement,maintained their dignity and cultural identity. Through her words and actions, Nan Dear confronts the audience with the realities of her community’s history, while also displaying an indomitable resolve to withstand and challenge these realities.

Errol Fisher

Errol Fisher is a young White encyclopedia salesman. He encounters Nan Dear, Gladys, and Dolly by accident, after taking a wrong turn on his route selling encyclopedias. Errol falls in love with Dolly and comes to admire her family. Gladys demonstrates a strength that Errol has never seen in his own mother, who is subservient to Errol’s father. The affection that Nan Dear and Gladys show Dolly is also alien to Errol, who has grown up with a strict and unfeeling father. Though he earnestly loves Dolly, he offends her with his ignorance of her culture and his assumption that his way of life must be superior to hers. He assumes she would be eager to leave her family behind to be his wife in the city, and he struggles to understand her insistence that she must remain with her loved ones. Ultimately, though, Errol comes to respect what Dolly wants for her future, and he promises to live any kind of life she wants as long as they can do so together.

Analysis

Fisher Errol Fisher emerges in Jane Harrison’s "Rainbow’s End" as a significant, though less central, character, who embodies the intersection of the Aboriginal community’s life and the broader Australian society during the 1950s. Portrayed initially as somewhat awkward and uncertain, he is introduced to the audience as he nearly falls off his bike upon encountering Dolly, signaling an immediate spark of attraction that complicates his interactions with the family. This initial interaction with Dolly sets the stage for Errol’s character development. The exchange with Gladys further illustrates his social position and perspective. When he tentatively approaches Gladys as she is chopping wood, mistaking her for a man, he immediately demonstrates cultural ignorance and nervousness. Apologising and stating his name, "Sorry er ma’am. My name is Errol Fisher," he provides a contrast to the women’s more assertive presence. This contrast is emblematic of the interplay between white Australians and Aboriginal people at the time—a dynamic of presumed authority and misunderstanding from the former, contrasted with an under-recognised competence and resilience from the latter. Errol’s intentions are primarily associated with business—selling encyclopedias as revealed in his conversation with Gladys—yet this transactional engagement serves as a backdrop to more complex intercultural exchanges. Gladys and Errol mention the encyclopedias simultaneously, indicating their previous interactions and the economic realities pressing on Gladys, who states, "We… I… won’t be needing them any more. All the shillings go into the meter box now," highlighting not only financial struggles but also the prioritisation of practical necessities over educational luxuries. His interactions with Dolly in the play are coloured with romantic interest, albeit fraught with the implications of such a relationship in a racially divided society. He compliments her name and attempts to bridge their cultural gap, however awkwardly. Since Errol also represents other characters in the play, his role is fundamental in bringing out the perspectives and reactions of Nan Dear, Gladys, and Dolly and propelling their narratives forward. Errol’s presence and character serve as a mirror that reflects and refracts the complexities of identity and interpersonal relationships within the stratified, mid-20th century Australia Australian society.

Leon (Cousin)

Leon, often referred to as simply “Cousin,” is Dolly’s cousin. He is aggressive and often drunk, and he disapproves of Dolly’s relationship with Errol. As a twisted form of punishment for that relationship, Leon rapes Dolly, resulting in her pregnancy. Leon’s assault of Dolly speaks to the reality of gendered violence within marginalised communities.

Papa Dear

Papa Dear is Nan Dear’s husband, Gladys’s father, and Dolly’s grandfather. Though he is not Gladys’s biological father, since Gladys was conceived from Nan Dear’s rape by a White man, Nan Dear considers him Gladys’s father. Papa Dear is an activist for Aboriginal rights, and his work takes him all over the country, which means he is rarely there for his family. He never appears in person in the play; he is only mentioned in dialogue and imagined in fantasy sequences. His absence leaves Nan Dear, Gladys, and Dolly alone to create an exclusively feminine space in their home.

Analysis

Dear Papa Dear, as depicted in Jane Harrison’s "Rainbow’s End," is a paternal figure whose presence is felt throughout the narrative despite being physically absent for much of the play. His character is crafted through the recollections and expectations of the other characters, particularly Nan Dear and Gladys. Papa Dear’s role is largely communicated through anecdotes and his influence on the family’s structure and values. Mentioned by Nan Dear as "busy doing good work. God’s work and hard work," Papa Dear is portrayed as a figure devoted to the betterment of the Aboriginal community. His dedication to activism and community work is further highlighted when Gladys recalls that he is "in Western Australia. Touring the communities there," and has been featured "in the newspaper and all," suggesting his actions and efforts are recognised and appreciated beyond their immediate surroundings. Papa Dear’s dream visitations reinforce his endearing place within the family, despite his absence. In an imagined scene, he is seen wearing "an old-fashioned hat and coat," hinting at a sense of nostalgia and timelessness associated with him. His brief entrance to kiss Gladys on the top of the head showcases a symbolic and loving connection with his family, despite his lack of direct intervention in their current struggles. The character of Papa Dear is particularly important to Dolly during a vulnerable moment when she reflects on her family tree, considering naming her unborn child Reg or Regina, "after Papa Dear." This indicates an enduring legacy and respect for him within the family, especially for his commitment to their people. While Papa Dear does not have a speaking role or much direct action, his presence resonates with the family and the audience, capturing the spirit of a generation of Aboriginal individuals who strived to improve conditions for their communities. Through the memories shared and the respect expressed by the characters, Papa Dear stands as a symbol of resilience, hope, and the ongoing struggle for activism and recognition within the Aboriginal community of the time.

The Inspector

The Inspector is a White man employed by the Australian government to inspect the living conditions of Aboriginal Australians, which includes the family’s humpy. As a representative of the Aboriginal Protection Board, the Inspector regulates and controls the lives of Aboriginal Australians, and that power extends to taking Aboriginal children away from their family. The Board took Gladys away from her family when she was a child, and she and Nan Dear are terrified that the Inspector will take Dolly away from them.

Analysis

In the play "Rainbow’s End," the inspector is a minor but telling character that presents a snapshot of the authority and surveillance that Aboriginal people endured in 1950s Australia. He appears in a scene where Nan and Gladys are inside the humpy, anxiously straightening everything, which sets a tone of tension and unease. The inspector serves as a symbol of the government oversight and control that loomed over the lives of the characters. Paying a visit to evaluate their living conditions, the inspector enters with a superficially pleasant demeanor that thinly veils the oppressive power dynamics at play. He greets the efforts of Nan and Gladys with an offhand compliment, "I say crocheted pillow shams. Such beautiful work!" which Gladys attributes to her mother’s handiwork.
