Sample 2 - High Scoring Rainbow’s End Essay

Topic: ‘Harrison’s play celebrates characters who show strength through adversity.’ Discuss. Set in Victorian town, Shepparton, Jane Harrison’s 2007 play, Rainbow’s End, explores the lives of three generations of Koori women as they navigate the setbacks and challenges faced by First Nations people in 1950s VictoriaIdeas & interpretationThe introduction plants the play in its precise historical decade — context as foundation, not garnish.. At a time where the government regulates the policies of AssimilationIdeas & interpretationNames the policy regime directly — the play's antagonist identified as a system. of the Indigenous community, these women are vulnerable to the impacts of racism, colonisation and suppression of knowledgeIdeas & interpretationThe introduction names the three pressures the body paragraphs will take in turn — a hidden essay map.. But despite facing discrimination at every turn, Harrison commemorates their resilience in the face of adversity. The women are able to become symbols of hope as they refuse to allow their struggles to define themIdeas & interpretationThe contention ends on transformation — struggle into symbol — giving the essay somewhere to travel.. Throughout Rainbow’s End, the struggle for the Indigenous community to pursue their dreams due to racial prejudice is highlighted in order to spotlight their ability to overcome it. This is demonstrated in scene ten in the bank managers office. Gladys tries to apply Dolly for a job as the bank teller, assuring the bank manager that she is fit for the job by advocating for her brains and witsStructure & evidenceThe scene is summarised only as far as the analysis needs — no plot for plot's sake.. But despite Dolly being a good candidate, the manager is hesitant about letting her apply. He argues that, “reliability is important,” implying that Dolly is unreliable despite never having met herIdeas & interpretationUnpacks the subtext of a single line of dialogue — inference is the skill that lifts evidence into analysis.. He makes this assumption due to his personal prejudiceIdeas & interpretationMotive inferred and named. Push further: connect the personal prejudice to the systemic policy the play indicts — the manager enforces a structure. to people of Dolly’s heritage. This demonstrates the racial discrimination that limits opportunities of First Nation’s people. Regardless of the bank manager’s doubts, Gladys pushes for the job as she is reminded of Dolly’s words, “have you learnt not to be shamed by them?”. Through this, Harrison highlights how the women’s power to overcome setbacks comes from within themselvesStructure & evidenceThe analysis returns to the topic's key idea and locates it precisely — topic-anchored, not merely theme-adjacent.. She champions these women for their ability to stand up for themselves in spite of the racially biased attitudes that are meant to limit them. This theme of hope is prevalent throughout the playStructure & evidenceThe paragraph ends by projecting the theme forward — a bridge to the next argument. and intends to inspire strength from within to tackle problemsIdeas & interpretationCloses on what the play wants for its audience — purpose-level thinking, beyond what the play shows.. Moreover, Harrison explores the intricacies of cultural identities and belonging, highlighting the strong connection to culture despite the colonisation that the characters face. Nan Dear is a compelling example of this. Nan’s frequent allusions to spirits or warnings from herLanguage & techniqueA speech pattern read as characterisation and cultural continuity at once — repetition treated as meaningful. “ancestors” demonstrate the strong indigenous traditions that survive and carry on even after the effects of colonisation. Intergenerational wisdom is significant in indigenous cultureIdeas & interpretationCultural context knowledge applied to interpretation — context serving the reading rather than decorating it. and Nan Dear’s frequent references to guidance from her ancestors attempts to pass that down through her childrenIdeas & interpretationNan's references read as transmission, not nostalgia — culture as an act, handed on., who may continue to succumb to assimilation but nevertheless is still deeply rooted in their cultural identitiesIdeas & interpretationChange and continuity held in one sentence — the nuance the topic's 'celebrates' deserves.. The reminders of tradition allows Harrison to assert that cultural identity cannot be erasedIdeas & interpretationA value-level claim about what the play asserts — ideas and values, the core of the first criterion. and is represented through characters like Nan Dear who have been through cultural erasure but continues to be a strong image of indigenous culture. Furthermore, resilience is present in overcoming adversity from the suppression of knowledge that characters throughout the play go through. Gladys is a representation of this as it is revealed to the reader that Gladys is illiterate. This was due to the assimilation policy that separated Gladys from her familyIdeas & interpretationA character's circumstance traced to a named historical policy — context doing causal work. and forced her into working at a young age rather than getting an education. Harrison demonstrates Gladys overcoming this in the last scene of the play where Gladys delivers a speech at the public meeting. Not only does she deliver it flawlessly, she delivers it with confidenceStructure & evidenceThe evidence is measured against the character's earlier self-doubt — change proven, not asserted., which Gladys has mentioned that she “gets nervous in front of people.” Harrison acknowledges the optimistic and determined attitude that Gladys uses as a tool to fight for her ambitions despite the systemic racism that has denied her basic human rights. Through Gladys Harrison aims to write about the ‘unsung’ heroes who were not expected to be a hero but was able to become one in spite of the adversity that she faces. In conclusion, “Rainbows End” champions the characters for their ability to overcome adversity due to their unwavering determination, offering a powerful testament to the power of human spirit when up against hardship. Through the use of quotes and direct references to the characters, Harrison tells a compelling story that aims to bring hope to the audience, reminding them that the difficulties that humanity faces is incomparable to the resilience that lies within each of us.

Sample 3 - High Scoring Rainbow’s End Essay

Topic: ‘Least here we do things our way- no one breathin’ down our necks’ Home is central to identity in Rainbow’s End. To what extent do you agree? Amongst the racism and oppression, it is clear that ‘home’ is of the utmost importance to the identity of Harrison’s Indigenous characters. Set against a backdrop of white Australian superiority in the 1950sIdeas & interpretationThe power structure named in the opening sentence — the essay's lens declared., ‘Rainbow’s End’ by Jane Harrison explores the central theme of ‘home’ in the context of identity. For the three generational main characters, their ‘humpy’ alongside the riverbank of the Mooroopna flats is integral to the make up of who they are and essential for Harrison to showcase the strength and resilience needed for First Nations to fight their marginalised status. Beyond the physical ‘home’, identity is shaped by the family and culture surrounding the characters through the lens of white and Indigenous Australians. Throughout the first part of the play, ‘home’ is physically represented as the ‘humpy’, a hut with a dirt floor and hessian-covered walls. Despite the poverty of the main character’s living conditions, it isn’t the place where they sleep that is important to themIdeas & interpretationDefines 'home' beyond the literal before arguing — conceptual ground-clearing that lifts the whole response. as much as the culture surrounding them. For the character of Nan, "the Murray…Is your place isn’t it” due to the immense sense of belonging she feels after her oppressive experienceStructure & evidenceBackstory used to explain present attachment — cause-and-effect reading across the play's timeline. with the Cummeragunja mission and the close proximity of her Aboriginal ‘family.’ In this way Harrison highlights the role culture and a physical ‘home’ playsStructure & evidenceThe evidence is explicitly handed back to the author — 'Harrison highlights' keeps authorial construction in view, which the criteria reward. in an individual’s identity. In addition, the pride taken in the appearance and homeliness of the ‘humpy’ is a further aspect of identity illustrated by Harrison. When in the play it comes time for the inspector to visit to the ‘humpy’, Harrison has Nan and Gladys ‘nervously’ straightening the cushionsLanguage & techniqueA single stage-direction adverb carries the family's fear — micro-evidence used precisely. and draws attention to the ‘beautiful work’ of the crocheted pillows, emphasising the importance of ‘home’ to the characters. This is brought to the attention of the audience again during the move to the ‘white and featureless’ Rumberala housing, and the characters immediately think of ‘making curtains’Structure & evidenceA tiny textual detail deployed to prove a large claim about identity — precise, purposeful evidence selection. as a way to reassociate their identity with the new housing. The contrast between the ‘home’ of the white Australian charactersStructure & evidenceComparative evidence across social worlds — analysis organised around contrast, a top-band structural habit. such as Errol and Nancy, and the housing in Rumbalara and the flats is reflective of the identities of the characters and how they feel entitled to act. For example, Nancy is portrayed as wealthy and privileged and therefore acts in a privileged and entitled wayIdeas & interpretationA minor white character used as a control group for the essay's claims about home and entitlement. Push further: one concrete Nancy moment would seal it., whilst the main characters are substantially underprivileged and act humble, with kindness despite adversities. The playwright suggests ‘home’ is more than a physical establishment but extends into a ‘home’ found within family and culture. The notion of family and culture as a ‘home’ is a central theme throughout the play, explicitly explored between the misalignment of Errol and Dolly’s definition of a ‘home’Structure & evidenceThe paragraph is built on a definitional clash between two characters — argument by contrast, the strongest way to organise a 'to what extent' discussion.. For the character of Errol, he believes a ‘home’ is a place to live, where only the wealth and geographical opportunities are relevant, however, Harrison utilises his conversation with Dolly to pinpoint to ideal that “a real home is where people are looking out for each other,” regardless of how many ‘presents’ are under the treeIdeas & interpretationThe material-versus-relational distinction is drawn from the play's own imagery — the analysis speaks the text's language while making its own point.. Furthermore, Errol reflects on the differences of their families, expressing to Dolly that his Dad “expects me to call him sir” and ultimately concluding that despite the poverty of the flat’s he bets ‘you have more fun than we do’. This conversation was pivotal to underlining the impactStructure & evidenceThe scene's function in the play's argument is named — pivotal — before the paragraph closes. Push further: say what changes after this conversation. of a ‘home’ with a supportive family and rich culture in creating a luster for life and powerful sense of identity. For the characters in the play, ‘home’ is closely intertwined with colonial suppression, the white Australian’s control over Aboriginal housing had a heavy influence on the identity of the characters. Throughout the play, Harrison makes reference to a difference in the perception of Aboriginals from the flat’s and ‘townie types of cross-over Aborigines’ depicting those who lived in town as slightly better off, with better opportunities. This differentiation underpins the impact of location on identity as the ‘white fellas’ feel the Indigenous characters need to ‘be absorbed into the community’ in order to be ‘assimilated’ and forced to act in alignment with white culture. The control that the white characters, such as the inspector, assert over housing is detrimental to the identity of the characters, it withdraws their control and forces them to abandon their cultureIdeas & interpretationThe mechanism of oppression is spelled out as a process — control withdrawn, culture abandoned — not just labelled as racism. in order to be accepted. Moreover, Harrison suggests that a ‘home’ can affect social mobility, Gladys in particular is obsessed with ‘fitting in’ and being accepted and it becomes a large part of her identity. The ‘white gloves’ and ‘Auntys shoes’ are a symbol of her need to assimilateLanguage & techniqueCostume props read symbolically — concrete objects carrying the assimilation theme. Push further: note who gives these objects and what that adds. to white culture, acting in direct contrast to the Aboriginal culture that makes up her ‘home’ and thereby her identity. Throughout the entirety of the play, Harrison brings forth the different types of ‘home’, both physical and as a culmination of family and cultureStructure & evidenceThe conclusion holds the essay's two definitions of home together — the whole argument recapped in one balanced phrase.. In this way it is evident to the audience that home is closely entwined with the identity of all the characters in the play, from Errol who has never experienced a ‘home’ within a family, to Dolly whom does not associate a ‘real home’ with a fancy establishment but instead with a loving family and cultural traditions. The exploration into the impacts of oppressive attempts from the ‘white fellas’ to force ‘assimilation’, displays how a ‘home’ is more than a roof over one’s head, but instead an embodiment of an individual’s unique identityIdeas & interpretationThe final sentence answers the topic's 'to what extent' with a settled, earned definition — resolution, not repetition. as formed by relationships, culture, pride and resilience.

Sample 4 - High Scoring Rainbow’s End Essay

Topic: Harrison’s play celebrates characters who show strength through adversity. Discuss. Jane Harrison’s play Rainbows End is a powerful exploration of the lives of three generations of Indigenous women, Nan Dear, her daughter Gladys, and granddaughter Dolly, in 1950s rural Australia. Harrison presents their daily struggles with poverty, limited access to education, and systemic racism, not to victimise them, but to celebrate their resilienceIdeas & interpretationThe introduction already interprets authorial purpose — engaging the topic verb 'celebrates' head-on rather than orbiting it. and determination. Through her portrayal of these women, Harrison critiques the entrenched hierarchies that marginalise Indigenous communities, while affirming the strength required to survive and resist. Ultimately, the play celebrates the capacity of Indigenous women to endure adversity, even as it recognises the costs of their survival. Harrison portrays how racial discrimination restricts Indigenous Australians’ opportunities and dreams. This is evident in Scene Ten, when Gladys visits the bank in the hope of securing Dolly a position as a teller. Gladys proudly emphasises her daughter’s academic success, noting that Dolly has completed her “leaving certificate”Structure & evidenceCredentials quoted before the rejection — the injustice set up to be felt. and is “top of her class,” in an attempt to counteract the stereotypes imposed upon Indigenous people. However, the bank manager dismisses Dolly’s achievements, questioning how she would “fit in” and whether she could be “relied on” to attend work daily, despite never having met her. Through the portrayal of the bank manager, Harrison highlights how deep-seated structural racism embedded in Australian institutions causes harmful stereotypes that prevent Indigenous communities from fulfilling their full potential in society, denying them equal access to employment. By juxtaposing the women’s ambitions with the bank manager’s prejudicesStructure & evidenceNames the structural technique (juxtaposition) and explains what it critiques — technique tied to meaning, never left as a label., Harrison critiques the injustice of a culture that confines Indigenous familiesIdeas & interpretationThe paragraph rises from a single scene to a claim about the culture the play indicts — scene to society. to “picking” and other forms of low-status labour regardless of meritIdeas & interpretationThe critique is grounded in the economics of the era — historical insight woven into the argument, not bolted on.. Yet she also affirms Gladys’ resilience, showing her refusal to abandon her daughter’s dreams despite repeated setbacks. In doing so, Harrison not only exposes how systemic racism denies equality but also celebrates the persistence of Indigenous women who continue to assert dignity and demand recognition against such barriers. Harrison exposes the entrenched poverty endured by Indigenous families to critique the systemic marginalisation of First Nations communities. Rather than presenting poverty as a personal failingIdeas & interpretationThe paragraph opens by rejecting the wrong reading before offering the right one — pre-empting a misinterpretation., Harrison illustrates how social and economic exclusion force the Dear family into degrading conditions and low-cost labour, while simultaneously highlighting their resilience in preserving dignity and hopeIdeas & interpretationThe topic sentence holds critique and celebration in one clause — the essay's balance in miniature.. Nan Dear’s ironic description of their riverbank “humpy” as “our own little palace” re-frames deprivation as pride, transforming humiliation into humourLanguage & techniqueThe irony of 'our own little palace' is unpacked into its double movement — two transformations in one line of dialogue.. Similarly, Gladys expresses frustration at Dolly’s reliance on “hand-me-downs,” revealing her determination to secure independence for her daughterStructure & evidenceEach quotation is immediately converted into what it reveals — evidence never left to speak for itself.. By infusing moments of humour and defiance into depictions of poverty, Harrison refuses to allow Indigenous identity to be defined by shameIdeas & interpretationA strong authorial-intent claim that the paragraph has earned through its examples — assertion after proof, not before.. Instead, she asserts the capacity of women like Nan and Gladys to maintain agency and self-respect in the face of adversity. Dolly’s dream sequence after her visit to the town tip symbolisesLanguage & techniqueA theatrical device — the dream sequence — used as evidence. Form-aware analysis of drama, which assessors specifically credit. a longing for a future beyond dispossession, foregrounding aspirations that colonial systems sought to deny. Through these portrayals, Harrison critiques structural inequalities, particularly the exploitation of Indigenous people as fruit pickers and scavengers, while simultaneously celebrating the resilience and strength of Indigenous women to endure and reimagine possibility. Thus, Harrison’s authorial intent lies not only in exposingStructure & evidenceThe paragraph resolves at the level of authorial intent with a not-only-but-also structure — a two-part verdict. the material realities of Indigenous poverty but also in affirming the power of humour, pride and aspiration as acts of survival against systemic injustice. Knowledge is presented in the play as both a pathway to empowermentStructure & evidenceA topic sentence with a doubled claim — the paragraph promises complexity and then delivers both halves. and a means of representing the differences in wisdom. Gladys has a strong belief that schooling will elevate Dolly beyond ingrained disadvantage, insisting that she “get an education” so she can “be someone.” This determination highlights her strength as a mother, willing to challenge generational cycles of poverty. Harrison deliberately frames this ambition with ironyIdeas & interpretation'Deliberately' keeps the playwright's designing hand in view — irony treated as constructed, not accidental.: while Dolly’s academic success is undeniable, she remains judged through stereotypes and institutional racism, suggesting that education alone cannot dismantle systemic barriers. At the same time, Harrison complicates the notion of knowledge by staging contrasts between Indigenous oral tradition and Western authority. When Errol dismisses Dolly’s experience with snakes, Gladys’ clarification that it was a harmless “carpet snake” undermines the authority of the encyclopedia and asserts that lived experience offers forms of wisdom inaccessible to formal schooling. By juxtaposing these knowledge systems, Harrison critiques the way white institutions privileged their own frameworks as universal while marginalising Indigenous perspectives. Her authorial intent is doubled to expose how education in the 1950s was arranged by race and class, yet also to celebrate the resilience of families like the Dears, who embraced education without relinquishing pride in their cultural knowledge. Harrison both honours the courage of Indigenous families who value education as a tool for advancement and critiques the system of schooling planted between classes in the 1950s. Harrison’s Rainbow’s End critiques the systemic racism that restricted Indigenous Australians while celebrating the resilience of women who refused to be silenced. By exposing institutional prejudice, entrenched poverty and competing forms of knowledge, she highlights both the barriers that denied equality and the strength of Indigenous families who asserted dignity despite them. In doing so, Harrison affirms that hope, humour and cultural pride are not just survival strategies but powerful acts of resistance against injustice.

Sample 5 - High Scoring Rainbow’s End Essay

Topic: ‘Rainbow’s End is a bitter condemnation of colonisation.’ Discuss. Cemented within a world enveloped by overpowering white culture, Rainbows End seeks to explore the lengths of condemnation expressed by Indigenous Australians, juxtaposed with the desire to be includedIdeas & interpretationThe introduction frames the play as a tension between two impulses — a contention with genuine interpretive risk.. Within the play, Harrison blatantly reveals consistent resistance and dislike aimed towards white authority yet contrasts this with younger generations who desire integration and inclusion in society. By depicting Indigenous women in this way, Harrison can characterise older generations as resistant to white knowledge and eternally furious at the erasure of Indigenous culture yet depicts younger characters as open to colonisation to succeed in their corrupted society. Overall, Harrison criticises the unjust dilution of Indigenous culture, simultaneously advocating for Indigenous Australians to continue fighting to be heardIdeas & interpretationThe contention ends by naming the play's call to action — the reading matches Harrison's own stated purpose for writing. over white dominance. Harrison calculatingly embeds Indigenous resistance throughout the play, through unwavering denial, which represents a resilience to maintain Indigenous culture, despite attempts to silence it. Harrison evidence this though Nan Dear showing her despise for Errol’s encyclopedias, asking him what it says, “in that there encyclops about Aboriginals”. Harrison’s lexical choice of characterising Nan through her informal and grammatically incorrect languageLanguage & techniqueReads dialect as a deliberate authorial choice carrying meaning — language-level evidence handled with metalanguage. of “that there” and “encyclops”, illustrates Nan’s refusal to submit to white assistance in education, as the encyclopedias are based off Eurocentric knowledgeIdeas & interpretationThe object of Nan's scorn is explained — her resistance is reasoned, not stubborn, and the essay shows why., maintaining her stance in continuing Aboriginal knowledge and cultureIdeas & interpretationNan's refusal framed as preservation, not obstinacy — the essay takes her side seriously. no matter the state of her academics. Nan Dear also directs this hate towards Errol, as he is the delivery boy for them, and is white, as she sees him as a messenger for white authorityIdeas & interpretationErrol is read through Nan's eyes — the essay inhabits a character's perspective to explain her behaviour., being cautious of letting him get too closeIdeas & interpretationBehaviour toward Errol explained through history — wariness given its reasons.. Harrison lambasts the erasure of Indigenous knowledge, yet cautiously condemns the anger being taken out on unrelated and innocent white individualsIdeas & interpretationThe essay lets Harrison critique both directions — rare interpretive courage that avoids a one-sided reading.. Harrison juxtaposes this condemnation of colonisation through dream sequences, when Gladys begins to dream of the possibility of Dolly becoming “a graduate”. Gladys’ willingness to accept and integrate into the complexities of white society in order to provide her daughter with a proper education, contrasts that of Nan’s ideologies relating to white assistance, demonstrating the differences in generational beliefsStructure & evidenceThe contrast between Nan and Gladys is named as the paragraph's organising axis — character pairs as argument.. As encyclopedias are based on white knowledge, this further illustrates Gladys’ will to accept their knowledge as superior to succeed. The dream sequence represents Gladys’ subconscious suggestion that her daughter may never be able to be “a graduate” due to her needing to follow Nan’s beliefs as the eldest of the family, being constrained to the limitations rightful morals to uphold Indigenous independence. Harrison champions the pursuit of education despite society restraining full ability yet criticisesIdeas & interpretationA balanced verdict sentence — champions yet criticises — showing the play's position is double-edged, not simple. the neglect of protecting Indigenous culture and forgetting true identity. Harrison impatiently lambasts the evident erasure of Indigenous culture, through white authorities directly and indirectly pushing it, which exposes their never-ending desire to abolish Aboriginal knowledge and existence. Harrison represents this through the humpy inspector asking if Gladys has heard of “the concept of assimilation”, as a promise that “things will change.” The contradictory nature of the inspectors’ intentions and promisesStructure & evidenceThe analysis holds the inspector's words against his behaviour — contradiction used as proof. represent white ignorance towards Indigenous perspectives and opinions, as the inspector didn’t let Gladys answer if she has heard of the “concept” of assimilation, continuing to talk about it as if she isn’t thereLanguage & techniqueA staging detail read for power — who is allowed to speak in the scene becomes evidence of erasure.. The inherent dominance from the inspector pushing for the absorption of Indigenous Australians into white society conveys the collective idea of abolishment, and then stating that “knowledge is power” continues to falsely represent the freedom of Indigenous Australians white authorities distribute. Harrison demonises the dominance of white imposed hierarchies that put Indigenous Australians at an unfair disadvantage, keeping them there without proper consultation. Harrison further bolsters this through the stage direction of Gladys and Nan “exchange[ing] worried looks”, after the inspector’s suggestion. Their “worried looks” emphasise the desire to remain where they are, away from the “mercy of the manager” which Nan Dear recalls from her traumatic days of living at a mission. The family can easily accept help from white authorities by submitting to their dominance yet refuse to so they can maintain their independence and keep their cultural heritage and identity intact, living of their own free will. Their desire to stay at their humpy and not receive better living conditions demonstrates their appreciation for the opportunity to live by themselves and the gratitude for where they are, no matter the difficulties that surround these unfavourable living circumstances. Harrison bolsters the idea of maintaining Indigenous heritage but questions the resistance towards white assistance if it is at the expense of health and safety. Harrison diametrically contradicts the stereotypical resentment towards colonisation through Indigenous youth desiring to submit to white dominance, which raises questions about the rightful path to follow when aiming to achieve Indigenous independence. Harrison depicts this through Dolly’s desire to be included in white society, when Dolly is attending a dance in Shepparton, and exclaims how “everyone’s going to be there”. Dolly being the youngest of the family is the most exposed to whitewashing and diluted Indigenous culture, hence her powerful ambition to attend dances and wear Eurocentric 1950s outfits and accessories, to be like “everyone” who’s going “to be there”. Her outfit is symbolic of the tensions between her socio-economic status and her dreams, acting as a constant reminder of her inability to completely be accepted in white society. Harrison praises the confidence of Indigenous youth to break free from limiting social and gender norms in order to achieve one’s dreams yet warns against the dangers of being denied acceptance from such rebellion. Harrison further reinforces a rebellion in the opposition to white superiority through Gladys’ dialogue when she dares to believe that Dolly can succeed by not letting “her address stop her in life”. Gladys’ rhetorical question towards Nan exemplifies the difference and experience of generations, Gladys believing Indigenous women can succeed without the acceptance of white people. She is met by Nan Dear’s cold response telling her to “get a grip”, snapping her back to reality and reminding her of the fact that they are a minority that is disadvantaged in a backwards society that will never favour them. Gladys daring to stray from the wisdom of her mother represents an underlying feeling of hope for change, despite claims and actions that proves change is unlikely to occur. Gladys and Dolly don’t harbour severe resentment towards their society like Nan Dear, as they have less experience with unwavering discrimination. It outlines their desires to blend into the white community at the expense of their autonomy, in order to gain increased access and succeed in achieving a better life than what they have. Harrison champions the unwavering objective of striving to be better in one’s life, while cautioning of the implications of aiming too high and ignoring the safety provided by one’s community. Conclusively, Harrison continuously exhibits justified resistance to colonisation of Indigenous culture yet remains hopeful that younger generations may have the courage to break the discriminatory cycle. Harrison encapsulates this through continued vilification of white knowledge and education, and the injustice of institutional authority, while postulating a juxtaposed change in views through the acceptance of colonisation within younger generations. Harrison ultimately condemns the unjust erasure of Indigenous culture and identity, while pushing the notion that the amalgamation of cultures may lead to increased opportunities for Indigenous Australians and should not be seen as an entirely demonised occurrence.

Part 04

Analytical Text Response Essays

In this part

1Introduction2What is Needed in an Essay3Planning4Analytical Text Response Structure5Proofreading6Prompt Types: Discuss, To What Extent, How Does…
Part 04 · Analytical Text Response EssaysChapter 1 of 5

Introduction

From the very beginning of your schooling career, you have been introduced to several different methods of writing essays. Although all structures have their advantages and disadvantages, it is essential that you have a fool-proof method of writing essays for your end of year examination. Having a solid method of writing analytical essays and practicing these throughout the year will result in a strong foundation, enabling you to tackle any essay question with confidence. The key to success lies not only in understanding the content but also in being able to present your ideas in a clear, structured, and coherent manner. With a well-practised approach, you will be able to break down complex texts, analyse them effectively, and construct a well-supported argument that demonstrates both depth and clarity of thought. In the following, we will explore a structured approach to writing an analytical text response. By familiarising yourself with this framework, you will be better equipped to organise your ideas, manage your time efficiently, and ultimately maximise your performance in the exam.

Part 04 · Analytical Text Response EssaysChapter 2 of 5

What is Needed in an Essay

An essay is a structured piece of writing that presents an argument, analysis, or narrative on a particular topic. The purpose of an essay is to communicate a clear, well-reasoned point of view, often backed by evidence and examples. Essays are typically composed of several key parts: an introduction, body paragraphs, and a conclusion. Each section plays a specific role in conveying the writer’s ideas and helping the reader understand and engage with the topic at hand.

Introduction

The introduction serves as the starting point of the essay, setting the stage for the discussion to follow. It introduces the topic, provides necessary background information, and presents the thesis statement, which outlines the main argument or purpose of the essay. A strong introduction grabs the reader’s attention, often through a compelling hook, and establishes the importance of the topic. It should also provide a clear roadmap of what the essay will cover, guiding the reader’s expectations and providing a sense of structure.

Body Paragraphs

The body of an essay is where the main arguments or points are developed and supported with evidence, examples, or analysis. Typically, an essay contains three body paragraphs, although this can vary depending on the length and depth of the topic. Each paragraph should focus on a single aspect of the thesis statement and contribute to the overall argument.

Conclusion

The conclusion is the final part of the essay, where the writer sums up the key points made in the body paragraphs and reinforces the thesis statement. It provides closure to the discussion and leaves a lasting impression on the reader. A strong conclusion restates the main argument in a fresh way, summarising the key insights presented in the essay. It may also offer suggestions for further exploration or suggest the broader implications of the topic.

Part 04 · Analytical Text Response EssaysChapter 3 of 5

Planning

Deconstructing the Prompt

When first planning your essay, you must directly respond to the prompt that is presented to you, and consider the key authorial messages that could help you respond with insight. This involves three important steps:

  • Find the key concepts. This will most likely correlate with the key concepts you have studied throughout the unit, you should also make sure to identify synonyms of these concepts too!

  • Find any instructional verbs. These are words that instruct you to answer the prompt in a certain manner, in English the main verbs to look out for is discuss, do you agree, to what extent or how.

  • Identify any expectations/limitations. These ore words that limit what you con include in your essays and set on expectation on what you should be discussing.

Example prompt: ‘The dream sequences in Rainbow’s End are the characters’ only escape from the depressing reality of life.’ Do you agree? This is a very straightforward prompt—you need to demonstrate a multiple-sided response to what Harrison perceives as the role of dreams and aspirations in her characters’ lives. There are no limits to what should be discussed here, but you are expected to focus your discussion on the ideas of dreams, escape, and reality as they relate to the values, identities, and choices of Nan Dear, Gladys, and Dolly. Consider the historical context of 1950s assimilationist Australia, racism, class, and the characters’ evolving sense of agency throughout the play.

Contention

Now that you understand what the prompt is asking, we can begin to think about what our interpretation of Harrison’s message is. Let’s consider the first prompt again: ‘The dream sequences in Rainbow’s End are the characters’ only escape from the depressing reality of life.’ Do you agree? We can approach this in multiple ways. Here are three simplistic interpretations (agree, disagree, somewhere in between):

  • Harrison suggests that dreams offer the characters comfort, but they cannot change the reality of their circumstances.

  • Harrison implies that dreams are the characters’ only refuge from a society that denies them dignity and opportunity.

  • Harrison demonstrates that it is action, not dreaming, that ultimately allows the characters to escape their circumstances.

However, these interpretations are quite general and lack specificity. They don’t tell the reader exactly what you are arguing. Let’s improve them with more precise language and deeper knowledge of the text:

  • Harrison portrays dreams as both sustaining and limiting, suggesting that Gladys’s fantasies of meeting the Queen preserve her dignity while simultaneously revealing how deeply colonial ideals of acceptance have been internalised.

  • Harrison implies that, for a family denied housing, education, and respect, the imagination becomes a vital site of survival and self-worth in a society that offers no material escape.

  • Harrison critiques passive dreaming by charting Gladys’s transformation from daydreamer to activist, suggesting that genuine escape from injustice comes not from fantasy but from finding one’s voice and demanding change.

These improved interpretations use specific language, engage with the complexity of the characters’ motivations, and demonstrate a sophisticated understanding of the play’s ideas.

Extending Sentences - Connectives

To create a ‘sophisticated’ idea you need to avoid single ideas, all of the contentions above are singular and lack connections to other ideas. We can connect them together using three simple connectives (and their advanced synonyms!)

Useful Conjunctions and Their Purposes
ConjunctionPurposeSynonyms
BecauseThis conjunction explains why the information you have stated is true, demonstrating greater understanding of the author’s purpose.As, as a result of, considering, being, for, through, seeing as.
ButThis conjunction adds contradictory information, allowing for greater debate.However, nevertheless, yet, despite, even so, nonetheless, except.
SoThis conjunction allows for inferencing the cause and effect of the statement, which demonstrates greater thinking and ideas about the text.Therefore, consequently, accordingly, leading to.

From here, we can take these singular idea contentions and extend them with these connectives in order to make multi-idea contentions that can create sophisticated links between ideas:

Topic Sentences

Now we can create topic sentences that allow to explore complex ideas linked using BECAUSE, BUT & so.

Authorial Message/Intent - NOT PLOT OR CHAPTER!

The sentence should be focused on an authorial message, not just a concept or character­ make the author active -the most common start is the author+ verb: E.g Harrison explores/communicates/illustrates etc.

Authorial Verbs
Analytical Verbs for Text Analysis
ConveysExploresDemonstratesEmphasises
CommunicatesDepictsIllustratesChallenges
IdentifiesFramesRevealsHighlights
StatesImpliesSuggestsUnderlines
Prepositional Phrases

Another way to structure this sentence is to use a prepositional phrase at the beginning of the sentence that introduces ideas to vary your sentence structure:

Throughout the text, Harrison explores..

Textual Analysis Phrases and Sentence Starters

ThroughFrom the outsetIn the absence ofUnder the
ByBeyond theWithin theTo the extent
DuringConcerning theWithout
Specificity and Relevancy

Examiners punish vague or general statements, especially those that lack clear links to the topic or the author’s message. For example: By emphasising the importance of dreams in the play, Harrison shows that they matter to Gladys. This is not technically incorrect—but it is far too general and superficial. It doesn’t demonstrate insightful analysis, nor does it show a clear understanding of Harrison’s authorial intent. This kind of vague sentence would not receive full marks. Here’s an improved version: By emphasising Gladys’s fantasies of royal recognition, Harrison portrays how dreams function not as an escape from reality, but as a fragile shield of dignity for those denied respect and opportunity by an assimilationist society. This is better. It is clear, specific, well-structured, and shows awareness of both the theme (dreams vs reality), character motivation, and authorial message. It also directly links to the contention: that dreams sustain the characters, even in circumstances that offer no material escape. The issue you will face is that there is only so much you can discuss in your paragraph from here, as you have only examined one idea—dreams sustain dignity. To fix this, lets’ go back to an old friend:

Because, But, So - Make a Complex Sentence!

Too often, students fail to complete long paragraphs because they are stuck repeating themselves or have run out of things to say about their topic they introduced. Therefore, if we have two ideas in our topic sentence, we can ‘hinge’ these sentences together which demonstrate complex understanding, writing and structure - all of them are key parts of the rubric you are judged on! Here’s an example:

By emphasising Gladys’s daydreams of royal recognition, Harrison conveys how, for a family denied dignity and opportunity, the imagination becomes a vital refuge from an unjust reality—ultimately suggesting that dreams alone cannot deliver change, and that true escape demands the courage to speak and act.

Now, you have two linked ideas:

  • Dreams preserve dignity and hope for the characters in an unjust society.

  • Dreaming alone, without action, cannot deliver genuine change.

This provides a clear structure for an entire paragraph and sets you up to use a hinge sentence later to develop further layers—e.g., how this ‘dreaming” can also reveal internalised colonial ideals or delay confrontation with injustice. By planning your paragraph in this way, you’ll ensure:

  • Specific and sophisticated analysis

  • Strong authorial intent

  • Complex understanding of the characters’ motivations and Harrison’s critique of assimilationist policies in 1950s Australia

Part 04 · Analytical Text Response EssaysChapter 4 of 5

Analytical Text Response Structure

Topics

There are three types of questions you may receive on a SAC or exam. These are the ‘Discuss’, ‘To what extent’ and ‘Do you agree?’ statements. These are all tackled in a slightly varying manner.

  • Discuss : This is probably the most common type of prompt that you will encounter. When dealing with such prompts, it is important that you discuss as many aspects of it as possible. One way to improve your discussion here is to include a paragraph that rebuts the prompt, like what you do in your persuasive essays. However, since it is a discuss prompt, you do not need to make the two sides of your argument extremely distinct.

  • To what extent: These topics require you to make a decision. Never say ‘it is to some extent’ in your contention. You really need to assign a portion of something. The best way to do this is to start with subordinating conjunctions like ‘whilst” or ‘although”. To What Extent questions rarely want you to agree or disagree - they want you to explore a shade of grey.

  • How: The ‘How’ topics usually focus on the construction of a text - your evidence should contain metalanguage like ‘characterisation’ for instance.

Introduction

The introduction is the first piece of your writing that will be examined. For this reason, it should be clear, direct and show your understanding of the topic. The introduction is there to show your examiner how you wish to respond to the set topic. If your topic contains a quote, ensure that you mention it somewhere in your introduction to show you are addressing the topic.

Structure

An introduction must include the following:

A General Line of introduction

Too often our introductions lack any insight and mainly begin with author+text+background information which has nothing to do with the actual topic. We should attempt to be relevant with our opening statement and discuss why this concept is important.

Textual Analysis Starters, Verbs, and Noun Phrases

Introduce the Text, Author and Contention

Now we introduce the text and author and the audience. Then state how it relates to the topic specifically. These need to be connected fluently, when writing we should be relying on connectives to make sure there is a logical flow in our expression. Depending on the prompt itself, it might be a wise idea to link either/both of characters and setting to the topic of the essay prompt. We can do this as such: Linking a Character to a Topic:

Sentence Starters: Character Discovery and Purpose
Introductory PhraseVerb of DiscoveryLinking Phrase

Realise/s

N.B. that the name of the book is always underlined. I.e. Sunset Boulevard or Rainbow’s End. Contention A contention is the author’s point of view on the topic. Since you are the author in this scenario, you need to write what your position on the topic. A contention should be short, to the point, and engage with the topic. On this falls the crux of your essay. Do not be afraid to agree/disagree with a topic. You are allowed to have a conditional contention.

Body Paragraphs

The body paragraphs form majority of your essay. The body paragraphs are the main reasons that the contention your provided is true. Your body paragraphs need to be able to demonstrate your understanding of the text and the fluency of your language. You should ensure that every sentence (and thus every word) brings something towards your argument.

Structure

Below is the structure for a body paragraph using ‘Th.E. S.A.M.L.’ Method. This method of writing body paragraphs was developed in order to maximise possible marks. Only the ‘TH’ and ‘L’ elements of this structure need to be in the order given.

Th
Thematic-based topic sentence

Your topic sentence needs to be based upon a theme in the text, not a character.

✗ ‘Norma Desmond’s obsession with herself ultimately leads to her downfall.’
✓ ‘Obsession with self is what ultimately leads to the downfall of characters.’
E
Evidence

Quotes and scenes from the text that support the theme.

S
Symbols

Symbols that relate to this theme — including historical and social context, and setting.

A
Authorial intention

What was the author intending?

M
Metalanguage

Discuss the metalanguage the author uses around this theme.

L
Linking sentence

Link back to your contention.

Only the Th and L elements need to appear in this order — the middle elements can be woven through the paragraph.

Basic Example ‘Th.E S.A.M’

‘Obsession with one’s self is what ultimately leads to the downfall of characters. This can be seen with Norma exclaiming ‘who else?’ when Joe questions her about her script. The script itself is also a representation of Norma’s obsession as she has written several hundred pages where she stars in every scene. Wilder uses this to demonstrate that Norma is unable to move on from anything besides herself. The slow camera pan whenever the script is shown foreshadows Norma’s impending doom which links is inherently linked to her self-absorption. In this way, obsession with self ultimately leads to the downfall of several characters in Sunset Boulevard.’

Writing About Ideas: Adding Adjectives to Nouns

One of the easiest ways of improving your writing is to add adjectives to nouns, this helps us to create an ‘idea phrase’. Here is a list of commonly used nouns in analytic writing, a good way is to use these as springboards to finding even more complex and advanced nouns.

We then can pair these ideas with adjectives of degree - these tell us how big, small, frequent, fast or slow something is, here is a list to help build your writing vocabulary:

Hinge Sentences

In your topic sentence, you can introduce two related ideas. This creates room for a more complex, insightful paragraph. But remember: when you shift focus from the first idea to the second, you must signal this change to the reader clearly. This is where the hinge sentence comes in. A hinge sentence acts like a bridge—it acknowledges what you’ve already explored, then transitions to the second idea you introduced in your topic sentence. Here’s how that works:

By emphasising Gladys’s daydreams of royal recognition, Harrison conveys how, for a family denied dignity and opportunity, the imagination becomes a vital refuge from an unjust reality—ultimately suggesting that dreams alone cannot deliver change, and that true escape demands the courage to speak and act.

Evidence + Analysis of First Idea

In the dream sequences where Gladys imagines being received by the Queen, Harrison frames fantasy not as idle escapism but as a shield of dignity for a woman excluded from the “decent” society she longs to join. Yet Nan Dear’s blunt dismissal—“And what bloody good would that do? Daydreams!” (p. 154)—exposes the limits of this refuge, highlighting how dreaming leaves the family’s material circumstances untouched.

Hinge Sentence (transitioning to Second idea)

However, while Gladys’s dreams preserve her sense of self-worth, Harrison also reveals that fantasy alone cannot deliver her family from injustice.

Analysis of Second Idea

Gladys’s transformation culminates in her address to the Rodney Shire Council, where she demands “proper schooling … The white people too — they need to be educated about us, and our ways” (p. 197). Harrison complicates the notion of escape by showing that it is voice and action, not fantasy, that begin to change the family’s circumstances, suggesting that dreams are only powerful when they inspire agency. By using a hinge sentence, you show your reader that you’re not just stacking ideas but developing an argument, deepening your interpretation, and showing the author’s nuanced message.

Concluding Dependant Phrases Paired with Analytic Verbs
Concluding Dependant PhraseAnalytic Verb
Even if Sophocles recognises...questions...
Whilst Sophocles endorses...illustrates...
Whether or not Sophocles implies...reveals...
In order to explore ... Sophocles ...demonstrates...

Conclusion

If you think of a conclusion as less of summarising everything, and more of combining all of these conflicting ideas and messages into one overall conclusive point. We can do this by using the following structure.

Structure

The structure for a conclusion can follow the same structure as an introduction. However, instead of introducing the text, you are concluding your discussion. Here is an alternative method: Topic:

‘The dream sequences in Rainbow’s End are the characters’ only escape from the depressing reality of life.’ Do you agree?

Conclusion Structure for Analytical Essay Writing
Purpose in ConclusionExample Sentence
Overview of how the whole text demonstrates the key concepts in the essay topicFundamentally, Rainbow’s End explores the tension between dreams and reality, particularly how fantasy can offer dignity, but not deliverance, to those denied opportunity by an unjust society.
Summary of the complications of this topicThroughout the play, Harrison complicates the notion of escape by showing that while Gladys’s daydreams preserve her sense of self-worth, they leave her family’s circumstances unchanged, challenging the romanticisation of hope without action.
Focus on the key authorial message in conclusive languageTherefore, Harrison critiques a society that forces its most marginalised to retreat into fantasy, asserting that genuine change lies not in dreaming of acceptance but in finding the voice to demand it, as Gladys does before the Shire Council.
The Initial Concluding Sentence

Here is a list of concluding and verb phrases you can use at the beginning of your conclusion.

Part 04 · Analytical Text Response EssaysChapter 5 of 5

Proofreading

Take a Break Before Proofreading

After finishing your essay, your mind is still fresh with ideas, but it may be too familiar with the text to catch all mistakes. Step away from the essay for at least 10–15 minutes. This will help you approach it with fresh eyes and a clearer mindset when you return to review it.

Read Aloud

Reading aloud forces you to slow down and focus on each word. It also helps you hear awkward phrasing or sentences that don’t flow well. Read your essay aloud, paying attention to how each sentence sounds. If something feels off or too long-winded, make adjustments.

Check for Structure and Coherence

A strong essay is logically structured with clear arguments and smooth transitions. Ensure each paragraph has a clear topic sentence. Verify your argument flows logically from one point to the next. Ensure your introduction sets up your essay well and your conclusion summarises without introducing new ideas.

Look for Clarity and Precision

Vague or unclear language can confuse your reader, even if the argument itself is solid. Check if any sentences can be more specific or concise. Remove unnecessary filler words like ‘really,’ ‘just,’ or ‘quite.’ Replace vague words like ‘things’ or ‘stuff’ with more precise terms.

Verify Your Thesis and Topic Sentences

Your thesis statement should be the central focus of your essay, and topic sentences should align with it. Double-check that:

  • Your thesis is clear and present in the introduction.

  • Each body paragraph clearly supports your thesis with an argument or evidence.

  • Topic sentences match the content of their respective paragraphs.

Proofread for Grammar and Syntax

Grammar mistakes, awkward phrasing, or incorrect sentence structure can weaken the professionalism of your essay. Read through your essay for common mistakes (e.g., subject-verb agreement, incorrect punctuation, run-on sentences). Use tools like Grammarly or even Microsoft Word’s spelling/grammar checker for a quick second look.

Check for Word Choice and Tone

The right word choice ensures your ideas are clear and impactful. Avoid overly complex words unless they are necessary for the context. Ensure you maintain an academic tone throughout the essay. Replace informal language (e.g., ‘kids’ or ‘stuff’) with more formal, specific terms.

Look for Repetition

Repeating the same word or idea can weaken your essay’s overall impact. Identify words or phrases that appear too often. Use synonyms or restructure sentences to avoid redundancy. Consider varying sentence structures to keep the reader engaged.

Check Punctuation and Spacing

Simple punctuation errors or inconsistent spacing can distract the reader. Verify commas, full stops, and apostrophes are used correctly. Ensure there is proper spacing after full stops and between paragraphs.

Ask Someone Else to Read It

Fresh eyes can often spot mistakes you may have missed. Ask a peer, teacher, or family member to read your essay. Be open to their feedback and use it to improve your writing.

Ensure It Meets the Task Requirements

Your essay should always answer the question and meet the criteria specified in the prompt. Revisit the question to ensure your essay directly addresses it. Check that all key parts of the task are covered and that your argument is relevant.

Part 04 · Analytical Text Response Essays

Prompt Types: Discuss, To What Extent, Do You Agree, How Does…

The instruction word in a topic tells you what kind of essay to write. Below are the four main prompt types with the approach for each, using Sunset Boulevard examples — the same skills transfer directly to Rainbow’s End.

1. The ‘Discuss’ prompt

‘Sunset Boulevard suggests that Hollywood destroys those who depend on it.’ Discuss.

‘Discuss’ asks you to explore multiple aspects of the idea, consider different perspectives, present a balanced argument, and develop a clear but nuanced contention. You do not have to strongly agree or disagree, but your response should show complexity.

Approach

  • Step 1 — Key words and main ideas. Here: the Hollywood industry, destruction/harm, dependence on fame, and the characters affected.
  • Step 2 — Form a nuanced contention. e.g. Billy Wilder’s Sunset Boulevard suggests that Hollywood can destroy those who rely on its approval, yet the film ultimately reveals that personal delusion and the industry’s exploitation together lead to tragedy.
  • Step 3 — Plan body paragraphs. 1. Hollywood abandons former stars (Norma). 2. The industry exploits desperate individuals (Joe). 3. Delusion and self-deception worsen the damage.

2. The ‘To what extent do you agree?’ prompt

‘Norma Desmond is more a victim than a villain.’ To what extent do you agree?

You must clearly state a position on a spectrum and support it, partially agree or disagree, and evaluate both sides of the argument. This type of prompt requires qualification and complexity.

Approach

  • Step 1 — Take a qualified position. e.g. While Norma Desmond is responsible for her destructive actions, Wilder portrays her primarily as a tragic victim of Hollywood’s cruelty and her own psychological isolation.
  • Step 2 — Plan paragraphs. 1. Norma as victim of Hollywood. 2. Norma as psychologically fragile and manipulated by illusion. 3. Norma as morally responsible for Joe’s death.

3. The ‘Do you agree?’ prompt

‘Joe Gillis is responsible for his own downfall.’ Do you agree?

You must take a clear position (yes / mostly / no) and present a clear argument. Unlike ‘discuss’, this usually requires stronger judgement.

Approach

  • Step 1 — Decide your position. e.g. While Joe’s moral compromises contribute to his downfall, Wilder suggests that Hollywood’s exploitative culture and Norma’s instability play equally significant roles.
  • Step 2 — Plan paragraphs. 1. Joe’s moral choices. 2. Hollywood’s exploitation. 3. Norma’s control and obsession.

4. The ‘How does…’ prompt

How does Billy Wilder depict the destructive nature of fame in Sunset Boulevard?

Some VCE English questions begin ‘How does Wilder…’ — these require you to focus, even more than other prompt types, on the methods and techniques the author uses to convey the message to the audience. You must explain what the director is showing, how the director shows it, and why the director presents it that way.

Key elements in this prompt: How → explain techniques · depict → representation · destructive nature of fame → theme.

Approach

  • Step 1 — Identify the key idea. Fame is destructive: it creates delusion, causes isolation, leads to moral compromise.
  • Step 2 — Identify the techniques. Characterisation (Norma), setting (the mansion), cinematography, symbolism, dialogue, narration (Joe).
  • Step 3 — Form a contention. e.g. In Sunset Boulevard, Billy Wilder depicts the destructive nature of fame through Norma Desmond’s tragic delusion, Joe Gillis’s moral compromise and the decaying world of Hollywood that traps its characters in illusion.
  • Step 4 — Plan paragraphs. 1. Norma represents the destructive effects of fame. 2. Joe shows how ambition leads to compromise. 3. Hollywood itself as a corrupt ‘dream factory’.

Body paragraph structure for ‘How does…’

Each paragraph should follow: Argument → Evidence → Technique → Effect. For example: Wilder portrays Norma Desmond as a tragic symbol of fame’s destructive power → this is evident through the decaying mansion → the setting symbolises… → through this imagery, Wilder suggests…

Quick strategy: answer three questions for any ‘How does…’ prompt — What idea is the author exploring? What technique do they use? What effect does this create for the audience?
e.g. Norma’s mansion → symbolism → shows the decay of fame → effect on the audience. That is analysis.

See also the 2025 assessors’ guidance on topic types and verbs and the Th.E.S.A.M.L. paragraph method.

Audience

The audience refers to anyone who is viewing, reading or listening to a text. 1. Begin to identify your audience by answering the following questions:

2. What are you writing about?

3. Who would be interested in this topic?

4. Is there more than one audience who might be interested in this?

5. What other texts exist that are like my idea? Who are their audience?

Part 05 · Creating TextsChapter 2 of 7

Purpose

Writing to Express

Use your imagination and creativity to explore and convey ideas

Examples include novels, short stories, plays, poems and films.

Usually involves imagined characters, places, situations and events.

Usually contain tension and conflict as characters seek to achieve their coals.

Writing to Explain

Use your imagination and creativity to explore and convey ideas

Examples include novels, short stories, plays, poems and films.

Usually involves imagined characters, places, situations and events.

Usually contain tension and conflict as characters seek to achieve their coals.

Use your imagination and creativity to explore and convey ideas

Examples include novels, short stories, plays, poems and films.

Usually contain tension and conflict as characters seek to achieve their coals.

Part 05 · Creating TextsChapter 3 of 7

Personal Journeys

From the Study Design Explorations of ‘life’ or biographical explorations - telling our stories, telling others’ stories, the problem of telling stories, appropriation of stories, who tells the stories and our history, missing stories, marginalised and elevated stories. Students could explore personal milestones, the effects of key events on their lives, or explore these ideas through the eyes of others. Students who have migrated can explore their stories of movement and disruption. They can explore the expectations of change and the language of a new place and culture.

Writing

  • Writing about personal journeys explores both the physical world and the internal world of oneself and others.

  • Reading and crafting texts about journeys provide opportunities to consider new places, perspectives, and self-discovery.

  • Personal journeys involve various experiences, including challenges, growth, and self-discovery.

  • Writing about personal journeys allows individuals to share unique experiences with others.

REMINDER: You have access to the exam generator and the prompt generator to practice your Section B skills.

Ideas

A personal journey can involve:

  • Travel to a physical place.

  • Psychological or emotional growth and change.

  • A combination of both, where physical travel leads to new insights.

  • Interior journeys may take a long time to occur due to significant events.

  • The most important aspect of a personal journey is change – the individual or group is fundamentally altered by it.

Part 05 · Creating TextsChapter 5 of 7

Sample Creating Texts Piece (19/20)

A high-scoring (19/20) Section B piece written to reflect and express, responding to the title below.

Title: ‘The Path Less Taken’

I was at a family dinner over Easter earlier in the year, and one of my aunts asked me a seemingly-innocuous questionStructure & evidenceOpens in scene, mid-tension — the reflective frame is established through story, not announcement. that made me quite uncomfortable. Usually, I am an upbeat, bubbly person, and most would tell you that I have high confidence. Unfortunately, the opposite is trueVoice & craftThe confession establishes the piece's honesty contract with the reader in sentence two.: every day I question the choices I have made and the impact they have had on my relationships.

When I entered high school, I was faced with a rather uncomfortable choice: I would have to pick a school sport to play during winter, or join a band. The extracurricular lady peered at me over her bifocalsVoice & craftA minor character sketched in one line — economy of characterisation that keeps a short piece moving., frowning, eyebrows drawn together and raised like little anthillsLanguage & techniqueA comic image that returns later — recurring motifs give a reflective piece the cohesion the criteria ask for.. The only band that had any space was the orchestra, and they needed a piccolo playerStructure & evidenceChance narrows to one instrument — the piece engineers its own inevitability..

Now, anyone who is acquainted with woodwind instruments would know that the piccolo is a tiny, shrieking version of the flute, and that it should be played only by an experienced flautist with excellent tone. But for a reason unknown both to my parents and myself, I, standing in my oversized blazer with my dress almost at my anklesVoice & craftOne costume detail places the narrator's age and awkwardness without a word of explanation., boldly offered my services to the school band. The lady in charge of extracurriculars frowned a little harder, and I could almost imagine the tiny ants running in and out of those hills. Then she sat back, sighed, and put my name down, one eyebrow still raised.

Obviously, I was terrible. However, I started practising more and more. I took a certain tormented delightVoice & craftSelf-aware, wry self-description — the reflective voice earns trust by being honest about its own oddness. in knowing that while other students were tucking into dinner, or doing a little homework, I was running up and down the same five notes with deliberate focusLanguage & techniqueConcrete practice detail — the writing shows obsession rather than declaring it.. As painful as my practice was for all within earshotVoice & craftSelf-deprecation keeps the obsession likeable — the narrator hears themselves as others do., the improvement it allowed me in the orchestra gave me surging motivation.

Sometimes in life, you are captured by an idea which is inexplicable to those who haven’t felt it. The sense of mastery I felt playing the piccolo made all other subjects at school pale in comparison. What was maths, with all its useless formulae repeated over and overVoice & craftA rhetorical question in the narrator's teenage register — the voice stays consistent with the remembered self., when music was a space with infinite room for improvement? Where every day’s practice contributed to notable growth, however small? Music was not just a hobby for me, but a pursuit which gave me the deepest sense of meaning. Over time, I came to love the piccolo more than anything else.

Unfortunately, as the years went by, my parents, who had initially been in favour of my playing an instrument, slowly started to prioritise other things. I should have been studying more maths, more science, writing more essays. I knew that they expected me to study like my friends, and to aim for medicine so I could become a doctor. As much as I tried, my enthusiasm for those subjects never matched what I felt for music. I practised more, and my grades fell further. I tried to hide reports from my parents (with little success). Finally, when I entered year 12, I committed a cardinal sin: I listed a music degree as my first university preference.

The decision had a negative impact on my parents. Our relationship turned to more fighting, and then stony, awkward dinnersLanguage & techniqueConflict rendered through mealtimes — the family's language of silence.. Parents often have high expectations for their children. Life is short. You only buy books for a new highschool year six timesIdeas & interpretationThe piece steps into the parents' perspective and counts what they lose — empathy that complicates the narrator's own case. with your child, and they only get the ages of 18-22 to do undergrad with people their own age. For parents, not only does time with children fly by, but in many cases the having of children cut their own professional lives short. I remember my mother constantly reminding me that perhaps she would have been so much further ahead in her workplace if not for the family, as much as she loved usIdeas & interpretationThe mother's sacrifice is honoured even while its pressure is described — generosity toward the antagonist deepens the reflection.. I think for her in particular, the lives of my siblings and myself were vicarious extensions of her own, through which she could live out different "maybe" paths.

You can understand, then, why I would have been so perturbed by my aunt’s question: "And what will you be doing at the end of the year?"

For my part, I could never see why my parents were so upset. My three older siblings all left secondary education and diligently commenced medical school. I, on the other hand, could only see one thing: I had one life to live, and I’d rather do anything than spend it dissecting bodies and examining skin conditions. Once you realise that life is short, you come to terms with the fact that you may as well spend it doing things you enjoy. How sad it would be to die doing something you hated out of a feeling of social obligation.

And yet, this is what so many of us do. Many of us end up trapped in degrees, then internships, then grad roles, then middle-management jobsLanguage & techniqueCumulative listing enacts the trap it describes — form mirroring meaning. chosen for us by our high-schooler selves, with extremely little experience to guide the decisionIdeas & interpretationThe argument widens from one family to a critique of how everyone chooses a life — the personal essay earns its general claim.. Most people enter this life because it is the socially acceptable thing to do, and also because it is extremely difficult to find direction. Artists, inventors, and scientists have to bet against conventional wisdom to pursue their innate dreams. However, conventional wisdom is usually rightIdeas & interpretationThe reflection genuinely turns against itself — complexity of thought, not a straight line to a moral. (which is why we call it wisdom), and so if you don’t have a good reason to ignore it, you probably shouldn’t. Perhaps, given how much I know my parents care about me, I do understand their worry after all.

So when my aunt asked me this question, the mood in the room dropped somewhat. I thought carefully about my response. Should I lie, tell her that I’ll be doing medicine too?Structure & evidenceThe climax staged as live inner debate — options weighed in real time before the answer. Should I say I was prevented by my poor grades? It was almost trueVoice & craftA three-word sentence that admits the temptation to lie — honesty about dishonesty, the reflective mode at its best.. But then I felt the weight of the truth settle upon me, and it was heavy and liberating all at onceLanguage & techniqueA compact paradox at the emotional climax — feeling rendered precisely, without melodrama.. I was different. Who cared? This was my life. "So, then? Will you be following your good older siblings?"

"You know, aunty," I twirled some spaghetti around my forkStructure & evidenceEnds back inside the opening scene with the title spoken in dialogue — frame closed, title integrated, purpose achieved.. "I’ve decided to walk the path less taken."
