Part 05 · Creating TextsChapter 6 of 7

Sample Creating Texts Piece: ‘Finding My Way’ (10/10)

A full-mark Personal Journeys piece responding to the title ‘Finding My Way’.

Stimuli used: a pair of feet on asphalt with painted arrows pointing in many directions; ‘The caterpillar transforms without thought. Its journey to a beautiful butterfly, inevitable. Why must my own metamorphosis be shaped by so many choices?’

I remember the day that I got off the plane so vividly. I remember the sun that day, a cruel god beating down upon me from its court of cloudless blueVoice & craftThe first image establishes voice and displacement at once — sensory writing doing thematic work.. It scorched my skin and left no prisoners. It was nothing like the benevolent sun over my homeland of Calcutta, where the gentle rays of morning would shine over the backs of a hundred thousand people, wrapped up in their idiosyncratic lifestyles since twilightVoice & craftThe remembered homeland is populated, not idealised — nostalgia given texture and inhabitants..

But there was no one here. There was just me, my father, and the red earthLanguage & techniqueThe new country reduced to three nouns — emptiness enacted by syntax. that stretched out before me in every direction, desolate and uninviting. My father, a self-titled opportunist, yearning to abandon the stagnancyVoice & craftThe father characterised through his own self-description — affectionate irony. that is the defining characteristic of the Indian middle class, saw Laxmi, the Hindu goddess of prosperity, hidden in every crackIdeas & interpretationA cultural frame carried into the new landscape — the father's optimism rendered through belief rather than adjectives. of the drought-stricken land.

I always thought my father was a little bit of an oddball. His brain was rattled around by the many, many wars it had been exposed to. In the same spirit, his nervous yet paradoxically poised energy set our house alive. It would wake up with the soft, repetitive chantsLanguage & techniqueThe house is personified as waking and sleeping — the family home made a living character in two sentences. of his Sanskrit prayers in the morning and fall asleep to his many lectures about the ideal governmental system come nightfall. It smelled like incense, asafoetida, and cumin, instead of the burnt smoke of the great Australian outback outside our porch door. It was the only piece of home in a strange new country. I cherished it with all my heart.

He was quick to find his way. In three years’ time, he was listening to The Beatles whilst fortifying himself with double helpings of Milo and Bengali egg fry, perusing an assortment of Hindi newspapers he got internationally delivered to us. The blokes at his sheep shearing job liked to alternate between calling him African and Indigenous Australian, slurs for both inclusive. He paid no heed, jovially punctuating his latest talesVoice & craftThe father's resilience is shown through how he tells stories, not stated — character through manner. of workplace discrimination with, "Their stones and sticks can only rattle my bones! In Calcutta, we used gulel when we really wanted to hurt someone." I didn’t have the heart to quip about his idiomatic misquoting. His spirit was indomitable. Nothing could faze him.

I, on the other hand, could not extricate myself from the diminishing gazes of my peers. Every remark on the failure of my foreign tongue to mold itself to the valleys and troughs of a new language, every question underpinned by perfunctory distaste, every snigger swirled in my head each night, consuming the liminal space between waking and sleepingLanguage & techniqueThe racism's psychological cost is located precisely — in the space before sleep — rather than described generally.. The movies I watched, the songs I liked, the food I ate, all underwent a strict metamorphosis like the caterpillar confined within the fecund dark of a chrysalisVoice & craftThe stimulus is woven into the piece's own imagery rather than quoted at — the meaningful connection the criteria require.. Slowly, one choice at a time, aligning more and moreLanguage & techniqueAnaphora enacts the meaning — the repeated structure performs the gradual assimilation it describes. with the Australian lifestyle. Slowly, one choice at a time, departing from my roots.

That was, until the diagnosis. "Hepatitis B," the doctor called it, the only bloke in town willing to look at a ‘coloured’ patient. "From infected needles, or maybe passed down in the womb from his mother." My father, try as he might, could not pronounce the name of the illness eating his liver alive. He jokingly called it a gift from our motherlandVoice & craftCharacter revealed through one line of reported speech — humour carrying grief, voice doing double work.. Over the next few months, I watched him disintegrate into a shell of himself. He could barely open his eyes most days, and when he could, he would talk. He would rasp his smooth tenor into a small tape recorderLanguage & techniqueThe dying voice given texture — 'rasp' against 'smooth tenor' measures the decline. into the early hours of the morning, flitting from English to Hindi to BengaliStructure & evidenceThe tape recorder plants the piece's resolution — storytelling as inheritance — long before the ending states it. as the disease continued its unending assault into his being.

When they complained to me, he laid his papery, wizened hand, which once, in a bygone era, would emanate vitality so potent it was palpable in his air, upon my shoulder and looked at me in the eye with a desperate so intense I felt it in my very soul. "You must find your way, beta. You must tell my story before my time is up."

When he passed, I lost the only person rooting for me, both on this side of the Indian Ocean and the one where I came from. I was lost without himVoice & craftAfter paragraphs of rich imagery, grief arrives in five plain words — the register drop is the emotional peak.. I felt stagnant, stuck. Our house suddenly felt as desolate as the golden land on my very first day. Over time, I have found my way. I honour his memory by telling his extraordinary storyStructure & evidenceThe father's dying instruction answered verb for verb — the piece keeps its promise. to anyone that will listen. I continue his tradition by hanging his portrait above my mantel and pestering my eldest daughter to hang a garland of fresh marigolds underneath it every morningStructure & evidenceResolution through inherited ritual — the journey lands somewhere earned, and the title is answered by action rather than statement., just as my father did with his father’s portrait, and how his father would have done with his father before him.

Because of him, I have spent a lifetime working as a social worker, aiding those fragmented because of migrationIdeas & interpretationThe personal journey widens into vocation — the framework idea resolved as a life, not a lesson., granting me the insurmountable pleasure of listening to a thousand storiesIdeas & interpretationThe journey resolves into listening — storytelling inverted from telling to receiving.. Many of them are like my father and I’s, each marked with their own anecdotes and idiosyncrasies. People, struggling, trying, grappling with making sense of a new world. I see the glint of my father’s smile in the face of a stranger coming to Australia to make a better life for themselves and their families. I hear his voice in their adversities. It feels like homeStructure & evidenceThe final line resolves the piece's two longings — father and home — in one closing image..

What made this a 10/10

Every scene threads the stimuli through fresh sensory imagery, recurring motifs, and a quiet, honest voice. That layering is what lifts a Section B piece from competent to top band.

Hover over a highlighted passage (or tap, on touch screens) to see what earns marks in that moment.
Part 05 · Creating TextsChapter 7 of 7

Sample Creating Texts Piece: ‘This is Me’ (10/10)

A full-mark Personal Journeys piece responding to the title ‘This is Me’.

Stimuli used: ‘Life isn’t about finding yourself. Life is about creating yourself.’; ‘My story is ready. There’s enough to share. As I have made plenty. Come, sit at my table. Listen and learn. And you will see me.’

"Good evening customers. Your next service to depart from Platform 2 will be the four-fifty-six Flinders Street running express from Newport to Footscray, Footscray to North Melbourne, and then stopping all stations to Flinders Street. Always remember to touch on your Myki at the start of your journey." The train announcer’s voice is a worn chime, filling the stark station like a frayed tapestry in an abandoned household. The station smells like it always does, redolent with the acrid tang of piss, sweat, and smoke. There is something self-righteous about the smell of a train station; none are exempt from its slightly carcinogenic, slightly metallic embraceVoice & craftUnromantic sensory specificity builds an honest, distinctive voice from the first paragraph..

A low mechanical groan swells beneath his feet as the train approaches, its headlights cutting through the dimness of the already-darkening winter skyLanguage & techniqueThe train given agency and effort — the mechanical world mirrors the narrator's. with a tired reluctance. The doors slide open with a hydraulic sigh. He steps inside. The 4:56 pm train from Newport Station on WednesdayStructure & evidenceHyper-specific time-stamping — the ordinary documented like an event, which is the piece's point., the 20th of August, the 2025th year of the Common Era is almost deserted.

He looks down at his hands, admiring the soft webbing connecting the valleys between his fingers. His calluses are peeling, revealing the angry red of new skin underneath. It distantly reminds him of the animal documentaries he used to watch as a kid. He recalls his grandfather teaching him about the rattlesnakes as they appeared on their family’s old LCD; how their cyclical shedding of skin added a new segment to their rattles. Each interlocked and keratinised bit of memory ultimately conjoined to create their characteristic rattles, allowing them to alert nearby friends or warn foe when shook. He feels a bit like that rattlesnake now, shedding another layerLanguage & techniqueThe rattlesnake becomes the piece's controlling metaphor — introduced early, transformed by the ending. of him, who he was (is?), as his sole ward against something bigger than him.

He straightens, instinctively brushing his hands against the worn fabric of his jeans, as though he can rid himself of that thin, flaking skin, of the evidence that he is still stuck in the process of becoming something else.

The carriage is quieter than he expects. Subdued, like a room where the conversation has only just endedLanguage & techniqueA simile chosen for atmosphere rather than display — the comparison is quiet, exact and original. and the last spoken consonant lingers. A man in a hi-vis vest dozes with his head tilted back, mouth slightly ajar and a tenuous string of droolVoice & craftUnglamorous observation keeps the narrator credible — the eye is honest before it is lyrical. tracing its way down the contours of his jaw. Two teenagers sit across from one another, sharing a set of earbuds, their heads bowed together in conspiratorial symmetryLanguage & techniquePrecise observational phrase-making — strangers rendered with affection, which quietly foreshadows the ending's connection.. The girl in green does not look upStructure & evidenceA stranger noticed and released — the city's indifference logged without complaint.. He takes a seat by the window.

As the train lurches forward, the station recedes into abstraction. The steel beams dissolve into streaks of grey, then into nothing at all. He watches his reflection instead. It hovers faintly against the glass, superimposed upon the passing city: a face that feels both intimately his and faintly unfamiliarIdeas & interpretationThe reflection motif states the identity problem in a single image — shown in glass, never explained.. The train gathers speed.

But at the city, the carriage doors part and the world rushes in. The platform is dense with motion. He takes note of the sudden layering of language, the multiplicity of accents folding over another like tenuous silk organzaVoice & craftThe city is perceived through the narrator's heritage — imagery rooted in identity, which is the piece's whole subject. of his mother’s sarees. Someone coughs sharply; someone else argues into a phone; a group of schoolboys with reckless affection. The air smells of coffee, of baked bread, and of the faint tang of iron from the rails.

He lingers. He feels it again, that distanceStructure & evidenceShort sentences slow the piece at its thematic centre — pacing controlled to make the reader sit in the feeling.. Not exclusion, really, but a kind of peripheral existence. As though he is still watching the city through the glass when in reality, he’s out in the cold, the gale lashing unforgivingly like a whip against his face. He looks down at his hands, at the peeling skin, scrutinising the rawness beneath. Shedding, again. Becoming, again.

He steps off the train and follows the crowd instinctively, letting the current of its movement take him toward Queen Victoria Market. It is not the quiet barter of daytime commerce that he expected. Tonight, the market radiates luminous against the winter chill, illuminated by strings of fairy lights and the glow of fire pits. The scents are the first to hit him: the rich spices from the steaming laksa, sweet smoke from grilled takoyaki, and the inviting swirl of mulled wine and spiced Milo in ceramic mugs held tight between gloved hands.

Luxuriating in the sensory overload, he moves deeper into the market. He spots Hungarian chimney cakes next to Malaysian satay, Latin American empanadas beside Turkish gozleme; the diversity of flavour becoming a rare map of the worldLanguage & techniqueA sensory catalogue lifted into metaphor — the list converted into meaning. in itself, condensed into the congested borders of one crowded laneway. At a food stall selling steaming xiaolongbao, an older man smiles at him and nods, the gesture small but genuine. The delicate crows feet of his smile remind him of his grandfather, the passionate twinkle in his eye as he taught the young boy before him, so many moons ago.

Suddenly trapped in the fossilised resin of memory, he remembers his grandfather’s parting words to him, "Life isn’t just about finding yourself, beta. You must remember to listen to the stories around you." He nods back to the older man, a little bit awkwardlyVoice & craftThe breakthrough kept awkward — growth without sudden fluency keeps it believable., but finally laden with the understanding that had eluded him priorStructure & evidenceThe grandfather's words return at the exact moment they can be understood — the piece's two timelines click together here..

He realises, watching the ebb and flow of the throng, locals and newcomers, laughter and music, old and young, all engaged in the shared act of eating, moving, and breathing as one under the winter light, that belonging is not a singular destinationIdeas & interpretationThe stimulus idea is resolved as an insight the narrative has earned, not stated up front — ideas developed through story.. It is a mosaic of interactions, layered and overlapping, like the rattles on the snake’s tail. Distinct segments forming something whole, something enduringStructure & evidenceThe controlling metaphor is resolved into the theme — craft and idea arriving together at the climax..

He leaves the market with nothing in his hands, but a warmth settled somewhere in his throat. A loosening, however slight, of the tight, invisible viceLanguage & techniqueChange measured in fractions — 'however slight' guards against overstatement. of unfamiliarity.

The next evening, he boards the 4:56 again. The carriage is fuller now, or perhaps he is simply noticing more. A conversation drifts across the aisle, punctuated by laughter. As the train comes to a grinding halt, someone shifts to make space without being askedStructure & evidenceThe climactic change is a tiny social gesture — restraint where a lesser piece would sermonise.. He catches his reflection in the window. It is still there, sullen and superimposed against the speeding city. However, the edges have softened. The distance, though not demolished, has narrowed into something traversable. This is me, he thinks.

"Now arriving at Flinders Street Station..." The train announcer’s voice is a worn chime and the station smells like it always does, like piss and sweat and smoke, but this time, he does not flinchStructure & evidenceThe circular structure repeats the opening with one change — quiet structural control that top pieces show.. He listens instead, as the city moves with him, no longer a current he moves againstIdeas & interpretationThe city-as-current metaphor resolves the belonging question in the piece's own terms — the answer arrives as an image., but something he is, slowly, learning how to inhabit.

What made this a 10/10

Every scene threads the stimuli through fresh sensory imagery, recurring motifs, and a quiet, honest voice. That layering is what lifts a Section B piece from competent to top band.

Part 06

Analysing Argument

Part 06 · Analysing ArgumentChapter 1 of 6

Essay Structure

Introduction

What you are trying to achieve

The purpose of an introduction is to ‘introduce’ both the irrefutable facts about the text you will be analysing, and your interpretation of the text. The best way to do this is to connect the text’s plain details with your early analysis of what the contention, tone and intended audience.

Include

  • Context and contention

  • Details (writer, publication, date and text type)

  • Major persuasive devices

  • Tone (consistant or shifting)

  • Intended audience and how you know this

Revise

A common mistake students make is that they turn their introductions into lists of everything they need to include. Remember to express yourself clearly, professionally and with style. This is the introduction to your analysiss and sets your assesor’s expectations for the rest of the piece.

Sentence structure

The first part of your introduction should focus on what issue is being discussed and why it is important. This will help you to create an opening to demonstrate a strong understanding of the article, for example:

The current trend of dangerous Tik-Tok challenges has once again sparked debate over whether we should be restricting access to the wildly popular app.

If we break it down it becomes:

  • The current trend - Phrase of recency

  • of dangerous Tik-tok challenges - Incident

  • has once again sparked - Verb phrase

  • debate - Issue

Here are some sample sentence builders:

Framing Contemporary Issues in Writing: Phrases of Recency, Cause, and Contention
Phrases of RecencyVerb PhrasesIssues
The latest......has once again raised...issue
The present......has sparked...debate
The recent......has led to...argument
The earlier......has motivated...discussions
The current......has caused...disputes
Once again......has generated...altercation
Recently......has engendered...discourse
Lately......has provoked...consultation
Over the past year......has propagated...dialogue
Key Information Phrase

In this section we should include key information that is usually found in the background information section of the text, including:

  • Author’s name

  • Connection of author to text

  • The form of the piece, the date and where it was published

  • How this links back to the issue you identified in your first sentence

For example, this sentence can look like:

Dr Marko Shellius, CEO of Psychiatric Safe Australia, has written a submission to the parliamentary inquiry into social media, in particular addressing Tik-Tok’s burgeoning influence over Australian teens.

If we break it down, it looks like this:

Authorial Context and Publication Framing for Argument Analysis
AuthorConnection of Author to the IssueForm and PublisherLink Back to the Issue
Dr Marko ShelliusCEO of Psychiatric Safe AustraliaHas written a submission to the parliamentary inquiry on social mediaIn particular addressing TikTok’s burgeoning influence over Australian teens

This order can also be moved around as well:

Form, Author, and Contextual Framing of the Issue
Form and PublisherAuthorConnection of Author to the IssueLink Back to the Issue
In a written submission to the parliamentary inquiry into social media,Dr Marko ShelliusCEO of Psychiatric Safe AustraliaAddresses TikTok’s burgeoning influence over Australian teens

Here are some sentence starters to help build these sentences:

an important…created a…responding to…
one of the…confessing…
Tones

Analysing argument involves understanding how authors use language to persuade their audience. This includes identifying the author’s tone, which refers to the overall feeling or attitude conveyed in the text. Recognising shifts in tone is crucial as it often accompanies changes in argument strategies. Authors use tone to appeal to the reader’s emotions, establish credibility, and guide the reader’s interpretation of the argument. This is the perfect place for you to show the breadth of your vocabulary. Choose a specific word that relates to the article. Always avoid using the terms ‘Positive’, ‘Negative’ and ‘Neutral’.”

Audience

The audience refers to anyone who is viewing, reading or listening to a text. When writing an analysing argument essay, it isn’t enough to just say your audience is ‘everyone’ or ‘all Australians’. You need to refine your idea of who you are writing for! Three questions to ask yourself

  • What is the author writing about?

  • Who would be interested in this topic?

  • Is there more than one audience who might be interested in this?

The key take-away is that you need to be as specific as possible when stating the intended audience.

Body Paragraphs

What you are trying to achieve

Each body paragraph should explore, in sequence, the major arguments that the author uses to support their contention. Remember you are being assessed on your ability to analyse the arguments the author uses and how the language they have chosen supports these.

Step 1: What?

Outline what the author is arguing in your own words. Make a comment on structure; if this is the first or final argument then what is the reason the author introduced this at this time.

Step 2: How?

Begin by identifying the persuasive language features that the author uses to substantiate their argument, include a quote illustrating your point (but no more than 5 words). But this is not enough, then you need to analyse the effectiveness of this approach and discuss how it positions the audience. If there is an image associated with this argument, you should talk about it here - treat it like a persuasive device. Depending on the argument, you may want to talk about how a group of persuasive devices work together. Note: Try to avoid repetition by constantly stating ‘The author uses X which makes the audience feel Y. This can come across tacky and quite robotic. To see examples of how this can be done in a successful way, look at the three high-scoring essays below.

Step 3: Why?

Finally, you need to infer why the author chose this approach, what is their persuasive intent? How would it appeal to their particular audience? How does it impact the tone of the article? How does this support their contention?

Step 4: Examine (Optional)

Does the tone change for this argument? Does the structure change? How does this position the audience?

Conclusion

What you are trying to achieve

In your conclusion, you are exploring both how the text concludes and wrapping up your analysis at the same time. It is an opportunity to discuss the text as a whole, and emphasises your analytical skills.

Include

  • Comment on the structure of the article, including whether there is a consistent or changing tone of writing.

  • Focus on the last paragraph of the article, evaluating how it supports the writer’s contention.

  • Evaluate whether the article developed more trust in the readers and explain why.

Note

Some students find they don’t have time for a conclusion. This isn’t ideal, but it isn’t the end of the world, as most of this information should have been explicit throughout. If you are running short on time, a sentence summarising how each argument leads to the contention is sufficient.

Complex Sentence and Paragraph Building

When analysing persuasive texts, it’s not enough to simply spot techniques—you need to explain what is happening, why it’s there, and how it affects the audience. Students often start with short, seperate sentences: ‘The writer uses emotive language. This makes the audience feel sympathy’ A stronger version combines ideas into one complex sentence: ‘Describing the victims as “cruelly abandoned” evokes a deep sense of sympathy in the audience, prompting them to see the government as neglectful and morally failing in its duty of care.’

Techniques for Stronger Sentences

  • Subordinating conjunctions – because, although, while, since, if

  • Embedded quotes – weave quotes into the sentence naturally

  • Relative clauses – who, which, that

  • Appositives – a technique aimed at…

By applying these sentence-building tools, your analysis will become more precise, connected, and persuasive—mirroring the sophistication expected in high-level responses. To help you identify and discuss these elements in a clear, structured way, we will now look at a step-by-step framework that breaks persuasive writing into its key components.

W.A.S.T.E.: F.A.T.!

This acronym is designed to assist you in crafting complex and sophisticated sentences that strengthen your analytical writing. In higher-scoring essays, writers deliberately weave these elements into their work to add depth, clarity, and precision. While it is not necessary to include every element in every individual sentence, you should aim to integrate all of them across the body of your essay. This means that, by the end of each body paragraph, the reader should be able to identify how you have employed these elements to develop your argument, enhance your expression, and engage the audience effectively. Consistent and purposeful use of these techniques will not only improve sentence complexity but also demonstrate a mature and well-structured writing style.

  • Words – Focus on the most persuasive words or phrases of the piece.

  • Argument – What is the supporting argument of each section?

  • Structure – Where is this located in the piece and why?

  • Tone – How does the author apply tone to be more persuasive in each section?

  • Effect upon the audience – How does it position the audience to:

    • Feel?

    • Act?

    • Think?

W.A.S.T.E.: F.A.T. Medium Example

The editorial uses emotionally charged words such as “climate catastrophe”, “tipping point”, and “irreversible damage” to stress the seriousness of the issue. The supporting argument is that urgent government action is necessary to prevent catastrophic environmental collapse. These phrases appear in the introduction so that the reader is immediately confronted with the scale of the problem. The author adopts an alarmist and urgent tone to heighten concern and prompt immediate attention. This positions the audience to feel fear and anxiety about the future, to act by pressuring politicians or signing petitions, and to think that if society does not act now, it will be too late.

W.A.S.T.E.: F.A.T. High Example

The editorial uses emotive phrases such as “climate catastrophe”, “tipping point”, and “irreversible damage” in its introduction to immediately confront the reader with the scale of the problem. By presenting the argument that urgent government action is needed to prevent environmental collapse, the writer establishes the issue as both pressing and unavoidable. Through an alarmist and urgent tone, the piece positions the audience to feel fear, act by pressuring politicians or signing petitions, and think that immediate intervention is essential.

Fill-in-the-blanks Analysing Argument Template for an Introduction

Your introduction should discuss the writer’s contention and intention, the specific audience, the form, and the publication details, as well as the context of the issue. You might like to make a comment about the tone of the piece in the introduction. Here is a sample introduction structure: The issue of ….. (issue) was addressed in a ….. (text type) for ….. (publication) on ….. (date). ….. (Author) contends, in a ….. (tonal word) and at times ….. (tonal word) voice that ….. (contention). The piece is predominantly ….. (words about overall structure or style) in style, and seeks to position its ….. (audience) to accept ….. (intention). Another sample introduction structure could look like this: Following ….. (context), debate resurfaced regarding ….. (issue). In a ….. (text type) for ….. (publication) on ….. (date), ….. (author) argues in a ….. (tonal word) and ….. (tonal word) fashion that ….. (contention), and seeks to ….. (intention). The piece, targeted at ….. (audience), ….. . For example: In the wake of the recent sentencing of murderer John Smith, Sarah Farah’s letter to the editor of The Daily Telegraph (9 June 2014), raises the issue of perceived injustice in the treatment of Australian citizens. In an aggressive tone, she contends that the penalty imposed on this man was too harsh and that such punishment represents a form of anti Australian discrimination. Thus, she hopes to prompt the Australian Government to take action to protect its citizens.

Part 06 · Analysing ArgumentChapter 2 of 6

Analysing Written Language

Terms and Strategies

Specific Word Choice (word connotation)

Writers choose particular words to provoke an emotional reaction in the reader or emphasise an idea. For example, if writing about abortion, the author may choose; "I read with disgust that the government is to ... kill, slay, slaughter, murder, butcher, exterminate, destroy, sacrifice, execute ... Emotive language is language that is not logical or rational - it reflects and expresses an emotional or subjective reaction. Such words help create mood. Many words have connotations or associations that the writer uses with deliberate intent to create a desired effect in the reader. Words may have positive or negative connotations. They may evoke intense imagery. You will be expected to discuss some targeted words and their relative connotations in your language analysis piece.

Particular language structure and features

Inclusive language (we, our) may be used to give the audience a sense of being part of a wider community of thought/action. Modifiers and qualifiers are words used to denote a level of impact. There is a difference between a writer choosing to use ‘some’ instead of ‘all’ or ‘most’ instead of ‘many’. Phrases such as ‘very good’ or ‘basically good’ also suggest different meaning to the reader. Students analysing language need to consider why specific lexical strings have been chosen by the writer as a form of persuasion.

Euphemisms

Euphemisms are words sometimes used to soften a more accurate but seemingly more offensive word or phrase. For example, euphemisms for ‘die’ include; passed away, lose one’s life, kick the bucket, croak. A company may say they are ‘downsizing’ instead offiring workers from the job.

Hypothetical Scenarios

Hypothetical scenarios are used to prompt the reader to imagine possible outcomes, often beginning with phrases like “Imagine if...”. These speculative examples draw attention to the consequences of actions or inactions, even if they are not real or likely. Writers use this technique to provoke reflection and emotional investment from the reader. For example: “Imagine if the world was ruled by tyrants who dominated our every thought daily...”

Repetition

Repetition involves deliberately repeating words or phrases for emphasis and to make an idea more memorable. Anaphora: repetition at the start of successive clauses. e.g. School is distracting. School is boring. School sucks. Epistrophe: repetition at the end of clauses. e.g. Follow the rules. Obey the rules. These are the rules.

Puns

Puns are humorous wordplays based on similar sounds or multiple meanings. They are often used to lighten tone or ridicule opposing views. Some puns also carry double meanings, especially in persuasive or satirical writing. e.g. “Time flies like an arrow; fruit flies like a banana.”

Irony

Irony occurs when the intended meaning of a statement contrasts with its literal meaning. Writers often use irony to highlight absurdities or criticise ideas indirectly. e.g. “Lovely day,” said during a storm.

Sarcasm

Sarcasm is a biting, mocking form of expression usually intended to hurt, ridicule, or convey contempt. It often overlaps with irony but has a more cutting tone. e.g. “Nice face. Shame about the rest.”

Exaggeration / Hyperbole

This technique involves overstating a point for dramatic effect. It can be used to emphasise importance or provoke an emotional response. e.g. “I’d rather cut off my arm than go back to work under those conditions!”

Humour

Humour engages the reader by entertaining them. It can also make controversial ideas more palatable or draw attention to flaws in opposing arguments. It is often used as a non-threatening way to critique.

Satire

Satire combines humour and criticism to ridicule individuals, institutions, or ideas. It often exposes hypocrisy or absurdity, making serious points in a humorous way.

e.g. A TV show like Frontline that uses mock-journalism to critique the media.

Oxymoron

An oxymoron combines two contradictory terms to highlight contrast or provoke thought. It is often used to emphasise complexity or irony. e.g. deafening silence, wise fool, bitter sweet

Assonance

Assonance is the repetition of vowel sounds within words or phrases to create rhythm, mood, or emphasis. Often used poetically or in slogans. e.g. “cool, calm and collected”

Simile

A simile compares one thing with another using the words ‘like’ or ‘as’. It helps readers understand unfamiliar concepts by likening them to familiar ones, often to create vivid imagery. e.g. My bedroom is like a pigsty. She is as skinny as a rake.

Metaphor

A metaphor is an implied comparison that states one thing is another, thereby transferring qualities from one to the other. Metaphors can make writing more persuasive by enhancing imagery and emotional appeal. e.g. I stepped into my pigsty, shut the door and climbed into bed. He is a pillar of strength.

Rhetorical Questions

These are questions posed for effect rather than to elicit an answer. They imply that the answer is self-evident, guiding the audience to agree with the writer’s point. e.g. Do you want to see your wife and children out on the streets in rags? Of course you don’t!

Scapegoats

A scapegoat is a person or group blamed for something they are not truly responsible for. This technique simplifies complex issues and focuses the reader’s anger on a convenient target. e.g. All our crime and unemployment problems are because of the government.

Assumptions

Assumptions involve treating beliefs or opinions as facts without proof. These are often subtle and can influence readers by appealing to what is generally believed to be true. e.g. He must be smart because he is on the computer every day.

Presumptions

Presumptions occur when statements are accepted as truths without critical examination. They appeal to common beliefs or cultural norms. e.g. If it’s natural, it’s good for you.

Analogy

An analogy compares two things that are alike in some respects to clarify a point or persuade readers that what applies to one must apply to the other. e.g. If birds can fly because they have wings, then maybe if we develop mechanical wings we can fly too. e.g. The human brain is like a computer.

Expert Opinion

This technique involves citing someone perceived to have authority or expertise on a topic. It lends credibility to an argument and can be persuasive even if the “expert” is unnamed. e.g. In the thirty-five years I was Head Surgeon at...

Case Studies and Anecdotes

Case studies and anecdotes are used to personalise or humanise an argument. Anecdotes are usually emotive and personal; case studies more formal and factual. Both aim to build credibility and emotional appeal. e.g. Pool fencing should be mandatory because my neighbour’s child drowned in an unfenced pool.

Empty Truths

Also known as tautologies, these are statements that repeat the same idea unnecessarily, often sounding meaningful but lacking substance. e.g. Boys will be boys. Enough is enough. Business is business.

Cause and Effect

This technique suggests that one event directly causes another. It is persuasive because it offers logical explanation, though causality may not always be valid. e.g. Peter crashed his car. Peter died.

Ends and Means / Means to an End

This approach justifies an action based on the outcome, even if the action itself is questionable. e.g. A criminal donates to a playground; the donation is good, but the source of funds is illegal.

Consequence of a Fact

This presents a pragmatic response to an existing situation by arguing that something should be done simply because a condition exists. e.g. Nuclear power exists, so we might as well use it.

Judging an Action by its Consequences

This technique justifies or condemns an action based on its results. It focuses on outcomes rather than intent. e.g. Stealing from the rich to give to the poor is acceptable because the poor are hungry.

Sweeping Statements / Generalisations

These make broad claims that apply to a group without considering individual differences. Generalisations can sound convincing but often oversimplify. e.g. All teenagers are irresponsible.

Cliché

Clichés are overused phrases that offer familiar shortcuts to meaning. Though persuasive due to their recognisability, they can also weaken writing if not used purposefully. e.g. What goes around comes around.

Co-existence

This assumes an automatic relationship between ideas or traits, such as assuming someone is good simply because they attend church. e.g. She goes to the local church, so she must be a good person.

Guilt by Association

This technique links people, ideas or groups with undesirable individuals or arguments in order to discredit them. e.g. Premier Kennett has behaved like Hitler since coming to power.

Questioning Motive

This involves undermining an argument by questioning the motives of the speaker, diverting attention away from the argument itself. e.g. The Health Minister’s decision to close the hospital is just an excuse to build a monument to himself.

Figures and Statistics

Numbers and data are used to give the illusion of objectivity and reliability. However, the reader must critically assess source, sample size, relevance, and interpretation. e.g. 300 people died on our roads last year...

Discredit Another Argument

This technique aims to dismantle opposing viewpoints by exposing flaws or inconsistencies in them. It draws attention away from the original argument and weakens the opposing side’s credibility.

Attack

This technique involves criticising or mocking opposing arguments or individuals. It is designed to weaken the opposition’s credibility and make their position less persuasive. Writers may use name-calling, ridicule, or other personal attacks to influence the audience’s perception. One form of attack is labelling the opposition in an attempt to belittle them.

Flattery and Praise

This technique involves listing individuals or groups who agree with the writer’s position in a positive light. By using terms that convey admiration or respect, the writer bolsters the authority of their own argument. e.g. “Leading scientists support this research.”

Use of Punctuation and Printing Style

Writers use punctuation and formatting deliberately to add emphasis, create subtext, or manipulate tone and flow. Common strategies include:

  • Inverted commas – suggest doubt or irony, even when quoting.

  • Exclamation marks – show emotional emphasis or urgency.

  • Dashes – indicate pauses, hesitation or parenthetical ideas.

  • Long sentences – may signal sophistication or rambling.

  • Short sentences – create punch, pace and immediacy.

  • Italics, CAPITALISATION, bold, underlining – all used for emphasis.

Vocabulary Use

The choice of vocabulary significantly affects tone and credibility. Writers use sophisticated or precise word choice to sound more authoritative or to target specific reader emotions. Students should be aware of:

  • Emotive connotations (e.g. slaughter vs kill)

  • Word strength and subtlety (e.g. sporadic vs occasional)

  • Replacing vague or weak terms with more accurate or academic language.

Improving vocabulary can enhance clarity and persuasiveness. For example:

  • Replace “straight away” with “immediately”

  • Replace “in the next paragraph” with “in the following section”

  • Replace “always” with “consistently”

Table of Common Persuasive Techniques

Persuasive Techniques Reference Table

Persuasive TechniqueDefinitionHow It Persuades
AdjectiveDescribing words usually connected to a noun (naming word). e.g. The bright red house (bright and red = adjectives; house = noun)Adds emphasis.
AlliterationRepeating and playing upon the same letter. e.g. A back-breaking job...Adds emphasis and reinforces meaning, especially where an idea is repeated. Often used to create a highly emotive image.
AnecdoteA short account or story of an entertaining or interesting incident. e.g. In my experience...Usually makes reader sympathetic and receptive to the point; can set up a character, then position reader to accept or reject that individual character.
AssociationsWords that create meanings beyond the literal meaning of the word. e.g. attractive, alluring, pleasing, charmingSuggests or influences reader response in certain ways, often subconsciously. Implied meanings need to be identified.
Attacks/praiseThe writer attacks or praises an opponent or idea. This is really an assertion of the writer’s viewpoint. e.g. denigrating or humiliating the person, discrediting opponents as unreliable, dishonest, suggesting unsatisfactory associationsOften effective in placing the reader in a position of agreement with the writer/speaker through forceful language and insistent claims, even without evidence.
BiasOne-sidedness in presentation of view/opinion. e.g. Eminem’s tour promoter obviously sees no danger in what Eminem says.Can subjectively influence the reader by intentionally only presenting one side of the argument.
ClichésWorn out, overused expressions. e.g. Fit as a fiddle, Turn over a new leaf, Pushing the envelopeAre familiar, often colloquial, so can offer a shortcut to convey meaning.
Colourful words and descriptive languageWords that are heightened, vivid, lively, and full of interest. e.g. Hot = blistering, sultry, muggy, suffocating...Produce a picture and/or induce an emotion. Engage reader by gaining attention and often put a new slant on familiar events and issues.
ContentionThe main line of argument in a text. A statement of the main idea being argued or debated. Focuses on WHAT is being argued.Pinpoints the issue and the writer’s point of view. Helps the reader identify the issue and evaluate the techniques used to persuade them to agree.
Emotional AppealsPlay on people’s emotions such as fears, insecurities, hopes, desires, and values. Can target: • A sense of justice or injustice • Family values • Tradition or custom • Patriotism • Moral valuesFrequently appeal to our values and attitudes, sometimes subtly. Can manipulate reader to take notice of issues by triggering emotional responses.
Emotive LanguageThe deliberate use of strong emotive words to play on reader’s feelings. e.g. sleazy, slimy, vicious, disgusting, outrageousEvokes strong emotional response to coerce or force agreement. Used to stimulate strong emotional reactions and manipulate responses.
Loaded Words (Labelling)Words that are loaded with associations. e.g. capitalist, communist, salesmanShorthand way of discrediting or praising by suggesting associations. Exploits emotional baggage of such words to sway opinion.
RepetitionRepeated words, phrases, sentence patterns, ideas.Gives emphasis and prominence to a point or idea; reinforces and makes reader remember the point.
Rhetorical QuestionsQuestions that have the answer embedded in them; often use irony.Manipulate the reader to agree because the answer is assumed. Disagreement would mean dismissing a point that commands agreement.
ToneRefers to the voice of the writer; the writer’s attitude to the subject and reader. Tone can be emotive. e.g. angry, sympathetic, sarcasticReflects writer’s attitude, which can influence the reader to agree or reject. Tone can signal a new direction or perspective in the writing.

Part 06 · Analysing ArgumentChapter 3 of 6

Analysing Visual Language

Many students find the analysis of images an accessible and enjoyable part of Year 12 English. It is important, however, to prepare for a variety of visual elements that may appear on your Unit 3 SAC task on analysing argument, as well as on Section C of the end-of-year examination. Below are some elements to keep an eye out for.

Context and Position

In order to fully understand the meaning of the visual elements in a text, you need to understand the context. Be sure to read any background information accompanying the task so that you know what the main issue is. Both the background information and the written material will help give you a sense of the visual’s purpose. Remember that the focus of Area of Study 2 in the Unit 3 SAC and in Section C of the exam is on the analysis of argument. Noticing where and why a visual element is placed in the written piece can help you to tackle the idea of how the argument is structured.

  • Is the visual at the start of the article to elicit empathy or shock?

  • Is it in the middle to reinforce a particular piece of evidence or reason?

  • Is it at the end to consolidate the argument as a whole?

  • Is it a frame or border to the written piece that contributes to the overall approach and tone?

  • Alternatively, if the visual is a completely separate text from the written piece, does it present the same or a different point of view on the issue?

Ask yourself: where is the visual positioned and why does it matter?

Colour and Design Elements

Although the task material for Section C of the exam will be printed in black and white, your Unit 3 SAC might well contain colour images. If this is the case, you can certainly explore the mood that is created by the use of colour, as well as any relevant connotations of the colour scheme. In a black-and-white image it is possible to discuss degrees of shading and how these affect meaning. It is important to remember that visual language can pertain to design elements in the text you are analysing. For example, in the task material for Section C of the VCAA sample exam the speaker’s first slide has illustrations incorporated into the year ‘2010’. The embedded images of fish, a flamingo, waves, a tree, and an adult and child holding hands help to promote the speaker’s arguments about the beauty of biodiversity and humanity’s central involvement in its protection. Also be alert to the design elements of internet text types such as web pages and blogs. You can, for example, discuss the use of icons, how they position the viewer and the symbolism embedded in them. Ask yourself: how do design elements, colour and/or shading of the visual contribute to its meaning?

Cartoons

Cartoons often accompany an opinion piece; they can also be stand-alone texts offering a viewpoint on a current issue – especially one that involves politicians or high-profile figures. To understand a cartoon it can be important to have an understanding of the issues circulating in the media in any given week. As I write, there are two quite different cartoons in The Age and The Australian that contain an image of a whiteboard, among other elements. To make sense of both cartoons, a reader would need to know that earlier in the week security guards in Parliament House used a whiteboard to shield a politician from photographers and journalists. Without this knowledge it would be difficult, if not impossible, to interpret the cartoons. Try to stay informed about current affairs as you practise analysing media texts, so that you can grasp their full meanings. Cartoons tend to exaggerate the features of those they represent and use minimal – often quite pithy – text. Be mindful of where this text is: does it appear in a speech bubble or balloon and therefore represent a person’s thoughts or speech? Or is the text elsewhere – on a sign, say, or framed as a caption – providing additional context or comment? The tone of a cartoon is an important feature to note. Political cartoons in particular are often sarcastic and position the reader to be critical of politicians and/or their views and policies. Ask yourself: how does the cartoon make me feel? Your response will give you a guide to the tone of this visual.

Photographs

Photographs can provide a wealth of material for analysis. Look for a range of elements, including:

  • whether the shot is low-angle (looking up at the subject), high-angle (looking down on the subject) or straight-on – for example, a low-angle shot of a child might give the impression that they have been able to overcome adversity and achieve success, whereas a high-angle shot might suggest that a person is vulnerable or remorseful

  • whether the subject is in the foreground (making them very prominent) or background (emphasising their context)

  • whether elements of the photograph are blurred or in soft focus

  • whether a filter (e.g. warm or cool) is used

  • where the horizon is placed in a landscape shot (e.g. far away can signal isolation or opportunity)

  • what is actually in the frame (mise en scène) and what might have been left out

  • whether a text overlay has been used and how that frames your reading of the photograph.

Ask yourself:What would the impact be if I changed an element of the photograph (e.g. camera angle or focus)? Answering this question will give you better insight into what the photograph is intending to achieve.

Charts and Diagrams

Charts and diagrams containing numerical information can be used to present evidence. Although you are not in a position to question the data, you can explore how the information and its visual presentation are used to strengthen and develop an argument. It is good to watch for:

  • what the visual elements suggest and how they connect to each other

  • whether a background image can be seen beneath a written overlay (e.g. an image of a gun beneath statistics about the number of deaths caused by shooting)

  • whether data is attributed to an authority (such as the Australian Bureau of Statistics). Sometimes charts and diagrams can look dry and intimidating.

Ask yourself:how does the information relate to the written text or texts? Does it support or contradict a written text and, if so, how?

Concluding Remarks

For the Exam, it is imperative that you compare the written and visual material supplied. It is also wise to compare the written and visual texts in the examination. Comparing written and visual material in terms of the arguments they present and the ways in which they position the audience will give your analysis more depth and cohesion, and demonstrate a stronger understanding of each.

Part 06 · Analysing ArgumentChapter 4 of 6

Practice Articles for Analysis

This Book Week, let’s celebrate the power of reading (not just the costumes)

By Tracey Cheung — August 21, 2023, 11.25am It’s officially that time of year when parents scour the shops for the perfect Book Week costume. For some, making sure their little one looks just like their chosen storybook character involves face-painting, finding the right glasses, hat, bow or cape — sometimes tail and ears. Among all this, it can be easy to lose the true meaning behind Book Week. Wendy Rapee, chair of Children’s Book Council of Australia, says the event is an opportunity to draw attention to the joy of reading and for advocating for stories in children’s lives. Rapee explains this is the reason behind this year’s CBCA Children’s Book Week theme: Read, Inspire, Grow.

“Like dressing up, reading books presents the opportunity to walk in someone else’s shoes, to experience adventures, feel things they have never felt and hopefully learn something about the world they live in,” says Rapee.

Over the years, I have found that reading stories to my daughter has helped me teach her helpful life skills and ways of thinking. And I’m not the only one with this experience. Sue Cahill, a student wellbeing leader at St Charles Borromeo Primary School in Melbourne, says she finds storybooks extremely powerful in teaching her school children how to deal with various issues, such as school refusal, friendships and bullying. She used the book I Am Jack by Susanne Gervay to help two boys in a bullying situation. In the book, Jack is bullied. Cahill says the story helped the boy being bullied to see that, just like Jack, he could also find his inner strength, reach out to others, be heard and change the situation. It also helped the bully see how it made the other child feel. Cahill explains that children see themselves in book characters, which helps make the message feel real.

“After reading to them, I would ask, ‘How would you feel? What would you have done differently?’” says Cahill.

“They feel safe when they’re not talking about themselves, but about the third person. This gets them to see it from another’s perspective and builds empathy. You can start to unpack the values and learnings, using the story in the book as a trigger for conversation.”

Learning valuable relationship skills

Venita Dimos is a lawyer, mediator and the author of children’s book series Mini and Milo. Each book in the series has a different theme, including mindset, communication and listening. As a lawyer and mediator, Dimos has witnessed the profound impact of unresolved conflict in families and workplaces. Her series was driven by a heartfelt need to teach children key relationship skills early on in life.

“Managing conflict is not a skill you are born with – it’s a skill you need to learn,” she says. “Unfortunately, most people don’t learn this in early childhood, so grow up learning on the job with their relationships at stake.”

Recently, I read Dimos’ Mini and Milo: The Teeny-Tiny Voice. At the time, my daughter had picked up a swear word from somewhere. When I told her to stop saying it, she said to me, “I want to stop but the voice inside my head keeps telling me to say it.” The book taught her that we have greater power over our thoughts than we think, which eventually helped her to stop swearing.

Discussion of sensitive topics

Books have also helped encourage conversations with my daughter about issues I have otherwise felt unsure about how to open myself. For example, while I found it awkward to start conversations about the topic of body safety, reading My Body! What I Say Goes! by Jayneen Sanders helped break the ice. Lucy Kippist, 44, a community manager of Mums & Co and mother of two from Sydney, found books helpful when helping her children get through the grief of losing their grandparents. She says How Can I Help Papa? A Child’s Journey Through Loss and Healing by Elissa Al-Chokhachy helped give her children new words to describe their feelings around grief. While reading, they learned that part of grieving someone you loved is to remember the happy times.

“It elicited some happy memories with their grandfather. We talked about the more fun things, like how he used to do Lego with them,” says Kippist.

Alyson Simpson, a professor of English and literacy education at the University of Sydney, says parents don’t need to expect that a reading session will be a literacy lesson.

“Allow children to explore the books,” she says. “My grandson finds more things in the pictures than I do sometimes.”

Fostering friendships

Elisha Casagrande, 35, found that her son was able to find a message in a book without any prompts from her. The Brisbane-based health coach recently started home-schooling her son, who was having trouble being apart from his friends, so she decided to read The Giving Tree by Shel Silverstein to him. In the book, a boy makes brief visits to a ‘living” tree several times over the years, until he becomes an old man.

“My son picked up that relationships can still last, provided there’s some connection over time, like the man who kept connecting with that tree,” says Casagrande.

When I asked my 5-year-old daughter who she was going to dress up as for her first Book Week, it was an easy choice: Cinderella. Although I know she is drawn to the story because of the pretty princess, after reading the book together, I also know she understands the morals behind the story, such as the importance of kindness.

I’m Melbourne Grammar’s school captain and I’m gay. The Presbyterian Church would have me sacked

By Daniel Cash — Melbourne Grammar School Captain
April 18, 2023 — 7.30pm

The Presbyterian Church of Australia has declared its right to refuse school leadership opportunities to students based on sexuality or premarital sexual activity. This comes in response to the Australian Law Reform Commission’s review of discrimination laws. The Church has reasoned that, in the case of discriminating against students based on sexual preference, gay students “would not be able to give appropriate Christian leadership in a Christian school which requires modelling Christian living.” I write this as captain of Melbourne Grammar School. I write this as someone who is gay, a fact about myself which I cannot change. I also write this as someone confused — surely ‘modelling Christian living” is not well exemplified in the practice of exclusion or discrimination? The Christian faith is built on the example Christ set in his life. His revolutionary compassion, so radical then as it still is now — care for the outcasts, the lepers, the unclean — inform the values of Christianity and direct the moral constitution of the Church. Jesus shocked the established powers of his time, his love the instrument of a legacy which left a world faith developing in its wake. His example leads me to wonder what Christ would make of this situation. If he was confronted with a body of young people — all, to remind the Presbyterian leaders, made in the image of God, in the imago dei — would he divide them and cast inequality among their ranks? Would he tell a portion of them they could not lead their friends, simply because those they love are of the same sex? Well, Jesus did not condemn the adulterers, did not avoid the pariahs, and surely would not deem some more capable of Christian living than others because of the details of their sexuality. I should not have to pen this opinion piece. Not in modern Australia. But here I am, a student, writing an open letter to a group of adults who have lived a good deal longer than I have, and my intention here (at the risk of sounding quite bold) is to educate. I ask the Presbyterian Church of Australia to withdraw their response which outlines the right to discriminate among children and adolescents based on sexuality or sexual activity. Firstly, it is a violation of the privacy of students and a rather clumsy attempt at preserving a worldview now thankfully frowned upon. Secondly, it is the most remarkable antithesis to the true Christian values (the central commandment ‘love thy neighbour as thyself” should be the directing maxim here).

And, thirdly, this desire to discriminate among young people is plainly unsettling. The sexuality of students is not something which should be the subject of so much scrutiny — as Stephen Fry once pointed out, the stricter branches of the Church often like to brand homosexual people as exhibiting unnatural sexual attitudes, though it is not the gay community which displays an unhealthy obsession with the regulation, repression and restriction of sex; rather, the shoe is on the other foot there. I am very lucky — I am secure in myself; I attend a school which values all its students equally. Yet so, so many children do not yet have this same confidence. The influence of the Presbyterian Church of Australia in declaring the right to discriminate against students based on sexuality is therefore mightily dangerous to some of the most vulnerable in our society. Those people behind this submission to the ALRC need to exhibit the compassion which their faith is built on, as do the other religious groups which have submitted similar requests. Organised religion is a powerful force for positivity. Those groups which tarnish the name of spirituality by using it to justify prejudice do a disservice to all people of faith, and they endanger all those who fall under their care.

— Daniel Cash, Melbourne Grammar School Captain

Confession of a Knox Grammar old boy: my son won’t lose sleep as I have

By Malcolm Knox — Journalist, author and columnist
September 2, 2022 — 10.15am An old schoolmate invited me to his home for dinner. We hadn’t seen each other in the 30 years since we had left Knox Grammar, the school that hit the headlines this week after boys were suspended or left after posting what their headmaster, Scott James, called “inappropriate images and … offensive commentary in an online private chatroom”. Much of the content was so stomach-turning it could not be published by The Daily Telegraph, which broke the story. Back to my schoolmate. Towards the end of a pleasant evening, he revealed he had never got over his bitterness at the horrendous abuse he had received at Knox (from other students, not the teachers who were secretly abusing boys sexually in that same era). The damage he revealed, even as an adult with a good life deriving from his mighty intelligence, his lovely young family and the educational headstart he’d had in life, was chilling. I thought: Uh-oh. After a silence, I said, “I want to know – was I responsible for any of that?” He said with a sharpness that showed how hurt he still was, “I wouldn’t have you in my home if you were one of them.” The awful truth for me was, I honestly wouldn’t have known if I had or hadn’t been “one of them”. In the discussion of the most recent Knox Grammar disgrace (like many private schools, it can count scandal among its many traditions), much focus is on online malignancy, in this case in an app the boys used called Discord. Attention has been drawn to the $10 million or so that taxpayers provide the school, including inadvertent state overfunding last year of some $1.8 million, on top of more than $80 million poured in by parents and donors. If you want to use the word “offensive”, annual school fees are up to $35,130. If they had kept pace with inflation since I was there, those fees would now be about $9000. The resultant shift towards economic elitism speaks for itself. When the blame is not placed on entitlement, toxic masculinity or social media, it is shifted back to those individuals and their parents. Poor examples at home, absence of morals, no limits. These incidents recur year after year, or the tiny sliver that is uncovered and reported, and responsibility becomes so diffuse that the remedy shrinks back to the simple punishment of the children involved. And so the preconditions continue. I have no idea whether adolescents today behave worse or better than in the past, nor whether teenage boys are any different from teenage girls. Many of us would like to think that the narrowing of economic entitlement produced by elevated school fees has lowered morality to a kind of local Eton-Harrow gutter. The online environment generates its own sewer mentality, which is also soothing to those who already believe electronic devices are the source of all evil, but I’m not so sure any of those factors is more to blame than any other. What I do know, from my schoolmate’s ongoing anger and my phantom guilt, is that the worst bullying from teenaged boys and girls stems from strength in numbers, and it is essentially a performance in front of a group. It is simplistic to think these boys are actually glorifying Adolf Hitler. What they are doing arises from an intoxicated over-exuberance that renders the perpetrators too stupid and too full of themselves to have the first idea of what they are doing. These particular teenagers are not solitary wolves brooding alone over why they are victims in this world (those are the really dangerous ones who, in countries where they have easy access to guns, go out and use them). The bully groups believe they are among the strong, whose group identity is consolidated in attacking the weak. They do know right from wrong; the problem is that “wrong” is just another boundary that they can compete with each other in crossing. You want to cause shock. You escalate it to anti-Semitism, gross misogyny and homophobia precisely because you know they are forbidden places; you want your friends to gasp and giggle at your audacity. Drunk on group approval, enclosed in your little group, you think your crime is victimless. This is not solo violence; it is one big circle-jerk. I recently stumbled across my HSC copy of Jane Austen’s Pride and Prejudice, which I had annotated in those days when our group’s competitive over-exuberance might have unwittingly – witlessly – harmed schoolmates. Aside from getting top marks for stating the bleeding obvious, Young Malcolm’s notes showed a peculiar viciousness towards Mrs Bennet. Austen makes her a comical figure, of course, but her interfering and alarmist ways evidently got right under Young Malcolm’s skin. He felt sorry for Elizabeth but sorriest for poor old Mr Darcy, the uber-wealthy landowner victimised by the hysterically anxious mother-of-five. Young Malcolm’s notes escalate into misogynistic territory, egged on by extra comments by friends, towards what we all knew were outrageous slurs against a type of woman. So much for the civilising power of Austen. We knew it was wrong; being wrong was what made it funny. This was two decades before social media, two decades before the private school fee explosion. It wasn’t as hateful or propagated as widely as what Knox boys in groups do in 2022, but it was on the way. So, although all the usual suspects feed into today’s brand of excess, I really believe the enduring toxicity goes back to competition, group dynamics and boys performing for each other. Unfortunately, it lies in a continuum with high-status performances in sport, on the stage, in the classroom, and in social life. Excess, good and bad, comes out of competitiveness. If we are going to deal with the dark side of boys trying to outdo each other, we also have to come to a more mature and rational reckoning with all sides of the competitive encouragements of late-stage capitalism. Boys conceal all sorts of emotions, not least their fear of the future and fear of who they really are, under the cloak of competitiveness that they are constantly told is a good thing. I do harbour hope. My son would never have dreamt of behaving like those Knox boys, even though he was on his device a lot and had a privileged education. I don’t put it down to good parenting. I put it down to his innate morals, his empathy after having been bullied himself, and his not being part of an alpha male group inebriated on their own performances. He’s a better person than I was at the same age, despite – or really, because of – not having as many friends, not being as full of himself and not being over-rewarded for competitive success. It also helped that he went to a school that did not see competitive success as the be-all and end-all, or ingrain the fear in young people that life would eventually sort them into winners and losers. I know for sure that in 30 years, he won’t be kept awake wondering if he unknowingly made one of his schoolmates’ life a misery.

Don’t ask me to give the Queen a minute’s silence, ask me for the truth about British colonialism

By Lidia Thorpe
Wednesday, 14 September 2022 — 12.07 AEST

‘We’re not grieving a singular human life, we’re reeling from the violence that is the legacy of the monarchy.” — Photograph: Mick Tsikas/AAP

News of the Queen’s death broke on the same day as my cousin’s funeral. My cousin, like more than 500 First Nations people in the last 30 years alone, died in custody. I was notified on Monday of another death in custody.

The institutions that British colonisation brought here — from the education that erases us to the prisons that kill us — are designed to destroy the oldest living culture in the world. That’s the legacy of the crown in this country.

The “British empire” declared a war on these shores, against this country’s First Nations peoples. This led to massacres. And you want a minute’s silence from me?

Their war continues and is still felt today — on our children, our men, our land, our water, the air we breathe. Yet we’re meant to kneel to the colonising force with our hands on our hearts?

It is insulting that the prime minister, Anthony Albanese, has called the 22 September public holiday a “National Day of Mourning for Her Majesty The Queen”, when First Nations people have called for 26 January to be acknowledged as a Day of Mourning since 1938.

We called for a Day of Mourning so that this country could understand how we’re still affected by colonisation today. We’re not grieving a singular human life, we’re reeling from the violence that is the legacy of the monarchy.

Who gave permission for our flag to be lowered to half-mast? That power has been taken away from us, again.

Our young people are turning to suicide, because they don’t see a future for themselves in the systems the monarchy created in this country.

I’m writing this in solidarity with First Nations people who are triggered by the glorification of our oppressor, to the mothers who will have to listen to people grieving the Queen while living in fear of their babies being stolen, and our kids who will have to pay their respects at school despite the British empire’s attempts to wipe us out.

Colonial governments are complicit in continuing the crimes against First Nations people because that is the agenda of the coloniser against First Nations people around the world. To think that people can stand and celebrate that is ignorant and painful.

First Nations people are proud, sovereign people. The British empire tried to wipe us out, but they failed. We are still here and we have an opportunity to do things differently in this country.

The “demise of the crown” comes with the opportunity to listen to grassroots First Nations people, who are telling the truth about the monarchy in this country.

Breaking the cycle of colonial oppression starts with truth-telling, and truth-listening. Understanding that this is our shared heritage will help us move forward and heal this country.

Truth-telling holds up a mirror to our society and asks: who are we? Where are we coming from, and where do we want to be?

Do we want to be a nation who can’t control our own affairs? Where our head of state is whoever was born into the right family? Do we want to champion stolen wealth and privilege, or are we a country that cares about equality and democracy?

We’re one of the few Commonwealth countries that doesn’t have a treaty with First Nations people.

Treaty is an end to the war. It’s when we come together to negotiate how we can live alongside each other, peacefully. Treaty will create a new national identity — one we can all feel proud of and a part of.

If we want to mature as a nation, the only mechanism to truly unite us is a treaty.

Join us in creating a nation that we can all celebrate.
