Part 10

Exam Checklists

In this part

1General Checklist for Section A Response2General Checklist for Section B Response3General Checklist for Section C Response
Part 10 · Exam ChecklistsChapter 1 of 3

General Checklist for Section A Response

Textual Understanding and Interpretation

  • Have I demonstrated a clear and accurate understanding of the text?

  • Have I explored the ideas, concerns, and values present in the text?

  • Have I considered the structure and language used in the text to convey meaning?

  • Have I shown awareness of the complexities or subtleties in the text’s ideas?

Response to the Topic

  • Have I clearly addressed the essay topic throughout my response?

  • Have I interpreted the implications of the topic (not just the literal meaning)?

  • Have I developed a relevant and thoughtful argument or discussion?

  • Have I used an appropriate strategy to explore the topic (e.g., thematic, character-based, structural)?

Use of Evidence

  • Have I supported my ideas with specific and relevant evidence from the text?

  • Have I explained how my evidence supports my argument?

  • Have I used quotations effectively and accurately?

Organisation and Coherence

  • Is my essay well-structured, with a clear introduction, body, and conclusion?

  • Are my ideas logically sequenced and connected?

  • Have I written clear topic sentences and developed each paragraph?

Language Use

  • Is my language accurate, appropriate, and mostly free of errors?

  • Have I used precise and expressive vocabulary to convey meaning?

  • Is my writing fluent and confident in tone?

Part 10 · Exam ChecklistsChapter 2 of 3

General Checklist for Section B Response

Engagement with Ideas and Stimulus

  • Have I considered the ideas raised by the title and stimulus in a thoughtful and meaningful way?

  • Have I connected these ideas to a clear and relevant Framework of Ideas?

  • Have I explored the implications or complexities of the ideas, rather than treating them superficially?

Purpose and Voice

  • Have I clearly established the purpose(s) of my text?

  • Have I maintained an appropriate and consistent voice throughout the piece?

  • Is the form of the piece suited to the intended audience and purpose (e.g., speech, monologue, letter)?

Control of Language

  • Have I used language that is clear, expressive, and appropriate for the form?

  • Have I used a rich and precise vocabulary to strengthen my writing?

  • Have I varied sentence structure and language features to create impact and cohesion?

Control of Text Structure

  • Is my writing well-organised and easy to follow?

  • Have I structured the text purposefully, with a clear beginning, middle, and end?

  • Have I used paragraphs and transitions effectively to guide the reader?

Part 10 · Exam ChecklistsChapter 3 of 3

General Checklist for Section C Response

Understanding of Argument and Contention

  • Have I accurately identified the contention of the text?

  • Have I understood how the argument(s) are structured and developed across the text?

  • Have I recognised the overall point of view being expressed by the author/speaker?

Analysis of Persuasive Language and Visuals

  • Have I analysed how language features (written or spoken) are used to persuade the intended audience?

  • Have I explained how visuals (if any) support or enhance the written or spoken argument?

  • Have I discussed how these elements work together to construct the overall persuasive strategy?

Expression and Language Use

  • Have I used language that is clear, precise, and appropriate to an analytical task?

  • Have I written fluently and used analytical vocabulary confidently?

  • Have I maintained a consistent and formal tone suitable for academic analysis?

Part 11

Key Takeaways from the 2024 Assessment Report

The 2024 VCE English exam, the first under the revised study design, demonstrated impressive student engagement and strong outcomes. According to the VCAA report, students were generally well prepared and confident. However, the report also identified key areas for improvement. This guide breaks down practical strategies for success in each section of the exam, grounded in the report’s findings.

In this part

1General Exam Advice2Section A: Analytical Response to a Text3Section B - Creating a Text (Framework of Ideas)4Section C - Analysing Argument5Final Exam Checklist6Final Advice
Part 11 · Key Takeaways from the 2024 Assessment ReportChapter 1 of 6

General Exam Advice

What Worked

  • Students clearly understood the structure and expectations of the exam.

  • Many responses demonstrated well-developed writing and analytical skills.

What Needs Improvement

  • Some students forgot to clearly label their chosen text (Section A) or Framework of Ideas (Section B).

  • Some responses misinterpreted the prompt or overlooked key words in the question.

Practical Tips

  • Label your work clearly. Always begin with: Section A: ‘Text: Macbeth | Prompt: ‘Ambition leads to downfall. Do you agree?’” Section B: ‘Title: Resist and Persist | Framework: Protest”

  • Build your vocabulary. Familiarise yourself with words like inescapable, perceptions, central concern, implication, etc.

  • Select the best-fit prompt. Choose the one that allows you to demonstrate your strongest understanding, not just the one that sounds easiest.

Part 11 · Key Takeaways from the 2024 Assessment ReportChapter 2 of 6

Section A: Analytical Response to a Text

Your task: Write a structured essay in response to one of two prompts for your studied text.

What Worked

  • High-scoring responses interpreted the topic with nuance and clarity.

  • Students demonstrated a strong understanding of authorial intent.

  • Responses were well structured and supported with relevant textual evidence.

What Needs Improvement

  • Some students only responded to part of the question (e.g. discussing ‘youth” but not ‘infatuation with youth”).

  • Others summarised the plot or characters instead of analysing the text’s ideas and values.

Practical Tips

Section A - Effective vs Ineffective Approaches
Effective ApproachIneffective Approach
Interpreting all keywords in the topicIgnoring modifiers (e.g. “unrelenting cruelty” vs “cruelty”)
Using a clear “what/how/why” structureListing unrelated examples
Connecting ideas across paragraphsTreating each paragraph as a standalone point
Focusing on how the author constructs meaningJust retelling the storyline

’ Tip: Write practice introductions where you break down the prompt. Ask:

  • What is the main idea or concept?

  • Are there two ideas in relationship?

  • What are the implications or assumptions?

Part 11 · Key Takeaways from the 2024 Assessment ReportChapter 3 of 6

Section B - Creating a Text (Framework of Ideas)

Your task: Create an original piece of writing that responds to a title and stimulus, grounded in one of the four Frameworks of Ideas.

What Worked

  • High-scoring pieces treated the title and stimulus as central, not secondary.

  • Strong voice, clear purpose, and tailored language made the writing stand out.

  • Responses showed thoughtful exploration of ideas relevant to the Framework.

What Needs Improvement

  • Some students ignored or misunderstood the Framework (e.g. writing about sports under the Protest framework).

  • Others forced pre-prepared stories to fit the title.

  • Lack of planning resulted in unstructured or unfocused pieces.

Practical Strategies

Planning Method

  • Framework: Personal Journeys

  • Title: Finding My Way

  • Stimulus: Butterfly emerging from a cocoon

  • Purpose: Reflective

  • Audience: Teenagers navigating cultural identity

  • Text Type: Memoir

Tips for Strong Writing

  • Use recurring imagery or motifs to strengthen cohesion.

  • Match tone to purpose: persuasive, reflective, emotive, or narrative.

  • End with intention: resolution, reflection, or call to action.

Part 11 · Key Takeaways from the 2024 Assessment ReportChapter 4 of 6

Section C - Analysing Argument

Your task: Analyse how a written and visual text positions its audience.

What Worked

  • Identifying the author’s contention and target audience.

  • Analysing tone, persuasive language, structure, and visuals.

  • Exploring how language is used to shape reader response.

What Needs Improvement

  • Avoid retelling the article.

  • Go beyond spotting techniques; explain their effect and purpose.

Practical Strategies

Section B: Effective Strategies and Examples
StrategyExample
Identify contention and purpose‘The writer argues that vaping is a growing public health crisis and seeks to alarm parents.”
Link language to effect‘By using the phrase ‘a ticking time bomb’, the writer creates a sense of urgency and panic.”
Analyse visuals‘The image of a distressed child reinforces the emotional appeal used in the article’s opening.”
Use precise metalanguageAppeal to fear, inclusive language, repetition, expert opinion, etc.
Part 11 · Key Takeaways from the 2024 Assessment ReportChapter 5 of 6

Final Exam Checklist

  • Have I practised responding to a range of prompts and titles?

  • Do I clearly understand my Frameworks of Ideas?

  • Can I write for a clear audience and purpose?

  • Have I reviewed unfamiliar vocabulary and metalanguage?

  • Can I plan and write all three sections under timed conditions?

Part 11 · Key Takeaways from the 2024 Assessment ReportChapter 6 of 6

Final Advice

The 2026 exam will reward students who think deeply, write purposefully, and tailor their writing to the demands of the task. Whether you’re analysing a text, creating your own piece, or evaluating argument and language, success lies in showing insight, clarity, and control. Strong writing does not come from memorisation—it comes from understanding. Prepare thoughtfully, practise deliberately, and revise reflectively.

Part 12

Key Takeaways from the 2025 Assessment Report

What the VCAA assessors reported from the 2025 English examination, broken down by section — the marking approach, the strategies that earned top marks, the pitfalls that cost them, and a priority checklist distilled for the 2026 cohort.

In this part

1General Exam Advice2Section A: Analytical Response to a Text3Section B: Creating a Text4Section C: Analysis of Argument and Language5Priority Checklist for 2026
Part 12

General Exam Advice

The 2025 examination produced near-identical averages across all three sections — 5.4, 5.4 and 5.5 out of 10 — with very few students at either extreme. The middle is crowded; the marks that separate a 5 from an 8 come from the specific skills assessors described, not from writing more.

SectionAverage /10What it was
A5.4Analytical response to a text
B5.4Creating a text (Frameworks of Ideas)
C5.5Analysis of argument and language

How assessors marked

  • Holistic, criteria-based, benchmarked. Every section was assessed against the published criteria and expected qualities (EQs), using benchmark scripts to set standards. The three EQs are interconnected: the quality of your ideas (EQ1) sets the level of challenge for structuring and substantiating them (EQ2), and language (EQ3) is judged by how well it communicates those ideas.
  • Everything is applied in relation to the topic. Sound ideas, apt quotations or fluent expression that do not serve the set task cannot score highly. This principle recurs through every section of the report.
  • First-draft conditions were respected. Assessors explicitly acknowledged that exam writing is timed first-draft work; fluency was not equated with perfection.

Administrative habits that mattered

  • Shade the box for your chosen text and topic in Section A, and for your Framework and stimulus in Section B — and write the Section B title at the start of your response.
  • Choose the topic that best lets you demonstrate your knowledge and skills, not the one you predicted.
  • Bring a dictionary. Assessors specifically noted students with no strategy for unfamiliar words in topics — some simply substituted "and" for a term they didn't understand, which derailed their essays.
Part 12

Section A: Analytical Response to a Text

The 2025 topics were deliberately narrow — specified ideas and values rather than broad prompts — and this shaped how responses needed to be structured, sequenced and evidenced. Students with topic-interpretation strategies handled both familiar and unfamiliar phrasings well.

Topic types

  • Propositional topics offered a proposition with a command term: Discuss, Do you agree? or To what extent do you agree? You may challenge the proposition where warranted, but never the topic's conceptual focus itself — a topic linking love and destruction requires discussing both, though "inseparable" can be contested.
  • Quotation topics paired a quotation with a proposition. There was no obligatory way to use the quotation, but how you used it revealed your understanding of what it conveys in context.

The verbs

The verb in a topic carries the author's purpose, and flattening every verb into a generic "presents" was unwise. Assessors also warned that verbs are often nominalised or implied, and new ones appear each year.

Used in 2025New in 2025
celebrates, challenges, condemns, demonstrates, displays, explores, highlights, mocks, shows, suggestsis (a cry for), depicts, glorifies, warns

Implications: where the top marks lived

There was a direct correlation between recognising a topic's implications and higher scores. Watch for: absolute terms ("there are no right choices"), connotation ("easy to overlook"), what a proposition is silent about, comparison ("requires more than individual effort" — the 2025 Rainbow's End topic), an included quotation, and the verb itself.

What high-scoring responses did

  • Interpreted before writing — considered what the topic implies, not just what it states.
  • Sequenced strategically — used opening and closing summary sentences in each paragraph to make ideas and their connections explicit, guiding the reader through the reading of the text.
  • Substantiated with range — drew on linguistic elements, characters, structure, plot and allusion, and discussed how these interplayed; assessors noted high scorers took "a more philosophic consideration of the ideas, rather than a literal consideration".
  • Resolved the topic — used the conclusion to actually answer the question, showing the interplay of context, audience and purpose.
  • Used language to communicate. Sophisticated vocabulary scored only when it carried sophisticated ideas; "poorly applied polysyllabic words" were called out explicitly.

What limited responses

  • Discussing a favourite theme adjacent to the topic instead of the topic (e.g. writing about patriarchy when the topic was about truth).
  • Addressing only half the topic — villainy but not victimhood in the Sunset Boulevard topic "individuals can be both victims and villains".
  • Substituting prepared material for the terms actually on the paper, or having no strategy for unfamiliar words.
Part 12

Section B: Creating a Text

Nearly half the state wrote on protest, yet all four Frameworks averaged within 0.4 marks of each other — choose the Framework that suits your ideas, not the popular one.

Framework% of studentsAverage score /20
Writing about country10%11.2
Writing about protest47%10.9
Writing about personal journeys25%10.9
Writing about play17%10.8

The rules of the task

  • The title is fixed. You cannot change or modify it; assessors ignored any additions. Choosing a title selects its Framework — no mixing and matching titles, Frameworks or stimulus sets. If your shaded box and your title disagreed, assessors followed the title.
  • Write the title at the start and shade both the Framework and stimulus boxes.
  • Stimulus ideas must come from your selected Framework's stimulus material, though how you use them is open.
  • Mentor texts don't need referencing, and there is no preferred form, style, purpose or audience — responses could explain, express, reflect or argue, and could adapt or subvert any prose form.

What lifted responses

  • Using the title as the focus while exploring Framework ideas purposefully through a cohesive text.
  • Making deliberate structural and linguistic choices that amplify ideas — the annotated high scorers moved economically between place and time, used imagery to imply location, and chose words that carried complex emotion.
  • Establishing a voice that connects strategically with the reader — one exemplar built "a thoughtful, confiding reflective voice" from its opening lines.
  • Trusting an adult reader to infer meaning, and guiding that inference deliberately.

What limited responses

  • Using stimulus literally (a story of children playing a board game with no other connection to the Framework or title).
  • Rambling texts with no observable purpose; simplistic or barely-related ideas; relying on plot alone.
  • Contorting a pre-prepared piece to fit the exam title, or writing for yourself rather than a reader.
  • Sensationalised content, cloying sentiment and clichéd language — assessors said these "were rarely the most powerful option". Remember an unknown adult reads this for assessment.
Part 12

Section C: Analysis of Argument and Language

The 2025 text was a letter to a community newsletter from a "local resident" proposing that a regional town replace its annual fireworks with an alternative celebration. Students found it highly accessible — which made the discriminators sharper.

Background information is not decoration

The publication details and the author's "local resident" persona had to be used: inferring the regional audience and its values, the author's altruistic credibility, and the unhurried "evolution, not urgency" framing enabled the most focused analyses. Read the background inferentially before writing — the analysis flows from it.

The argument structure most students saw

  • Establishing a shared value: love of the celebration in its beautiful setting.
  • Raising previously unconsidered problems — environment, frightened pets (with a photo), noise-sensitive residents, cost — aligned with community values.
  • Rebutting reasons to keep the status quo.
  • Presenting the alternative as a "win-win" (with an inspiring second visual).

What separated skill levels

  • Identification is not analysis. Most students could list arguments and label techniques; far fewer could explain how the sequencing of ideas served the purpose, or what a device actually did to the intended audience.
  • Interplay was the discriminator. The strongest responses explained how argument, written language and visuals worked together — nearly everyone noticed the frightened dog photo, but skill showed in explaining its contribution to the developing argument, not just "appeals to emotion".
  • Strategic selection beat exhaustive coverage. Discussing every element was impossible in the time and wasn't expected; choosing the elements most central to the persuasion correlated strongly with success.

Language worth recognising

Assessor-noted elements included appeals to parochial pride, social justice and economic responsibility; strategic characterisation ("beloved pets", "little Fido or Fluffy", "cheaper and smarter"); escalation ("a single wayward spark ... devastating consequences"); reflection-prompting rhetorical questions; inspirational collective language ("Together we have the opportunity ... to lead the way"); and closing the door on alternatives ("a no-brainer").

Part 12

Priority Checklist for 2026

The habits below fall straight out of the 2025 report — each one addresses something assessors explicitly rewarded or penalised.

Before the exam

  • Build a topic-interpretation routine: identify the conceptual focus, the command term, the verb, and at least two implications before planning.
  • Rehearse unfamiliar-verb strategy — and pack a dictionary.
  • Practise writing to a fixed title in Section B without bending a pre-prepared piece to fit.
  • For Section C practice, always start from the background information and write down audience, purpose and author positioning before analysing.

In the exam

  • Shade every box: text and topic (Section A); Framework and stimulus (Section B) — and write the Section B title first.
  • Section A: open and close each paragraph with sentences that link your ideas to the topic; use the conclusion to resolve the question.
  • Section B: keep the title in view as you write; make every structural and word choice serve your purpose and reader.
  • Section C: select the few elements most central to the persuasion and explain their interplay — argument, language and visuals together.
  • Everywhere: sophisticated language only where it communicates a sophisticated idea.

Summarised from the VCAA 2025 English examination report. Read alongside the 2024 takeaways and the assessment criteria.

Part 13

Effectively Studying For Exams

In this part

1Assess your current study habits2Create a personalised study schedule3Organise your study materials4Implement active recall and spaced repetition5Utilise practice exams and past papers6Develop a healthy study-life balance7Collaborate with study groups8Seek help when needed9Fine-tune your exam day strategy10Week-by-Week Revision Program
Part 13 · Effectively Studying For ExamsChapter 1 of 9

Assess your current study habits

Identifying strengths and weaknesses

Take a few minutes to jot down what’s working well in your current study routine. What areas could use some improvement? Consider factors like time management, focus, and understanding of different subjects.

Setting realistic goals

Based on your assessment, you could start by setting 2-3 specific, achievable goals for improving your study habits. Make these goals SMART: Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant, and Time-bound. Write them down and place them somewhere visible as a daily reminder.

Part 13 · Effectively Studying For ExamsChapter 2 of 9

Create a personalised study schedule

Now that you know where you stand, it’s time to map out a path to exam success. Think of your study schedule as a roadmap to help you navigate your way through VCE studies.

Time management tips

List all your subjects and the topics you need to cover for each. Assess how much time you have until your exams. Consider prioritising subjects based on difficulty and your current performance. Using a study schedule template Remember: Be realistic with your schedule. If you’re not a morning person, a sunrise study session may not suit you. Try to work with your natural rhythms, and not against them.

Find a study schedule template that works for you – digital or physical, whichever works best.

Block out fixed commitments like school, extracurriculars, and family time. Allocate study blocks for each subject, ensuring a mix of subjects each day. Don’t forget to schedule breaks and ‘me time’ to avoid feelings of burnout.

Part 13 · Effectively Studying For ExamsChapter 3 of 9

Organise your study materials

A cluttered space can contribute to a cluttered mind. Here are some strategies to effectively organise your study materials.

Gathering resources

Collect all your textbooks, notes, and past papers for each subject. Organise digital resources into clearly labelled folders on your computer. Consider creating a ‘study toolkit’ with essentials like highlighters, sticky notes, and index cards.

Creating a study-friendly environment

Find a quiet, well-lit space where you can focus without distractions. Ensure you have a comfortable chair and desk at the right height. Keep your study area clean and organised – a tidy space can do wonders for your concentration.

Part 13 · Effectively Studying For ExamsChapter 4 of 9

Implement active recall and spaced repetition

Below are some tried and true study techniques you might like to explore.

The power of self-testing

After studying a topic, close your books and try to recall as much as you can. Create flashcards for key concepts and test yourself regularly. Explain concepts out loud as if you’re teaching someone else.

Spacing out your study sessions

Avoid cramming by spreading out your study sessions over time. Review material at increasing intervals: e.g. 1 day later, 3 days later, 1 week later, etc.

Part 13 · Effectively Studying For ExamsChapter 5 of 9

Utilise practice exams and past papers

Another way to effectively prepare for exams is by practising past exams. Here are some techniques you might find helpful.

Simulating exam conditions

Find past papers or practice exams for your subjects. Set up a quiet space and time yourself as you would in a real exam. Avoid using your notes or textbooks during these practice sessions.

Analysing your performance

Mark your practice exams using the relevant marking schemes, if available. Identify areas where you lost marks and focus on improving these in your study sessions. Keep a log of common mistakes to avoid repeating them in the real exam.

Part 13 · Effectively Studying For ExamsChapter 6 of 9

Develop a healthy study-life balance

It’s important to look after yourself and practice self-care during this time. Find what works best for you, or try our ideas below.

Incorporating breaks and relaxation

Use techniques like the Pomodoro method: 25 minutes of focused study followed by a 5-minute break. Schedule longer breaks for activities you enjoy – maybe a quick game of basketball or a chat with friends. Practice mindfulness or meditation to help manage stress and improve focus.

Maintaining physical and mental well-being

Aim for 7-9 hours of sleep per night – your brain consolidates memories and learning during sleep. Incorporate regular exercise to improve your mood and reduce stress. Eat a balanced diet with brain-boosting foods like blueberries, nuts, and fatty fish.

Part 13 · Effectively Studying For ExamsChapter 7 of 9

Collaborate with study groups

Two heads are better than one, and a study group can be a powerhouse of knowledge, inspiration and motivation.

The benefits of peer learning

Explaining concepts to others can help solidify your own understanding. Gain new perspectives and insights from your peers. Stay motivated and accountable with group study sessions.

Organising effective study sessions

Set clear objectives for each study session. Use tools like shared Google Docs or virtual whiteboards for collaborative note-taking.

Part 13 · Effectively Studying For ExamsChapter 8 of 9

Seek help when needed

Remember, asking for help isn’t a sign of weakness.

Utilising teacher support

Don’t hesitate to approach your teachers with specific questions. Attend any extra help sessions offered by your school or community. Ask for feedback on your work.

Exploring additional resources

Explore online tutorials or video explanations for challenging topics. Consider getting a tutor for subjects where you’re really struggling. Join online forums or study groups for your specific subjects.

Part 13 · Effectively Studying For ExamsChapter 9 of 9

Fine-tune your exam day strategy

When your exams are approaching – make sure you’re feeling prepared.

Pre-exam preparation

Get a good night’s sleep before the exam. Eat a nutritious breakfast to fuel your brain. Arrive at the exam venue early to avoid last-minute stress.

During the exam tactics

Read through the entire paper before starting to answer questions. Manage your time wisely – allocate minutes to each section based on mark values. If you get stuck on a question, move on and come back to it later.

Part 13 · Effectively Studying For Exams

Week-by-Week Revision Program

Twelve weeks of structured revision mapping this site’s chapters and tools to a plan. Count back twelve weeks from the exam and start; if you have fewer weeks left, begin at the matching week number and compress the early stages. Adjust around your SACs — the order matters more than the calendar.

Weeks 12–11 · Rebuild the foundations

  • Read: your text’s Background and Context and Values chapters (Sunset Boulevard / Rainbow’s End), then What is Needed in an Essay.
  • Do: re-watch the film / re-read the play once, casually. Skim the scene summaries or quote tables alongside.
  • Tool: 10 minutes of Quote Flashcards daily — start building recall now, when it’s cheap.

Weeks 10–9 · Master the evidence

  • Read: the Thematic and Character Quote Banks for your text, and the Symbols chapters.
  • Do: build a one-page theme map: each major theme, three quotes, one symbol, one technique. The Glossary supplies the technique names.
  • Tool: flashcards daily; add the Technique Quiz twice a week.

Weeks 8–7 · Learn the craft of the essay

  • Read: Planning, Analytical Text Response Structure (the Th.E.S.A.M.L. method), and the the Prompt Types chapter.
  • Study: one annotated exemplar per week (SB / RE) — click through every highlight, one colour category at a time.
  • Tool: Essay Outline Builder — plan two essays per week without writing them. Micro-Drills for topic sentences.

Weeks 6–5 · Write, mark, rewrite

  • Do: one full timed Section A essay per week (Practice Topics & Timer, 60 minutes). Mark it with the Essay Marker or swap with a friend against the criteria.
  • Read: the 2024 and 2025 assessor takeaways — especially the topic-verb guidance.
  • Fix: run a paragraph through the Vocabulary Upgrader after each essay.

Weeks 4–3 · Sections B and C

  • Read: Creating Texts with its three scored sample pieces, and Analysing Argument including the vocabulary bank.
  • Do: one timed Section B piece and one timed Section C analysis per week; mark both with the Essay Marker. Use the practice stimuli and practice articles.

Weeks 2 · Full dress rehearsals

  • Do: at least one complete three-hour exam under conditions — the Exam Generator makes a fresh paper, or use a practice exam. Fifteen minutes reading time, no phone, all three sections.
  • Mark: every section against the criteria; note which section costs you most and give it the spare hours this week.

Week 1 · Final week

  • Read: the Exam Checklists, the Priority Checklist for 2026, and Exam Format one last time.
  • Do: light passes of the quote banks and flashcards; one plan a day with the Outline Builder — no more full essays after mid-week. Sleep is revision.

Pair this program with the advice in the rest of Effectively Studying For Exams — spaced repetition, study-life balance, and exam-day strategy.

Study Tools

Glossary of Techniques

Every film and persuasive technique from the guide in one alphabetical reference.

Ad hominem Metalanguage

Attacking the person making an argument rather than the argument itself.

Adjective Persuasive technique

Describing words usually connected to a noun (naming word). e.g. The bright red house (bright and red = adjectives; house = noun) — Adds emphasis.

Aerial shot Film technique

A shot with the camera positioned directly above a subject.

Allegory Metalanguage

A narrative whose characters and events stand for a parallel moral, political or historical meaning.

Alliteration Persuasive technique

Repeating and playing upon the same letter. e.g. A back-breaking job... — Adds emphasis and reinforces meaning, especially where an idea is repeated. Often used to create a highly emotive image.

Allusion Metalanguage

An indirect reference to another text, event or figure that imports its associations into the work.

Anaphora Metalanguage

Deliberate repetition of a word or phrase at the start of successive clauses to build rhythm and force.

Anecdote Persuasive technique

A short account or story of an entertaining or interesting incident. e.g. In my experience... — Usually makes reader sympathetic and receptive to the point; can set up a character, then position reader to accept or reject that individual character.

Antithesis Metalanguage

Setting opposing ideas in balanced phrasing to sharpen the contrast between them.

Appeal to authority Metalanguage

Citing experts, credentials or institutions so the audience defers to the position.

Appeal to fear Metalanguage

Raising threatening consequences so the audience feels the risk of not agreeing.

Appeal to hip-pocket Metalanguage

Framing an issue around cost and personal finances to engage self-interest.

Appeal to patriotism Metalanguage

Invoking national identity and pride so agreement feels loyal and dissent disloyal.

Archetype Metalanguage

A character type or pattern recurring across literature that carries instant associations.

Aside Metalanguage

A short remark delivered to the audience that other characters do not hear.

Associations Persuasive technique

Words that create meanings beyond the literal meaning of the word. e.g. attractive, alluring, pleasing, charming — Suggests or influences reader response in certain ways, often subconsciously. Implied meanings need to be identified.

Attacks/praise Persuasive technique

The writer attacks or praises an opponent or idea. This is really an assertion of the writer’s viewpoint. e.g. denigrating or humiliating the person, discrediting opponents as unreliable, dishonest, suggesting unsatisfactory associations — Often effective in placing the reader in a position of agreement with the writer/speaker through forceful language and insistent claims, even without evidence.

Bandwagon Metalanguage

Suggesting most people already agree, pressuring the audience to join the majority.

Bias Persuasive technique

One-sidedness in presentation of view/opinion. e.g. Eminem’s tour promoter obviously sees no danger in what Eminem says. — Can subjectively influence the reader by intentionally only presenting one side of the argument.

Call to action Metalanguage

A direct instruction telling the audience what to do next.

Characterisation Metalanguage

The methods an author uses to construct a character: dialogue, actions, appearance, others' reactions.

Chiaroscuro Metalanguage

Strong contrast of light and shadow in a frame, often signalling moral ambiguity or noir style.

Cliché Metalanguage

A worn expression whose overuse signals lazy or manipulative reasoning — or is deployed knowingly.

Clichés Persuasive technique

Worn out, overused expressions. e.g. Fit as a fiddle, Turn over a new leaf, Pushing the envelope — Are familiar, often colloquial, so can offer a shortcut to convey meaning.

Close-up shot Film technique

Tightly frames a person or a subject. Enables the audience to see the emotions of a character or details on a subject.

Colloquial language Metalanguage

Informal, conversational wording that builds rapport and an ordinary, relatable persona.

Colourful words and descriptive language Persuasive technique

Words that are heightened, vivid, lively, and full of interest. e.g. Hot = blistering, sultry, muggy, suffocating... — Produce a picture and/or induce an emotion. Engage reader by gaining attention and often put a new slant on familiar events and issues.

Connotation Metalanguage

The emotional or cultural associations a word carries beyond its literal meaning.

Contention Persuasive technique

The main line of argument in a text. A statement of the main idea being argued or debated. Focuses on WHAT is being argued. — Pinpoints the issue and the writer’s point of view. Helps the reader identify the issue and evaluate the techniques used to persuade them to agree.

Cumulative listing Metalanguage

Piling up examples or details so the sheer accumulation feels like proof.

Diegetic sound Film technique

Sound within the world of the film — characters can hear it.

Dramatic irony Metalanguage

When the audience knows something a character does not, creating tension or poignancy.

Emotional Appeals Persuasive technique

Play on people’s emotions such as fears, insecurities, hopes, desires, and values. Can target: • A sense of justice or injustice • Family values • Tradition or custom • Patriotism • Moral values — Frequently appeal to our values and attitudes, sometimes subtly. Can manipulate reader to take notice of issues by triggering emotional responses.

Emotive Language Persuasive technique

The deliberate use of strong emotive words to play on reader’s feelings. e.g. sleazy, slimy, vicious, disgusting, outrageous — Evokes strong emotional response to coerce or force agreement. Used to stimulate strong emotional reactions and manipulate responses.

Epigraph Metalanguage

A quotation placed at the start of a text that frames how it should be read.

Establishing shot Film technique

Usually the first shot of a scene to establish the location and environment.

Euphemism Metalanguage

A softened expression that makes something unpleasant more palatable — or hides it.

Expert opinion Metalanguage

Quoting a specialist to lend borrowed credibility to a claim.

Extreme close-up Film technique

A more intense close-up that might focus on a specific feature.

Film noir lighting Film technique

Low-key lighting used to emphasise shadows and create strong contrasts.

Flashback Metalanguage

A scene set earlier than the main narrative, used to reveal context or motive.

Foreshadowing Metalanguage

Hints or clues that prepare the audience for later events.

Framing Film technique

The composition of characters or subjects within a single shot.

Framing device Metalanguage

A narrative structure that encloses the main story, shaping how it is read.

Generalisation Metalanguage

A sweeping claim about a whole group from limited cases; persuasive but vulnerable to rebuttal.

High angle shot Film technique

Subject is filmed above eye level to make them appear vulnerable or weak.

Hyperbole Metalanguage

Deliberate exaggeration for emphasis or effect.

Imagery Metalanguage

Language appealing to the senses to create vivid impressions.

Inclusive language Metalanguage

Pronouns like 'we' and 'our' that position the audience as sharing the writer's view.

Intertextuality Metalanguage

Meaning created through a text's references to or echoes of other texts.

Irony Metalanguage

A gap between what is said and what is meant, or between expectation and outcome.

Jargon Metalanguage

Specialised technical vocabulary that can build authority or exclude an audience.

Juxtaposition Metalanguage

Placing contrasting elements side by side so each sharpens the other.

Loaded language Metalanguage

Words carrying strong connotations that smuggle judgement into apparent description.

Loaded Words (Labelling) Persuasive technique

Words that are loaded with associations. e.g. capitalist, communist, salesman — Shorthand way of discrediting or praising by suggesting associations. Exploits emotional baggage of such words to sway opinion.

Long shot Film technique

Used to show a subject from a distance, establishing them within a setting.

Low angle shot Film technique

Subject is shot from below eye level to make them appear powerful, dominant, or morally superior.

Metaphor Metalanguage

A direct comparison describing one thing as another to transfer its qualities.

Mid shot Film technique

Usually shows an actor from the waist up. Focus is on the characters but also shows the environment they are in.

Mise en scène Film technique

All elements contained in a single frame (lighting, costuming, positioning, set, props).

Montage Metalanguage

A sequence of shots compressed together to convey time passing or accumulating meaning.

Motif Metalanguage

A recurring image, phrase or idea that accumulates meaning across a text.

Motifs Film technique

A dominant or recurring prop, image, or symbol (e.g. mirrors, frames, candles, chandeliers).

Narrative voice Metalanguage

The perspective and personality through which a story is told.

Non-diegetic sound Film technique

Sound added in post-production — characters cannot hear it (e.g. soundtrack, narration).

Over the shoulder shot Film technique

A medium close-up shot from behind another character’s shoulder, typically used in conversations.

Oxymoron Metalanguage

Two contradictory terms fused together to provoke thought.

Panning shot Film technique

When the camera moves horizontally from its fixed position and mirrors the motion of looking from side to side.

Paradox Metalanguage

A statement that seems self-contradictory yet reveals an underlying truth.

Pathos Metalanguage

An appeal to the audience's emotions.

Personification Metalanguage

Giving human qualities to non-human things.

Point of view shot Film technique

Mimics what a character in a scene is seeing, putting the audience into their perspective.

Pun Metalanguage

Wordplay exploiting double meanings, often to disarm or mock.

Rebuttal Metalanguage

Directly addressing and dismantling an opposing argument.

Repetition Persuasive technique

Repeated words, phrases, sentence patterns, ideas. — Gives emphasis and prominence to a point or idea; reinforces and makes reader remember the point.

Rhetorical question Metalanguage

A question posed for effect, leading the audience toward an implied answer.

Rhetorical Questions Persuasive technique

Questions that have the answer embedded in them; often use irony. — Manipulate the reader to agree because the answer is assumed. Disagreement would mean dismissing a point that commands agreement.

Sarcasm Metalanguage

Bitter, mocking irony directed at a target.

Satire Metalanguage

Humour, irony or exaggeration used to criticise people, institutions or ideas.

Setting Film technique

The environment in which the film is set. Can shift over time.

Simile Metalanguage

A comparison using 'like' or 'as'.

Soliloquy Metalanguage

A speech delivered alone on stage, giving direct access to a character's inner thoughts.

Stage directions Metalanguage

A playwright's instructions for action, delivery and staging — key evidence when analysing drama.

Statistics Metalanguage

Numerical evidence that lends an argument an air of objectivity and precision.

Straw man Metalanguage

Misrepresenting an opposing view in a weakened form so it is easier to knock down.

Symbolism Metalanguage

Objects, settings or images that carry meaning beyond their literal presence.

Three-point lighting Film technique

Technique using key light, back light, and fill light to evenly illuminate a subject.

Tone Persuasive technique

Refers to the voice of the writer; the writer’s attitude to the subject and reader. Tone can be emotive. e.g. angry, sympathetic, sarcastic — Reflects writer’s attitude, which can influence the reader to agree or reject. Tone can signal a new direction or perspective in the writing.

Tone shift Metalanguage

A deliberate change in tone mid-text that redirects the audience's response.

Tricolon Metalanguage

A three-part list whose rhythm makes a point feel complete and memorable.

Two shot Film technique

A medium shot showing two actors in a single frame.

Understatement Metalanguage

Deliberately downplaying something so its true scale lands harder.

Vernacular Metalanguage

Everyday or regional language that grounds characters in place, class or community.

Zoom in / Zoom out Film technique

A change in focal length during a shot to magnify or reduce the subject.
